Counting quadratic points on Fano varieties
Francesca Balestrieri, Kevin Destagnol, Julian Lyczak, Jennifer Park, Nick Rome

Abstract
. This paper initiates the systematic study of the number of points of bounded height on symmetric squares of weak Fano varieties. We provide a general framework for establishing the point count on Sym 2 ⁡ X \Sym^{2}X . In the specific case of surfaces, we relate this to the Manin–Peyre conjecture for Hilb 2 ⁡ X \Hilb^{2}X , and prove the conjecture for an infinite family of non-split quadric surfaces. In order to achieve the predicted asymptotic, we show that a type II thin set of a new flavour must be removed. To establish our counting result for the specific family of surfaces, we generalise existing lattice point counting techniques to lattices defined over rings of integers. This reduces the dimension of the problem and yields improved error terms. Another key tool we develop is a collection of results for summing Euler products over quadratic extensions. We use this to show moments of L L -functions at s = 1 s=1 are constant on average in quadratic twist families.



1.
Introduction

The central object of study in arithmetic geometry is the set of rational points on varieties over number fields. However, focusing on the set of
F
-points of a variety for a single number field
F
provides a limited perspective on the arithmetic of the variety. A richer perspective is given by looking at all
{\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}
-points, while accounting for the action of the absolute Galois group
\Gal({\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}/{\mathbb{Q}})
. The set of closed points of a variety
X
defined over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, corresponding to the orbits under
\Gal({\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}/{\mathbb{Q}})
of
X({\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}})
, admits a partition by their degree

\bigsqcup_{r=1}^{\infty}X({\mathbb{Q}},r),\quad\mbox{where}\quad X({\mathbb{Q}},r):=\{P\in X:[{\mathbb{Q}}(P):{\mathbb{Q}}]=r\}.

A point of degree
r
gives rise to a rational point on the symmetric power
\Sym^{r}X=X^{r}/S_{r}
and the arithmetic of
\Sym^{r}X
is intimately related to the study of
X({\mathbb{Q}},r)
, the set of the degree
r
points on
X
.

In the case of curves
X=C
, the sets
\Sym^{r}C({\mathbb{Q}})
have been studied extensively over many years: Faltings
[
Fal83
]
(rational points on the image of
\Sym^{r}C
mapped to
\Jac(C)
), Gross–Rohrlich
[
GR78
]
(
C
being the Fermat curve), Hindry
[
Hin87
]
(
r=2
), Mazur
[
Maz77
]
(
C=X_{0}(N)
), Harris–Silverman
[
HS91
]
(
r=2
), Abramovich–Harris
[
AH91
]
(
r=2,3,4
), Vojta
[
Voj92
]
(heights of points of bounded degree), Masser–Vaaler
[
MV07
]
(
C={\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and general
r
), and many others.
Studying fixed degree points has been particularly fruitful for modular curves, yielding applications such as modularity of elliptic curves over totally real number fields
[
FLHS15
,
DNS20
,
Box22
]
, classification of torsion groups of elliptic curves over number fields
[
Kam92
]
, and the resolution of variants of Fermat’s Last Theorem over number fields
[
FS15
]
.

In contrast, the
{\mathbb{Q}}
-points on
\Sym^{r}X
for higher dimensional
X
have been less studied. While there have been results on the existence of degree
r
points, such as the work of Coray
[
Cor76
]
and Creutz–Viray
[
CV23
,
CV
]
, the only results on the quantitative study of
\Sym^{r}X({\mathbb{Q}})
so far were restricted to the case when
X
is either isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
or when
X
is
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, these results are listed in §
1.2.3
.
For the quantitative study of
\Sym^{r}X
, we want to describe the asymptotic behaviour of the counting function

N_{\mathbb{Q}}({\mathcal{U}},B):=\#\{x\in{\mathcal{U}}:H(x)\leq B\},

where
{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\Sym^{r}X({\mathbb{Q}})
is a suitable subset and
H
is an anticanonical adelic height on
\Sym^{r}X
.

1.1.
Main results

The purpose of this paper is twofold. The first objective is to provide a systematic framework for the quantitative study of the set of rational points on the symmetric square of any smooth weak Fano variety
X
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. Our second objective is to use this framework to count the rational points on the symmetric squares of non-split quadric surfaces.
We observe that the case of surfaces is of particular interest, as when
\dim X=2
the counting problem on
\Sym^{2}X
falls under the Manin–Peyre conjecture.

Conjecture 1.1

(Manin–Peyre)
.

Let
V
be a smooth weak Fano variety over a number field
F
such that
V(F)
is Zariski dense, with a relative height function associated to the anticanonical line bundle. Then, there exists a cothin subset
{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq V(F)
and a constant
c_{V,F}
, called the
Peyre constant
which depends on the height, such that

N_{F}({\mathcal{U}},B)\sim c_{V,F}B(\log B)^{\rank(\Pic V)-1}.

(1.1)

Furthermore, the value of the leading constant takes the form
c_{V,F}=\alpha(V)\beta(V)\tau(V)
, whose definitions we recall in §
2.4
.

Thus we verify this central conjecture for the symmetric squares of our infinite family.

1.1.1.
General framework for counting quadratic points

Let
X/{\mathbb{Q}}
be a weak Fano variety. If
\dim X=1
then
\Sym^{2}X\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and the point count is already well understood. If
\dim X\geq 2
then to count rational points on
\Sym^{2}X
we start by partitioning these points as

\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}})=\{[P_{1},P_{2}]:P_{1},P_{2}\in X({\mathbb{Q}})\}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2}\{[P,\bar{P}]:P\in X(K),[{\mathbb{Q}}(P):{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\}.

The natural strategy for studying quadratic points on
X
, used by Schmidt in the case of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}

[
Sch95
]
, is to count the
K
-points on
X_{K}
in each quadratic field
K
and then sum all these contributions over almost all quadratic extensions
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
. For a single quadratic field
K
the number of points of bounded height in
X(K)
is predicted by the Manin–Peyre conjecture.
In order to execute our game plan to count rational points on
\Sym^{2}X
, we must be able to sum the leading term in Conjecture
1.1
over the quadratic extensions
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
.
We show that
\rank(\Pic X),\alpha(X)
, and
\beta(X)
do not vary under base change for all but finitely many quadratic extensions (see Proposition
4.4
). This implies that the central difficulty in summing the Peyre constants over quadratic fields lies in summing the Tamagawa numbers
\tau(X_{K})
over almost all
K
.

The first main result in this paper is not only an asymptotic for this sum in full generality, but also the direct relation to the Tamagawa number of the crepant resolution
\Hilb^{2}X\to\Sym^{2}X
in the case that
X
is a surface.

Theorem 1.2

(Theorem
6.10
)
.

Let
X/{\mathbb{Q}}
be a smooth weak Fano variety.

(a)

If
\dim X=2
, that is
X
is a generalised del Pezzo surface, then as
Y\to\infty

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|\leq Y\end{subarray}}\tau(X_{K})=\tau(\Hilb^{2}X)\log Y+O_{X}(1).

(b)

If
\dim X>3
, then

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|\leq Y\end{subarray}}\tau(X_{K})

converges absolutely as
Y\to\infty
.

The proof requires a deep understanding of the connections between the Peyre constants of the original variety
X
and of its quadratic base changes
X_{K}
. In the case of a surface
X
, we additionally need to relate these invariants to the Peyre constants of
\Hilb^{2}X
.

An application of the strategy of counting by field requires a very precise uniform control of the point counts in quadratic fields. In practice, providing such uniform point count estimates over all quadratic extensions
K
seems to be very difficult – we refer to §
1.2.3
for more details. One major takeaway from this paper is that if one can provide uniform enough estimates for the
K
-points in almost all quadratic extensions
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
, then our machinery immediately gives the point count for
\Sym^{2}X
for any weak Fano variety
X
of dimension at least
3
. In the case of surfaces, there are is an additional hurdle to overcome, namely determining the correct relation between the
B
in Conjecture
1.1
and the
Y
in Theorem
1.2
which is captured by the
cutoff
(cf. §
1.2.2
).

1.1.2.
Establishing the quadratic Manin–Peyre conjecture for an infinite family

For any del Pezzo surface
X
the crepant resolution of singularities
\Hilb^{2}X
is weak Fano. Thus, the point count on
\Sym^{2}X
falls under the remit of the Manin–Peyre conjecture, explaining the appearance of
\Hilb^{2}X
in Theorem
1.2
(a). Previously, the Manin–Peyre conjecture for
\Sym^{2}X
of a surface
X
had only been established for
X={\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
by Le Rudulier
[
LR14
]
, building on the counting results of Schmidt
[
Sch95
]
.
As the second main objective of this paper, we produce an asymptotic formula for the counting function of
\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}})
for
X
belonging to an infinite family of non-split quadric surfaces, thus proving the quadratic Manin–Peyre conjecture for this infinite family. Interestingly, one has to remove a new flavour of type II thin set to obtain the predicted leading constant (see Lemma
1.5
).

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
d
be a squarefree integer and let
X_{d}=\{x^{2}-dy^{2}=zw\}\subseteq{\mathbb{P}}^{3}
. Then there is an infinite family of anticanonical adelic heights on
X_{d}
for which there exists an explicit thin set
{\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq\Sym^{2}X_{d}({\mathbb{Q}})
such that

(a)

We have the asymptotic formula

N_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Sym^{2}X_{d}({\mathbb{Q}})\setminus{\mathcal{Z}},B)\sim c_{\Hilb^{2}X_{d},{\mathbb{Q}}}B\log B,

which agrees with the order of magnitude predicted by Manin
and
the leading constant predicted by Peyre;

(b)

There exists an explicit
c_{\mathcal{Z}}>0
such that

N_{\mathbb{Q}}({\mathcal{Z}},B)\sim c_{\mathcal{Z}}B\log B.

For the heights under consideration, see Definition
9.4
and Remark
9.32
. We also note that the thin set
{\mathcal{Z}}
contributes with the same order of magnitude to the total counting function and hence needs to be removed to obtain the predicted leading constant.

The surfaces whose quadratic points are studied in Theorem
1.3
are del Pezzo surfaces of degree 8.
The next surfaces for which the quantitative study of quadratic points could be in reach are those for which the Manin–Peyre conjecture is already settled for the rational points.
For example, the conjecture is known to hold for
{\mathbb{Q}}
-points on del Pezzo surfaces of degree
\geq 6

[
BT95
]
, split del Pezzo surfaces of degree 5
[
dlB02
]
, del Pezzo surfaces of degree 4 with a conic fibration
[
dlBB11
]
, certain singular quartic del Pezzo surfaces
[
BD09b
,
Lou12
,
LB12b
]
, and certain singular cubic surfaces
[
Fou98
,
dlBBD07
,
BD09a
,
LB12a
]
. Moreover, Châtelet surfaces have received considerable attention
[
dlBBP12
,
dlBT13
,
dlBB12
,
Des16
]
, culminating in the recent proof of the Manin–Peyre conjecture for all Châtelet surfaces by Woo
[
Woo
]
.
Theorem
1.3
illustrates that it is now possible to move beyond counting
{\mathbb{Q}}
-points and investigate systematically the quantitative arithmetic of higher degree points on surfaces.

Remark 1.4
.

The Manin–Peyre conjecture has been extended to the realm of algebraic stacks by Ellenberg–Satriano–Zureick-Brown
[
ESZB23
]
and Darda–Yasuda
[
DY
]
, unifying Conjecture
1.1
with other completely distinct arithmetic problems, such as the distribution of elliptic curves with certain properties or Malle’s conjecture on the distribution of number fields with a given Galois group. Recently, Yin
[
Yin
]
provided upper and lower bounds of the expected order of magnitude for the stacky point count on the stacky symmetric square of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. The techniques used in Theorem
1.3
can be adapted to the stacky setting, and in forthcoming work of the authors, we will prove an asymptotic formula for the stacky version of the count.

1.2.
Additional notable results

In the course of proving the two main theorems of our paper, we also offer the following new results and insights.

1.2.1.
A new form of thin sets.

In Manin’s original prediction, it was observed that one might need to remove accumulating closed subsets. However, due to the many counterexamples that appeared throughout the years (see e.g.
[
BT96
,
LR14
,
BHB20
,
Gao23
]
), the modern formulation of the Manin–Peyre conjecture requires the potential removal of
thin
subsets instead. An important question is about understanding the type of thin sets that should be removed, and a conjectural answer has been provided by Lehmann–Sengupta–Tanimoto
[
LST22
]
. In a recent AIM workshop entitled “Degree
d
points on algebraic surfaces”, it was asked what the shape of the accumulating subset in Manin’s conjecture for
\Sym^{r}X
ought to be
1
1

1

This question can be found in the notes from the Problem Session at:
http://www.aimpl.org/degreedsurface/1/
.

Through the cases of
\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
and
\Sym^{2}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
, new interest in the study of thin sets and their connection to Manin’s conjecture was stimulated by the work of Le Rudulier
[
LR14
]
. In fact, the set described by Le Rudulier is a special case of a thin set which needs to be removed in general in order for the Manin–Peyre conjecture to hold.

Lemma 1.5

(Lemma
4.1
)
.

Let
X/{\mathbb{Q}}
be a variety. For a fixed quadratic extension
K
, the points
[P_{1},P_{2}]\in\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}})
for conjugate
P_{i}\in V(K)
form a thin set of type II.

In fact, Le Rudulier’s thin set corresponds to the étale extension
{\mathbb{Q}}\times{\mathbb{Q}}
and the thin set
{\mathcal{Z}}
in the statement of Theorem
1.3
is the union of the points in Lemma
1.5
for
K={\mathbb{Q}}\times{\mathbb{Q}}
and
K={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{d})
.

1.2.2.
Quadratic points of small height on surfaces

The Northcott property tells us that there are only finitely many quadratic points of bounded height on
X
. Therefore if the height of a quadratic point is bounded, then the discriminant of its field of definition should be too.
Theorem
1.2
(a) shows that for surfaces the sum of
\tau(X_{K})
over all quadratic fields diverges. For the Manin–Peyre conjecture to hold the sum over fields
K
must be truncated at a power of
B
, since
\rank\Pic(\Hilb^{2}X)=\rank\Pic(X)+1
by Proposition
5.1
. More precisely, there should exist some
\gamma>0
, called a
cutoff
, such that for all
K
and
P\in X(K)\setminus X({\mathbb{Q}})

2\gamma\log H(P)\geq\log|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|+O(1).

The factor of 2 is included for convenience in §
7
.
Understanding precisely which fields can contribute points of bounded height is a subtle point.
Silverman
[
Sil84
]
first studied this problem for points in projective spaces and abelian varieties. We extend the study of cutoffs to weak Fano varieties in §
7
, to determine where to truncate the sum over quadratic fields. We are able to prove general properties of cutoffs, including their existence, that they only depend on the underlying line bundle of the height function, and their behaviour under certain geometric constructions. In addition, we provide techniques for producing lower bounds for cutoffs and determining the smallest possible cutoff.

Remark 1.6
.

In order to prove Theorem
1.3
, it is pivotal that the smallest possible cutoff for
X_{d}
be computed precisely, as it contributes to the final leading constant.
In Lemma
9.3
we explicitly compute the cutoff
\gamma=1/2
. In fact, we show that this is the optimal cutoff and that the alpha constants are related by the expression
\alpha(\Hilb^{2}X)=\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot\gamma\cdot\alpha(X)
. While we expect this relationship to hold for some other surfaces
X
, it is much more complex in general, as can be seen in the example of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
(see Remark
7.27
).

1.2.3.
Uniform point counting

In order to prove Theorem
1.3
, we must produce new asymptotic formulae for the point counts of
X_{d}(K)
for which the main and error terms are both explicit in their dependence on the underlying field
K
, so that we can sum them over varying
K
. This is the first instance of such uniform counts on varieties of dimension at least 2 and of projective degree at least 2.

Proposition 1.7
.

Let
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
be any quadratic field other than
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{d})
, and let us write
M=K(\sqrt{d})
. For any
B\geq 1
we have that
\#\{P\in X_{d}(K):H_{K}(P)\leq B,{\mathbb{Q}}(P)=K\}
is equal to

c_{X_{d},K}B+O_{d,\epsilon}\left(B^{7/8}\frac{(h_{M}R_{M})^{3/4}(1+\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s))}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/2}}\right).

There are many examples of the Manin–Peyre conjecture being proven for varieties defined over number fields of degree larger than
1
via every known method, whether using universal torsors
[
Sch79
,
FP16
,
BD
]
, Poisson summation
[
BT95
,
CLT00
]
or the circle method
[
Ski97
,
BV14
,
FM17
]
.
However, it is standard to fix the field and allow all error terms to depend on it in a completely unspecified manner. Thus, none of these results can be used directly in the study of symmetric powers.

While it is likely that these methods might provide some uniformity if the proofs are scrutinised closely, there is a further complication for which we need to account. Every quadratic field contains the rational numbers; hence, if one counts all the points in a given quadratic field, one counts the rational points repeatedly for each field. To avoid this enormous overcount, it is necessary, as in Proposition
1.7
, to count only the
pure
points – namely, those points whose field of definition is the quadratic field itself. Finding a way to capture only these points is a major obstacle in extending the standard techniques of Manin’s conjecture to the setting of symmetric squares.

These two problems – uniform point counting and counting pure points – have previously only been simultaneously overcome for (linear subvarieties of) projective space
{\mathbb{P}}_{F}^{n}
:

•

Schmidt
[
Sch95
]
,
r=2
, any
n
,
F={\mathbb{Q}}
;

•

Gao
[
Gao95
]
,
r\geq 3
,
n\geq r+1
,
F={\mathbb{Q}}
;

•

Widmer
[
Wid10a
,
Wid10b
]
, any
r
,
n>\frac{5r}{2}+4+\frac{2}{[F:{\mathbb{Q}}]r}
, any number field
F
.

This allowed Schmidt, Gao and Widmer to count degree
r
points on
{\mathbb{P}}_{F}^{n}
in their respective regimes. Moreover, Guignard
[
Gui17
]
was able to count degree
r
points on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{F}
for any number field
F
when
r
is a prime and
n>r+2
. However the error terms achieved in the
n=1
cases of these results are always insufficient for the field by field approach to succeed. Instead, Schmidt and Masser–Vaaler
[
MV07
]
were able to count degree 2 and general degree
r
points, respectively, on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
by parametrising the points via polynomials and counting these.
A similar viewpoint was used by Kass–Thorne
[
KT
]
to produce an asymptotic of the predicted magnitude for
\Hilb^{2}{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
, and by Derenthal
[
Der
]
who established Manin’s conjecture for the chordal cubic fourfold, which is isomorphic to
\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{P}}^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

1.2.4.
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattices

Since Schmidt’s approach combined with the traditional geometry of numbers machinery falls just short of what is needed to establish Proposition
1.7
, we instead develop the theory of counting in
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattices culminating in a general version of Davenport’s lemma, Proposition
8.11
. An ideal in a number field
M
is not only invariant under multiplication by integers, but also under multiplication by the integers in any fixed subfield. We exploit this extra symmetry by viewing an ideal in a number field not as a finite dimensional
{\mathbb{Z}}
-module, but rather as a finite dimensional module over the ring of integers of a fixed subfield
F
of
M
. In this way, we are able to reduce the dimension of the problem, affording us better control on the successive minima and thus the final error term.

Indeed if
F/{\mathbb{Q}}
is a number field of degree
d
,
\Lambda
an
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice of rank
n
and
{\mathcal{S}}
a suitable bounded region of diameter
R
, then the error term in the classical Davenport’s lemma saves a factor of
\frac{R}{\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},nd}}
where
\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},nd}
is the largest
{\mathbb{Z}}
-successive minimum of
\Lambda
. In comparison, when viewing
\Lambda
over
\mathcal{O}_{F}
, the saving is of
\frac{R}{\lambda_{F,n}}
, where
\lambda_{F,n}
is the largest
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-successive minimum, which in practice makes the error term much easier to control, and in turn bound, effectively.

The only previous instances of counting in
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattices are due to Bombieri–Vaaler
[
BV83
]
, Broberg
[
Bro01
]
and Glas–Hochfilzer
[
GH
]
. However, each of these assumes we are given an explicit basis of the
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-vector space which the lattice spans. This becomes increasingly tricky to do explicitly and uniformly as one varies ideals and as one varies the underlying field. To handle this issue we develop from the ground up an entirely basis-free version of the theory of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice point counting in §
8
.

We believe that the advantage gained from the use of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattices and from the framework developed in this paper has potential to be useful for a wide variety of problems in arithmetic statistics. Indeed our success suggests the philosophy that one should perform lattice point counting over the largest field whose size does not need to be controlled.

1.2.5.
New analytic results

In order to establish Theorem
1.2
, we need to sum Euler products over prime ideals in quadratic number fields for varying quadratic fields.
This requires us to produce results on summing multiplicative functions evaluated at discriminants of quadratic fields (Proposition
6.2
) which may have further uses beyond the focus of this paper.
Another key ingredient (Proposition
6.3
) is an on-average truncation of twisted Artin
L
-series which will also likely be of independent interest.
An immediate consequence of this truncation result is the following formula for all the moments of the value at
s=1
of Artin
L
-series in quadratic twist families.

Proposition 1.8

(Special case of Corollary
6.12
)
.

Let
\rho
be a Galois representation which factors through a finite extension
L/{\mathbb{Q}}
, and
t\geq 1
an integer. There exists an explicit constant
C_{\rho,t}>0
such that for any
Y\geq 1
and any
\epsilon>0
we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|\leq Y\\
K\text{ lin.\ disjoint from }L\end{subarray}}L(1,\rho\otimes\chi_{K})^{t}=C_{\rho,t}Y+O_{t,\epsilon}\left(Y^{\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon}\right),

where
\chi_{K}
is the quadratic character associated to the field
K
.

One way to think about this result is that the values
L(1,\rho\otimes\chi_{K})
are constant on average. This is a fact which we take repeated advantage of throughout the paper. For instance, in Lemma
9.27
, we prove a uniform bound for the number of ideals of bounded norm in a biquadratic field, in the spirit of recent work of Lowry-Duda–Taniguchi–Thorne
[
LDTT22
, Thm. 3]
. We replace a
\log^{3}X
term in their error term by the residue of the associated Dedekind zeta function. When this error is summed, we gain important savings by exploiting that this residue is constant on average.

As another example of how these ideas could prove independently useful, in §
6.5
, we establish the following corollary on the moments of class numbers of quadratic fields.

Theorem 1.9

(Consequence of Corollary
6.12
)
.

For any
Y\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{D}^{\pm}(Y)
denote the set of positive or negative fundamental discriminants, respectively, of absolute value less than
Y
.
Let
r\geq 1
be an integer. There is an explicit constant
\widetilde{c}_{r}>0
such that for any
\epsilon>0
we have

\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{-}(Y)}h(d)^{r}=\widetilde{c}_{r}Y^{\frac{r}{2}+1}+O_{r,\epsilon}\left(Y^{\frac{r}{2}+\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon}\right),

and

\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}^{+}(Y)}h(d)^{r}R(d)^{r}=\widetilde{c}_{r}Y^{\frac{r}{2}+1}+O_{r,\epsilon}\left(Y^{\frac{r}{2}+\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon}\right),

where
h(d)
denotes the class number of the quadratic field
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{d})
and
R(d)
denotes the regulator of
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{d})
.

In the case of real fields, this improves upon the
r=2
error term due to Taniguchi
[
Tan08
]
and is the first example of such asymptotics for
r>3
. Asymptotics for all moments were produced by Raulf
[
Rau16
]
with error terms saving a factor of
Y^{\epsilon}
, however her sums are ordered by regulator of the field in the spirit of Sarnak
[
Sar82
,
Sar85
]
instead of by discriminant as above. Moreover, in the imaginary case, Theorem
1.9
improves the best known exponent for the error term in this problem,
\frac{r}{2}+\frac{3}{4}+\epsilon
, established by Wolke
[
Wol72
]
in 1972.

1.3.
Sketch of the setup and roadmap to the proof

Our setup is the following. We work on the
S_{2}
-quasitorsor
\pi\colon X^{2}\to\Sym^{2}X
ramified along the diagonal
\Delta^{(2)}=\{[P,P]\colon P\in X\}\subseteq\Sym^{2}X
, which is an accumulating subset for the rational points. By using standard properties of torsors (see e.g.
[
Sko01
]
), away from the diagonal
\Delta^{(2)}
we can partition the rational points on
\Sym^{2}X
using the twists
\pi^{\sigma}
indexed by
\sigma\in\HH^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},S_{2})
. We prove that such a twist is a natural morphism
\pi^{\sigma}\colon\Res_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}X_{K}\to\Sym^{2}X
, where
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
is the quadratic étale extension determined by
\sigma
. This geometrically justifies counting rational points on
\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}})
by counting pure
K
-points on
X_{K}
for all quadratic extensions
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
. Building on the work of Loughran
[
Lou15
]
, we use the connection to the restriction of scalars to understand some of the invariants of
\Sym^{2}X
and
\Hilb^{2}X
, such as the Tamagawa number, in terms of those of
X_{K}
. Similarly, we can exploit this connection to understand the Brauer–Manin set of
\Sym^{2}X
in terms of those of the
X_{K}
.

Given this setup, we can now detail the roadmap to the proof.
In §
2
, we recall standard conventions and facts around Peyre’s conjecture. Experts may skip ahead, but we note that, in order to facilitate the comparison across different fields, we use a more general notion of adelic metric and reinterpret parts of the Manin–Peyre conjecture in terms of Galois invariant objects over an algebraically closed field. In §
3
, we undertake the comparison between the geometry of the symmetric square and the restriction of scalars, using the twists of the quasitorsors described above. In §
4
, we describe the various invariants in the conjecture in terms of the arithmetic of
X
over a quadratic extension
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
. In §
5
, we elaborate the full Manin–Peyre prediction for
\Hilb^{2}X
for any del Pezzo surface
X
. In §
6
, we develop the tools to sum the Tamagawa numbers of
X_{K}
for all quadratic extensions, culminating in the proof of Thereom
1.2
. In §
7
, we develop the theory of cutoffs, and in §
8
the theory of general
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattices. Then §
9
is devoted to the infinite family
\{X_{d}\colon d\in{\mathbb{Z}}\textrm{ squarefree}\}
of non-split quadric surfaces. By viewing these as a restriction of scalars, we establish the optimal cutoff and prove uniform counting theorems for
K
-points.

Notation

As is customary in analytic number theory,
\epsilon
will be a small positive real parameter that will not be fixed but instead is allowed to shrink as the proof goes on.

Throughout,
F
,
F^{\prime}
,
K
and
L
will be fields. Usually
F
will be the base field,
F^{\prime}
a general field extension,
K
a quadratic extension and
L
a field of definition or splitting field. By a variety over
F
, we will mean a separated scheme of finite type over
F
. We will denote by
V
any
F
-variety and
X
will refer specifically to a weak Fano variety of dimension at least 2 which for us are geometrically integral, and smooth and projective over the base field.

If
F
is a field, we fix once and for all a separable closure
\overline{F}
, and we denote by
\Gamma_{F}:=\Gal(\overline{F}/F)
the absolute Galois group of
F
.

If
F
is a number field we will write
\Omega_{F}
for the set of places of
F
, and we frequently use
v
for a general element. We will write
\Omega_{F}^{\infty}
for the subset of archimedean places. For a finite place
v
of a number field
F
dividing a rational prime
p
we define

|x|_{v}=|N_{F_{v}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}}(X)|^{1/[F_{v}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}]}_{p},

hence we get a valuation
|.|_{p}
on
\bar{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, or even on its completion
\mathbb{C}_{p}
.
For an extension of number fields
L/F
, we write
\Delta_{L/F}
for the fundamental relative discriminant, which is an ideal in
F
. Fractional ideals of
{\mathbb{Q}}
we might identify with their positive generator.
The Haar measures
\mu_{v}
on the
F_{v}
are normalised such that
\mu_{v}(\mathcal{O}_{v})=1
. For the infinite places we use the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{R}}
and twice the Lebesgue measure on
{\mathbb{C}}
.

We denote by
\mathbb{S}
a finite set of places of
{\mathbb{Q}}
which contains the archimedean place, and on occasion we will write
S=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathbb{S}\\
p<\infty\end{subarray}}p
. By
\prod^{S}_{p}
, we mean a product of primes which are not contained in
\mathbb{S}
. Similarly,
\sum^{S}_{n}
refers to a sum over all positive integers which have no prime factors in
\mathbb{S}
.

We use the variable
\xi
to classify quadratic étale extensions
F_{\xi}
of the base field
F
. In the case of both quadratic extensions of
\mathbb{R}
we define
\delta_{\xi,\infty}=\frac{1}{2}
and over
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
we define the weighted local factor of the discriminant
\delta_{\xi,p}=\frac{1}{2}|\Delta_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p,\xi}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}|_{p}
. For a choice of local extensions
\Xi=(\xi_{v})\in\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times,2}
we define
\delta_{\Xi}=\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\delta_{\xi,v}
. The collection of all quadratic fields
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
for which
K_{v}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}
corresponds to
\xi_{v}
for all
v\in\mathbb{S}
is denoted
\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}
. The set of fields in
{\mathcal{F}}_{\Xi}
with absolute discriminant bounded by
Y
is denoted
{\mathcal{F}}_{\Xi}(Y)
. Finally, we write
{\mathcal{F}}_{\Xi}^{\circ}(Y)
for those fields in
{\mathcal{F}}_{\Xi}(Y)
which are linearly disjoint from a known field
L
, usually the splitting field of
\Pic{\overline{X}}
.

As in the introduction,
d
will always be a squarefree integer and
X_{d}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{3}_{\mathbb{Q}}
the surface defined by
x^{2}-dy^{2}=zw
. We refer throughout to the counting functions
N_{K}({\mathcal{U}},B)
and
N_{K}^{*}({\mathcal{U}},B)
which denote, respectively, the number of
K
-points in the cothin set
{\mathcal{U}}
of height at most
B
and the number of those points which are pure
K
-points.
Peyre’s constant for the variety
V
over the number field
F
will be denoted
c_{V,F}
. In general, the dependency on the field is sometimes omitted when
F={\mathbb{Q}}
.

We denote by
\Delta
the diagonal in
X\times X
which descends to
\Delta^{(2)}
on
\Sym^{2}X
, and pulls back to
\Delta^{[2]}
on
\Hilb^{2}X
. In general, the superscript
(2)
will be adorned for an object living on
\Sym^{2}X
and the superscript
[2]
for an object on
\Hilb^{2}X
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Adelic metrics

We follow Zhang
[
Zha95
]
in defining the notion of adelic metrics.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
F
be a global field and
v
a place of
F
. Consider a quasi-projective
F
-variety
V
with a line bundle
(V,\mathcal{L})
together with a collection of norms
\|.\|_{v}
on the
\mathbb{C}_{v}
-vector spaces
\mathcal{L}(P_{v})
for all
P_{v}\in V(\mathbb{C}_{v})
. We say that
\|.\|_{v}
is a
v
-adic metric
if for all open
U\subseteq V
and every section
s\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{L})_{\mathbb{C}_{v}}
the following two conditions hold.

(i)

The map
\|s(\cdot)\|_{v}\colon U(\mathbb{C}_{v})\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is
v
-adically continuous.

(ii)

For any
\sigma\in\Gal(\mathbb{C}_{v}/F_{v})
, and
P_{v}\in U(\mathbb{C}_{v})
we have

\|\sigma(s)(P_{v})\|_{v}=\|s(P_{v}^{\sigma})\|_{v}.

If
{\mathcal{L}}
is very ample, then for a basis
s_{i}\in\Gamma(V,\mathcal{L})
the
usual
v
-adic metric
is given for each
P\in U({\mathbb{C}}_{v})
by

\|s(P)\|_{v}=\left(\max_{i}\left|\frac{s_{i}(P)}{s(P)}\right|_{v}\right)^{-1}.

Definition 2.2
.

Consider a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
together with a collection of
v
-adic metrics
\{\|.\|_{v}\}_{v\in\Omega_{F}}
.

(a)

If
{\mathcal{L}}
is very ample we say that it is
adelically metrised
if there is a basis
s_{i}\in\Gamma(V,\mathcal{L})
such that the function

\left(\max_{i}\left|\frac{s_{i}(P)}{s(P)}\right|_{v}\right)\cdot\|s(P)\|_{v}

(2.1)

is bounded on
V({\mathbb{C}}_{v})
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
and identically
1
for all but finitely many
v
.

(b)

A general line bundle
\mathcal{L}
is
adelically metrised
if there are two very ample adelically metrised line bundles
({\mathcal{L}}_{i},\|.\|_{i,v})
such that
{\mathcal{L}}\cong{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\otimes{\mathcal{L}}_{2}^{-1}
and
\|.\|_{v,1}
is the product of the
v
-adic metrics
\|.\|_{v}
and
\|.\|_{2,v}
.

In addition, if
\|s(P)\|_{v}
in (a) is the usual adelic metric defined by a basis
s_{i}\in\Gamma(V,\mathcal{L})
, then we say that
\|.\|_{v}
is
induced by a model
.

Remark 2.3
.

One can show that if (
2.1
) is bounded on
V({\mathbb{C}}_{v})
for all
v\in\Omega_{F}
, then it is bounded for any generating set
s_{i}\in\Gamma(V,{\mathcal{L}})
. Also, it implies that that for a given
P
and
s\in\Gamma(V,{\mathcal{L}})
the norm
\|s(P)\|_{v}=1
for all but finitely many
v
.

For proofs of these fact the reader is referred to
[
LR14
, §1.1]
.

Remark 2.4
.

It is more typical to define adelic metrics following
[
Pey95
]
on
V(F_{v})
instead of
V({\mathbb{C}}_{v})
; the advantage of our choice in following
[
Zha95
]
is the compatibility of adelic metrics over field extensions. Indeed, we can define an adelic metric on
(V_{F^{\prime}},{\mathcal{L}}_{F^{\prime}})
from the one on
(V,{\mathcal{L}})
for any finite field extension
F^{\prime}/F
. Let
P_{v}\in V({\mathbb{C}}_{v})
and
\|\cdot\|_{v}
the
v
-adic metric on
{\mathcal{L}}(P_{v})
. Given
v^{\prime}|v
a place of
F^{\prime}
and
P_{v^{\prime}}\in V_{F^{\prime}}({\mathbb{C}}_{v^{\prime}})=V({\mathbb{C}}_{v})
, we can simply take
\|\cdot\|_{v^{\prime}}:=\|\cdot\|_{v}
as the norm on
{\mathcal{L}}(P_{v^{\prime}})
. Then it is easily seen that this adelic metric is also induced by a model for all but finitely many places.

Adelic metrics allow us to define heights.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be an adelically metrised line bundle on a quasi-projective variety
V
over a number field
F
.

(i)

For a finite extension
L/F
we define the
relative adelic height
on
V(L)
by

H_{{\mathcal{L}},L}(P)=\prod_{v\in\Omega_{L}}\|s(P)\|_{v}^{-[L_{v}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{p}]}

where
s
is a local section of
{\mathcal{L}}
which does not vanish at
P
.

(ii)

The
absolute adelic height
of
P\in V(\overline{F})
is defined by

H_{{\mathcal{L}}}(P)=H_{{\mathcal{L}},L}(P)^{\frac{1}{[L\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}}

where
L/F
is any finite extension such that
P\in V(L)
.

It is well known that the height does not depend on the choice of
s
and that the absolute height is independent of the particular choice for
L
.

Example 2.6
.

In the case that the adelic metric on the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{n}}(1)
consists of the usual metric at all places
v
, we recover the
naïve absolute
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-height
of a point
P\in{\mathbb{P}}^{n}(L)

H_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}(P):=\prod_{v\in\Omega_{L}}\max\{|x_{0}|_{v},\ldots,|x_{n}|_{v}\}^{\frac{[L_{v}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}]}{[L\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}}.

Let
V
be a quasi-projective variety over a number field
F
. Fix a finite extension
L/F
and
\mathcal{U}\subseteq V(L)
, and let
H
be an absolute anticanonical height function on
V(\bar{F})
. For a real parameter
B>1
, we denote the point count

N_{L}(\mathcal{U},B):=\#\{P\in\mathcal{U}\colon H(P)\leq B\}.

We will also require a count of points whose field of definition is precisely
L
,

N_{L}^{*}(\mathcal{U},B):=\#\{P\in\mathcal{U}\colon H(P)\leq B\text{ and }F(P)=L\}.

We reserve the right to drop the subscript if the number fields in question
F
and
L
are both just
{\mathbb{Q}}
. By Northcott’s theorem, these two sets are finite if
\mathcal{L}
is ample.

2.2.
The Tamagawa measure

Given a
v
-adic metric
\|.\|_{v}
on the anticanonical line bundle of a smooth quasi-projective variety
V/F
, we define the
local Tamagawa measure

\omega_{v}
on
V(F_{v})
as follows.

Let
n=\dim V
. Locally around
P\in V(F_{v})
this measure is constructed in the following way. Consider an anticanonical top degree form
s=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}
constructed from local coordinates
x_{i}
on an open
U\subseteq V
around
P
. We define the local measure
\omega_{v}^{[s]}:=\mbox{d}x_{1}\cdots\mbox{d}x_{n}
on
V(F_{v})
as the pullback of
\mbox{d}\mu(x_{1})\cdots\mbox{d}\mu(x_{n})
along
c(F_{v})\colon U(F_{v})\to\mathbb{A}_{F}^{n}(F_{v})=F_{v}^{n}
induced by the chart
c\colon U\to\mathbb{A}_{F}^{n},P\mapsto(x_{i}(P))_{i}
, where
\mu(x_{i})
is the usual Haar measure. The measure
\omega_{v}^{[s]}
does depend on the chosen form
s
, but not on its representation. The rescaled measure

\|s\|_{v}^{[F_{v}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}]}\omega_{v}^{[s]}=\left\|\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\right\|_{v}^{[F_{v}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}]}\mbox{d}x_{1}\cdots\mbox{d}x_{n}

is independent of the choice of
s
. These local measures glue to give the local Tamagawa measure
\omega_{v}
on
V(F_{v})
.

We combine the local measures
\omega_{v}
to form the
Tamagawa measure

\omega
on
V({\mathbb{A}}_{F})
. The product of
\omega_{v}(V(F_{v}))
does not converge, so we proceed as follows. For a non-archimedean place
v\in\Omega_{F}
, let
{\mathbb{F}}_{v}
be the associated residue field of characteristic
p
and let
q_{v}=\#{\mathbb{F}}_{v}
. Let
I_{v}
be the inertia group at
v
for the extension
L/F
and consider
(\Pic\overline{V})^{I_{v}}
, the inertia invariant part of the absolute Picard group. The Frobenius morphism
x\mapsto x^{p}
on
{\mathbb{F}}_{v}
lifts to a conjugacy class in
D_{v}/I_{v}
and so induces an automorphism on
(\Pic\overline{V})^{I_{v}}
which we denote by
\Frob_{v}
.
We define

L_{F,v}(s,\Pic{\overline{V}}):=\det\left(1-q_{v}^{-s}\Frob_{v}|(\Pic{\overline{V}})^{I_{v}}\right)^{-1}.

Note that this local factor is invariant under conjugation and hence well defined.
Now for a finite set of places
\mathbb{S}
, which contains the archimedean places, we introduce

L_{F,\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{V}})=\prod_{v\not\in\mathbb{S}}L_{F,v}(s,\Pic{\overline{V}}).

Artin
[
Art24
, Satz 3]
proved that this Euler product converges for
\Rea(s)>1
. Define

\lambda_{v}=\begin{cases}L_{F,v}(1,\Pic{\overline{V}})&\text{ if }v\notin\mathbb{S};\\
1&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then the product measure

\omega=|\Delta_{F}|^{-\frac{\dim V}{2}}\lim_{s\to 1}\left[(s-1)^{\rank(\Pic V)}L_{F,\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{V}})\right]\prod_{v}\lambda^{-1}_{v}\omega_{v}

(2.2)

converges on
V({\mathbb{A}}_{F})
and is independent of
\mathbb{S}
.

2.3.
Thin sets

It has been understood since the genesis of Manin’s conjecture that certain accumulating subsets contribute too much to the count and must be removed. The modern formulation, following the counterexamples of Batyrev–Tschinkel, Le Rudulier and Browning–Heath-Brown
[
BT96
,
LR14
,
BHB20
]
, allows for the removal of a thin set.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
V
be a geometrically irreducible, integral, and quasi-projective variety over a field
F
. A subset
{\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq V(F)
is

(i)

a
thin set of type I
if it is contained in
Z(F)
for a closed subscheme
Z\subsetneq V
.

(ii)

a
thin set of type II
if it is a subset of
f(W(F))
for a generically finite dominant morphism
f\colon W\to V
of degree
\deg f>1
with
W
geometrically irreducible, integral, and quasi-projective variety over
F
.

(iii)

a
thin set
if it is contained in a finite union of thin sets of type I and II.

2.4.
The Manin–Peyre conjecture

In this section, we present a version of the Manin–Peyre conjecture, which applies to all smooth weak Fano varieties.

Definition 2.8
.

We say that a projective, geometrically integral variety
V
over a number field
F
is
weak Fano
if it is normal, Gorenstein, and
\omega_{V}^{\vee}
is big and nef.

While our formulation differs from the usual statement of the Manin–Peyre conjecture
1.1
, we will show in Lemma
2.11
that these are equivalent. Our version below is particularly useful under base change, as we will see in §
4.2
.

Conjecture 2.9

(Manin–Peyre)
.

Let
V
be a smooth weak Fano variety over a number field
F
and let
H_{F}
be a relative adelic height on the anticanonical line bundle
\omega^{\vee}_{V}
on
V
. Then there exists a cothin subset
\mathcal{U}\subseteq V(F)
and a constant
c_{V,F}
such that

\#\{P\in\mathcal{U}\colon H_{F}(P)\leq B\}\sim c_{V,F}B(\log B)^{\rho(V)-1}\quad\text{ as }B\to\infty,

where
\rho(V):=\rank((\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}})
.

The order of magnitude was first predicted by Manin and his collaborators
[
BM90
]
,
[
FMT89
]
; Peyre
[
Pey95
]
gave an explicit expression for the leading constant (although the
\beta
factor appeared later, following the work of Batyrev–Tschinkel
[
BT95
]
and of Salberger
[
Sal98
]
).

We remark that the counting function in the above statement of the Manin–Peyre conjecture uses a relative height. However, we are interested in the counting functions
N_{F}(\mathcal{U},B)
and
N_{F}^{*}(\mathcal{U},B)
which are defined using an absolute height. This is necessary for the heights to remain consistent over finite field extensions, but this choice can lead to potential confusion so the reader is warned to beware.

The explicit form of the constant
c_{V,F}:=\alpha(V)\beta(V)\tau(V)
appearing in Conjecture
2.9
is predicted by Peyre
[
Pey95
]
. Similarly to
\rho(V)
mentioned above, we introduce a different formulation for
\alpha(V)
, originally defined as a volume in the dual lattice
C^{1}_{\textup{eff}}(V)^{\vee}\subseteq\left[\Pic V\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{R}}\right]^{\vee}
by the expression

\displaystyle\alpha(V):=\frac{1}{(\rho(V)-1)!}\int_{C^{1}_{\textup{eff}}(V)^{\vee}}e^{-\langle K_{V},y\rangle}dy,

where
dy
is the usual Lebesgue measure.
We then show in Lemma
2.11
(c),(d) that our new formulation of
\alpha
is equal to the original definition by Peyre.

Conjecture 2.10

(Peyre’s constant)
.

Using the same setup as above, let
C_{\textup{eff}}^{1}({\overline{V}})
be the effective cone in
\Pic{\overline{V}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{R}}
and consider the cone in
(\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{R}}
given by

C_{\textup{eff}}^{1,\Gamma_{F}}({\overline{V}}):=C_{\textup{eff}}^{1}({\overline{V}})\cap\left[(\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{R}}\right].

Let
C_{\textup{eff}}^{1,\Gamma_{F}}({\overline{V}})^{\vee}
be its dual cone in
(\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{Z}}}{\mathbb{R}}
, and let
K_{\overline{V}}
be the canonical class. Then

c_{V,F}=\alpha(V)\beta(V)\tau(V),

where

\alpha(V):=\frac{1}{(\rho(V)-1)!}\int_{C^{1,\Gamma_{F}}_{\textup{eff}}({\overline{V}})^{\vee}}e^{-\langle K_{\overline{V}},y\rangle}dy,

\beta(V):=\#\HH^{1}(F,\Pic\overline{V})\quad\text{ and }\quad\tau(V):=\omega\bigl(V({\mathbb{A}}_{F})^{\Br V_{F}}\bigr)

and
\omega
is the Tamagawa measure on
V({\mathbb{A}}_{F})
.

We conclude with the equivalence of the two formulations of the Manin–Peyre conjecture.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
V
be a smooth, proper, geometrically integral variety over a number field
F
with
\Pic{\overline{V}}
a finitely generated abelian group. Then

(a)

\rank(\Pic V)=\rank((\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}})
;

(b)

\Pic V\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}}=(\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}}
;

(c)

C^{1}_{\textup{eff}}(V)=C^{1,\Gamma_{F}}_{\textup{eff}}({\overline{V}})
.

(d)

Under the identification in
(c)
, if
y\in C^{1}_{\textup{eff}}(V)
and
y^{\prime}\in C^{1,\Gamma_{F}}_{\textup{eff}}({\overline{V}})
correspond to each other, then
\langle K_{V},y\rangle=\langle K_{{\overline{V}}},y^{\prime}\rangle
.

Proof.

(a),(b)

Under the assumptions on
V
and
F
, the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence yields the exact sequence

0\to\Pic V\to(\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}}\to\Br F.

It follows that the quotient
(\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}}/\Pic V
injects into
\Br F
, which is a torsion group, and we get that
((\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}}/\Pic V)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}}=0
. We conclude that
\Pic V\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}}\to(\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}}
is an isomorphism of finite-dimensional
{\mathbb{R}}
-vector spaces, which in turn implies that

\rank(\Pic V)=\rank((\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}})
.

(c)

We note that, by definition and by (b),

C^{1,\Gamma_{F}}_{\textup{eff}}({\overline{V}}):=C^{1}_{\textup{eff}}({\overline{V}})\cap((\Pic{\overline{V}})^{\Gamma_{F}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}})=C^{1}_{\textup{eff}}({\overline{V}})\cap(\Pic V\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}}).

We claim that
C^{1}_{\textup{eff}}({\overline{V}})\cap(\Pic V\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}})=C^{1}_{\textup{eff}}(V)
.
First, let
\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}\in C^{1}_{\textup{eff}}({\overline{V}})\cap(\Pic V\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}})
. In particular,
\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}
is effective and there is some
{\mathcal{L}}\in\Pic V\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{R}}
with
\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}={\mathcal{L}}\otimes_{F}\overline{F}
. Since
V
is a proper
F
-variety and field extensions are flat we have, by
[
Har13
, Prop. III.9.3]
, that the space of global sections is compatible with base change to
\overline{F}
in the sense that

\Gamma(V,{\mathcal{L}})\otimes_{F}\overline{F}=\Gamma({\overline{V}},{\mathcal{L}}\otimes_{F}\overline{F}).

This proves that
{\mathcal{L}}
is effective if and only if
\overline{{\mathcal{L}}}
is effective.

(d)

This follows from the identification in (c) and by the fact that for proper schemes the canonical divisor and the intersection pairing behave well under flat base change.∎

3.
Symmetric squares and restriction of scalars

In this section we will recall the basic properties of the symmetric square of a variety
X
, which will itself be a variety if
X
is quasi-projective. However, it will be singular along the diagonal
\Delta^{(2)}\subseteq\Sym^{2}X
of dimension
\dim X
. To be able to work with line bundles, we will assume
\dim X\geq 2
, so that all varieties are smooth in codimension
1
.

The goal is to count points on
\Sym^{2}X
which we will accomplish by passing through the restriction of scalars. In the first subsection, we discuss points, line bundles, height functions and Brauer classes on the symmetric square
\Sym^{2}X
and how they relate to the associated objects on
X
. Next, we discuss these same objects on the restriction of scalars. In the third subsection, we state the geometric relationship between the symmetric square and the restriction of scalars, tying together the work of the previous two subsections.

Only in the case that
X
is a surface is the Hilbert–Chow morphism
\Hilb^{2}X\to\Sym^{2}X
a crepant desingularisation
[
Fog68
, Thm. 2.4]
. In this case, the point count is predicted by the Manin–Peyre conjecture for
\Hilb^{2}X
. In the final subsection, we study the Hilbert scheme of two points on a surface in more detail in preparation for the computation in §
5
of the leading term for the point count on
\Sym^{2}X
predicted by the Manin–Peyre conjecture
1.1
.

3.1.
The symmetric square

In this section, we are always going to take
X
to be a smooth, quasi-projective, geometrically integral variety of dimension at least
2
over a number field
F
.
We state some known and new properties of the symmetric square of
X
, denoted
\Sym^{2}X:=X^{2}/S_{2}
. This is the variety whose points are unordered pairs of points of
X
.

Let us write
[P_{1},P_{2}]
for the class in
X^{2}(\overline{F})/S_{2}
of
(P_{1},P_{2})\in X^{2}(\overline{F})
. The Galois action on
X^{2}(\overline{F})
induces one on
X^{2}(\overline{F})/S_{2}
. The following properties are well known.

(a)

The
F
-points of
\Sym^{2}X
are the
\overline{F}
-points which are fixed by the absolute Galois group
\Gal(\overline{F}/F)
. That is:

\Sym^{2}X(F)=\{Q:=[P_{1},P_{2}]\in X^{2}(\overline{F})/S_{2}\colon Q\text{ is fixed by}\Gal(\overline{F}/F)\}.

More concretely, the Galois group either fixes each component or it exchanges the two points. This leads to the decomposition

\Sym^{2}X(F)=\{[P_{1},P_{2}]:P_{1},P_{2}\in X(F)\}\ \sqcup\bigsqcup_{[K:F]=2}\{[P,\overline{P}]:P\in X(K),F(P)=K\}.

(b)

The diagonal
\Delta\subseteq X^{2}
descends to the diagonal
\Delta^{(2)}\subseteq\Sym^{2}X
parametrising points
[P,P]
for
P\in X
, and it gives the singular locus of
\Sym^{2}X
(to see this, note that the stabiliser of
S_{2}
at these points is non-trivial).

(c)

The quotient
\Sym^{2}X
is Gorenstein and so admits an invertible dualising sheaf
\omega_{\Sym^{2}X}
, by applying
[
Wat74
, Thm. 3]
to the
S_{2}
-invariant subrings of local rings of
X^{2}
.

In what follows, we let
\pi:X^{2}\to\Sym^{2}X
be the natural quotient map; since
\pi
is a finite surjective morphism and
\Sym^{2}X
is normal as the finite quotient of a normal integral quasi-projective variety, see e.g.
[
MFK94
, Ch. 0, §2]
), we have that
\pi
induces a norm map, which we denote by
N_{\pi}
, sending line bundles on
X^{2}
to line bundles on
\Sym^{2}X
, see e.g.
[
The18
,
Tag 0BCX
]
). We will also denote by
\pi_{i}:X^{2}\to X
the natural projection onto the
i
-th component, for
i=1,2
.

Definition 3.1
.

For a line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
on
X
, we induce a line bundle on
\Sym^{2}X
via

\mathcal{L}^{(2)}:=N_{\pi}(\pi_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{L}}).

We note that this line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}
on
\Sym^{2}X
is identical to the one produced by Le Rudulier
[
LR14
, p. 30]
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
X
be a weak Fano variety of dimension at least
2
over a number field
F
.

(a)

The line bundles
\omega^{(2)}_{X}
and
\omega_{\Sym^{2}X}
are naturally isomorphic.

(b)

The line bundle
\omega_{\Sym^{2}X}^{\vee}
is big and nef. In particular,
\Sym^{2}X
is weak Fano.

(c)

An adelic metric
\|.\|_{v}
on
{\mathcal{L}}
induces a natural adelic metric
\|.\|_{v}
on the line bundle
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}
on
\Sym^{2}X
defined by

\|s(Q)\|_{v}=\|\pi^{*}s(P_{1},P_{2}))\|_{v}

for
Q=\pi(P_{1},P_{2})
, where the
v
-adic norm on the right is the induced adelic metric on
\mathcal{L}^{\boxtimes 2}
on
X\times X
obtained by pulling back and taking the product.

Proof.

(a)

Since
\dim X\geq 2
, it follows that the diagonal
\Delta\subseteq X^{2}
has codimension
\geq 2
in
X^{2}
. It follows that
\pi
is étale in codimension 1 and thus that we have a natural isomorphism
\omega_{X^{2}}=\pi^{\ast}\omega_{\Sym^{2}X}
. Moreover, we know that
\omega_{X^{2}}=\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{X}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}\omega_{X}
(
[
Har13
]
Exercise II.8.3(b)), and that
\pi^{\ast}(\omega_{X}^{(2)})=\pi^{\ast}(N_{\pi}(\pi^{\ast}_{1}\omega_{X}))=\pi^{\ast}_{1}\omega_{X}\otimes\sigma^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}_{1}\omega_{X}=\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{X}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}\omega_{X}
, where
\sigma
is the non-trivial element of
S_{2}
. Hence,

\pi^{\ast}(\omega_{\Sym^{2}X})=\pi^{\ast}(\omega_{X}^{(2)})=\pi_{1}^{*}\omega_{X}\otimes\pi_{2}^{*}\omega_{X},

and using effective descent to
\Sym^{2}X
yields the natural isomorphism
\omega_{\Sym^{2}X}=\omega^{(2)}_{X}
.

(b)

To prove nefness, let
C\subseteq\Sym^{2}X
be a curve and consider
\tilde{C}:=\pi^{-1}C\subseteq X^{2}
, then
\pi_{*}\tilde{C}=2C
as
\pi
is finite in codimension
1
of degree
2
. Since
\omega^{\vee}_{X^{2}}=\pi^{\ast}\omega^{\vee}_{\Sym^{2}X}
, by the projection formula we get

2\omega^{\vee}_{\Sym^{2}X}\cdot C=\omega^{\vee}_{\Sym^{2}X}\cdot\pi_{*}\tilde{C}=\pi^{*}\omega^{\vee}_{\Sym^{2}X}\cdot\tilde{C}=\omega^{\vee}_{X^{2}}\cdot\tilde{C},

and we conclude from the nefness of the anticanonical line bundle on
X^{2}
.

For nef line bundles, bigness is equivalent to positivity of the volume, see
[
Laz04
, Thm. 2.2.16]
. From
\vol\omega^{\vee}_{X^{2}}=\vol(\pi^{\ast}\omega^{\vee}_{\Sym^{2}X})=\deg\pi\cdot\vol\omega^{\vee}_{\Sym^{2}X}
by e.g.
[
Hol08
, Lem. 3.3.6]
and the bigness of
\omega^{\vee}_{X^{2}}
, we conclude that
\omega^{\vee}_{\Sym^{2}X}
is big.

(c)

The proof for surfaces in
[
LR14
, Prop. 1.33]
also applies to the general case. ∎

From the naturally induced adelic metric on
\Sym^{2}X
we obtain a natural height function.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
X/F
be as above and suppose that
X
is equipped with an adelically metrised line bundle
\mathcal{L}
. With the respect to the natural adelically metrised line bundle
\mathcal{L}^{(2)}
on
\Sym^{2}X
we obtain the absolute height function
H^{(2)}
on
\Sym^{2}X(\bar{F})
which is given by

H^{(2)}_{{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}}(Q):=H_{{\mathcal{L}}}(P_{1})H_{{\mathcal{L}}}(P_{2})

on a point
Q=[P_{1},P_{2}]
.

We also need to understand how to descend elements of the Brauer group
\Br X
down to
\Br(\Sym^{2}X)
. First we need to understand the following fact about Brauer groups of fields, which we will apply to function fields of geometrically integral varieties.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\kappa^{\prime}/\kappa
be a Galois extension of fields with Galois group
G
. An element
\sigma\in G
induces a group homomorphism
\sigma^{*}\colon\Br\kappa^{\prime}\to\Br\kappa^{\prime}
. The following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br\kappa^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br\kappa^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cores_{\kappa^{\prime}/\kappa}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cores_{\kappa^{\prime}/\kappa}}

Proof.

We identify
\Br\kappa
and the Galois cohomology group
\HH^{2}(\Gal(\overline{\kappa}/\kappa),\overline{\kappa}^{\times})
and similarly
\Br\kappa^{\prime}=\HH^{2}(\Gal(\overline{\kappa}/\kappa^{\prime}),\overline{\kappa}^{\times})
. We note that
\cores_{\kappa^{\prime}/\kappa}\colon\Br\kappa^{\prime}\to\Br\kappa
is the norm map on the abelian groups by dimension shifting from the
0
-th cohomology groups
\cores_{\kappa^{\prime}/\kappa}:\kappa^{\prime\times}\to\kappa^{\times}
. Since the action of
\sigma^{*}
leaves the corestriction map unchanged on the
0
-th cohomology groups, the diagram above commutes.
∎

Definition 3.5
.

Consider
\mathcal{A}\in\Br X
. For a projection
\pi_{i}\colon X^{2}\to X
we define
\mathcal{A}^{(2)}:=\cores_{\pi}(\pi_{i}^{*}\mathcal{A})\in\Br\left(\Sym^{2}X\right)
using the finite morphism
\pi\colon X^{2}\to\Sym^{2}X
.

The following proposition can be generalised to arbitrary symmetric powers of quasi-projective geometrically integral varieties over number fields, but we will only require it for symmetric squares.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
X
be a variety as above defined over a number field
F
.

(a)

The Azumaya class
\mathcal{A}^{(2)}
is independent of the chosen projection
\pi_{i}
.

(b)

Consider a local point
Q_{v}\in\Sym^{2}X(F_{v})
, which has the form

\begin{cases}[P_{v,1},P_{v,2}]&\text{ for $P_{v,i}\in X(F_{v})$, or}\\
[P_{\mathfrak{v}},\bar{P}_{\mathfrak{v}}]&\text{ for $P\in X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})$}\end{cases}

for a quadratic field extension
K_{\mathfrak{v}}/F_{v}
with the unique place
\mathfrak{v}
. In the respective cases we have

\inv_{v}{\mathcal{A}}^{(2)}(Q_{v})=\begin{cases}\inv_{v}{\mathcal{A}}(P_{v,1})+\inv_{v}{\mathcal{A}}(P_{v,2})\\
\inv_{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathcal{A}}(P_{\mathfrak{v}}).\end{cases}

Proof.

(a)

Let
\sigma:X^{2}\to X^{2}
denote the map that swaps the two factors, so that
\pi_{1}\circ\sigma=\pi_{2}
. The induced map on the Brauer groups
\sigma^{*}:\Br(X^{2})\to\Br(X^{2})
then satisfies
\sigma^{*}\pi_{1}^{*}({\mathcal{A}})=(\pi_{1}\circ\sigma)^{*}({\mathcal{A}})=\pi_{2}^{*}({\mathcal{A}})
.

Furthermore,
\sigma
restricts to the trivial automorphism on
\Sym^{2}X
, and thus it corresponds to the non-trivial element of the Galois group of the function fields
{\Gal(\kappa(X^{2})/\kappa(\Sym^{2}X))\cong{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}}
. The latter isomorphism comes from the fact that
\pi:X^{2}\to\Sym^{2}X
is generically
2
-to-
1
. Now the smoothness of
X^{2}
implies
\Br(X^{2})\subseteq\Br\kappa(X^{2})
and noting that
\cores_{\pi}=\cores_{\kappa(X^{2})/\kappa(\Sym^{2}X)}
, it follows that
\cores_{\pi}(\pi_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}})=\cores_{\pi}(\pi_{2}^{*}{\mathcal{A}})
, that is,
{\mathcal{A}}^{(2)}
does not depend on the choice of the projection
\pi_{i}
by Proposition
3.4
.

(b)

Let us consider the case
Q_{v}=[P_{v,1},P_{v,2}]
first. Note that since the action of
S_{2}
on
X^{2}
swaps the factors, it follows that for any
{\mathcal{B}}\in\Br(X^{2})
we have that

\cores_{\pi}{\mathcal{B}}([P_{v,1},P_{v,2}])={\mathcal{B}}(P_{v,1},P_{v,2})+{\mathcal{B}}(P_{v,2},P_{v,1}).

Hence for
{\mathcal{B}}=\pi_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
we obtain

\inv_{v}{\mathcal{A}}^{(2)}(Q_{v})=\inv_{v}{\mathcal{A}}(P_{v,1})+\inv_{v}{\mathcal{A}}(P_{v,2}).

For the second case
[P_{\mathfrak{v}},\bar{P}_{\mathfrak{v}}]
for
P\in X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})
we obtain

{\mathcal{A}}^{(2)}(Q_{v})=\cores_{K_{\mathfrak{v}}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}}{\mathcal{A}}(P_{\mathfrak{v}}).

Since residue maps (as defined by Serre) commute with corestriction, see
[
CTS21
, Prop. 1.4.7]
, they are the negative of those defined by Witt
[
CTS21
, Thm. 1.4.14]
, and invariant maps are defined in terms of residue maps
[
CTS21
, Defn. 13.1.7]
, we obtain

\inv_{v}{\mathcal{A}}^{(2)}(Q_{v})=\inv_{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathcal{A}}(P_{\mathfrak{v}}).\qed

3.2.
Restriction of scalars

Let
V^{\prime}
be a variety over a number field
F^{\prime}/F
. We record some facts on the restriction of scalars functor
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{Sch}_{F}\to\mathbf{Set},S\mapsto V^{\prime}(S\times_{F}F^{\prime})
.

Let
L\subseteq\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}
be the normal closure of
F^{\prime}
. We consider all embeddings
j\colon F^{\prime}\to L
. We will write
V^{\prime(j)}
for the
L
-variety obtained by pulling back
V^{\prime}
along
j
. For an
F
-automorphism
\sigma
of
L
we have a natural isomorphism
V^{\prime(\sigma\circ j)}\to V^{\prime(j)}
over
\sigma^{*}\colon\Spec L\to\Spec L
.

Proposition 3.7
.

(a)

The morphisms
\sigma^{*}
endow
\prod_{j}V^{\prime(j)}
with a descent datum. If
V^{\prime}
is quasi-projective over
F^{\prime}
, then this datum is effective, and hence descends to an
F
-scheme
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}
representing this functor.

(b)

If
V^{\prime}
is smooth and (quasi-) projective over
F^{\prime}
, then
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}
is smooth and (quasi-) projective over
F
, respectively.

(c)

The natural bijection
\nu_{V^{\prime},A}\colon\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}(A)\xrightarrow{\cong}V^{\prime}(A\otimes_{F}F^{\prime})
for an
F
-algebra
A
can be realised as follows: an
A
-point on
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}
pulls back to an
A\otimes_{F}F^{\prime}
-point on
\left(\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}\right)_{F^{\prime}}
, which gives a point in
V^{\prime}(A\otimes_{F}F^{\prime})
under the counit of adjunction

\epsilon_{V^{\prime}}\colon\left(\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}\right)_{F^{\prime}}\to V^{\prime}.

Proof.

(a)

See e.g.
[
JLM+22
, Thm. 6.1.5]
.

(b)

For the quasi-projective part, see e.g.
[
JLM+22
, Prop. 6.2.10]
; the projective part follows from e.g.
[
BLR90
, §7.6, Prop. 5]
since if
V^{\prime}
is separated and of finite type over a field
F^{\prime}
, then its restriction of scalars is also separated and of finite type, and hence a variety, over
F
. If
V^{\prime}
is projective, then it is proper and quasi-projective, and so its restriction of scalars is also proper and quasi-projective over
F
, and thus projective over
F
. For the smoothness part, see e.g.
[
BLR90
, §7.6, Prop. 5(h)]
.

(c)

An
A
-point on
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}
corresponds to an
A\otimes_{F}L
-point on
\prod_{j}V^{\prime(j)}
which respects the effective descent data. Equivalently, we could have given the
A\otimes_{F}L
-point on one of the factors
V^{\prime}_{L}
. This respects the effective descent data for
L/F^{\prime}
and hence descends to coincide with the counit
\left(\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}\right)_{F^{\prime}}\to V^{\prime}
.∎

We now study line bundles and adelic metrics under restriction of scalars. The main goal is to translate an adelic metric on the anticanonical bundle
\omega^{\vee}_{V^{\prime}}
to one on
\omega^{\vee}_{\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}}
.

First, consider line bundles and their sections. In the notation from above, we see that a line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
on
V^{\prime}
over
F^{\prime}
pulls back to line bundles
{\mathcal{L}}^{(j)}
on
V^{\prime(j)}
for each
j
. The line bundle
\boxtimes_{j}{\mathcal{L}}^{(j)}
on
\prod_{j}V^{\prime(j)}
is Galois invariant and descends to a line bundle
\Res{\mathcal{L}}
on
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}
. Similarly, a section
s\in\Gamma(V^{\prime},{\mathcal{L}})
induces sections
s^{(j)}\in\Gamma(V^{\prime(j)},{\mathcal{L}}^{(j)})
, and the section
\boxtimes_{j}s^{(j)}
descends to a section
\Res s
of
\Res{\mathcal{L}}
.

The following proposition from
[
Lou15
]
makes specific the relationship between line bundles and sections of an
F^{\prime}
-variety and its restriction of scalars to a base field
F
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
V^{\prime}
be a smooth quasi-projective
F^{\prime}
-variety. We use the setup and notation from above.

(a)

The map
\Pic V^{\prime}\to\Pic(\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}),{\mathcal{L}}\mapsto\Res{\mathcal{L}}
is an injective homomorphism.

(b)

The map
\Gamma(V^{\prime},{\mathcal{L}})\to\Gamma(\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime},\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}),s\mapsto\Res s
is an isomorphism of
F
-vector spaces.

(c)

There is a canonical morphism
\Res\omega_{V^{\prime}}\to\omega_{\Res V^{\prime}}
, and this is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(a)

This was proven in
[
Lou15
, §2.2]
.

(b)

By flat base change of cohomology, the sections of
\Res{\mathcal{L}}
are the Galois invariant sections of
\boxtimes_{j}{\mathcal{L}}^{(j)}
on
\prod_{j}V^{\prime(j)}
. The sections of the product were computed in
[
Lou15
, proof of Lem. 2.2]
to be
\prod_{j}\Gamma(V^{\prime(j)},{\mathcal{L}}^{(j)})
. Since
\sigma\in\Gal(L/F)
acts on the components by the natural
\sigma_{*}\colon\Gamma(V^{\prime(j)},{\mathcal{L}}^{(j)})\to\Gamma(V^{\prime(\sigma\circ j)},{\mathcal{L}}^{(j)})
, we conclude that the Galois invariant sections are
\Gamma(V^{\prime},{\mathcal{L}})
.

(c)

See
[
Lou15
, Lem. 2.2]
.∎

Now, we construct the natural adelic metric
\{\|.\|_{v}\}_{v\in\Omega_{F}}
on
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}
(via the line bundle
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}{\mathcal{L}}
) from an adelic metric
\{\|.\|_{v^{\prime}}\}_{v^{\prime}\in\Omega_{F^{\prime}}}
on
V^{\prime}/F^{\prime}
using
{\mathcal{L}}
. Fix a place
v\in\Omega_{F}
and an embedding
F\to{\mathbb{C}}_{v}
. The isomorphism in Proposition
3.8
(b) identifies vector spaces over
F^{\prime}
and
F
respectively, so we see that it induces a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\Gamma(\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime},\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})_{{\mathbb{C}}_{v}}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime},\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\otimes_{F}{\mathbb{C}}_{v}

\displaystyle\cong\Gamma(V^{\prime},{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\otimes_{F}{\mathbb{C}}_{v}

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{\phi\colon F^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{C}}_{v}}\Gamma(V^{\prime},{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})\otimes_{\phi}{\mathbb{C}}_{v}=\prod_{\phi}\Gamma(V^{\prime(\phi)},{\mathcal{L}}^{(\phi)}),

where we have written
V^{\prime(\phi)}
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{(\phi)}
for the pullback of
V^{\prime}/F^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{L}}
along the
F
-embedding
\phi\colon F^{\prime}\to\mathbb{C}_{v}
. From the adelic metric on
{\mathcal{L}}
we obtain
v^{\prime}
-adic metrics on each
{\mathcal{L}}^{(\phi)}
.
Also, by the properties of the restriction of scalars we have

\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}({\mathbb{C}}_{v})\cong\prod_{\phi}V^{\prime(\phi)}({\mathbb{C}}_{v}).

It is important to note that in both isomorphisms the
[F^{\prime}\colon F]
factors are indexed by the
F
-embeddings
\phi:F^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{C}}_{v}
. In the part (b) of the following result we express the natural adelic described in (a) in terms of the places
v^{\prime}\mid v
, as they correspond to
F
-embeddings of
F^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{C}}_{v}
up to conjugacy.

Proposition 3.9
.

(a)

Let
t=(s_{\phi})\in\Gamma(\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime},\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime})_{{\mathbb{C}}_{v}}
and
Q=(P_{\phi})\in\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}({\mathbb{C}}_{v})
viewed as a point in
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}({\mathbb{C}}_{v})
. Then

\|t(Q)\|_{v}:=\prod_{v^{\prime}\mid v}\prod_{\phi}\|s_{\phi}(P_{\phi})\|_{v^{\prime}}^{\frac{[F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\colon F_{v}]}{[F^{\prime}\colon F]}}

defines an adelic metric on
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}{\mathcal{L}}
.

(b)

For a point
Q\in\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}(F_{v})
, corresponding to
(P_{v^{\prime}})_{v^{\prime}\mid v}\in\prod_{v^{\prime}\mid v}V^{\prime}(F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})=V^{\prime}(F_{v}\otimes_{F}F^{\prime})
, and
s\in\Gamma(V^{\prime},{\mathcal{L}})
we have

\|\Res s(Q)\|_{v}=\prod_{v^{\prime}\mid v}\|s(P_{v^{\prime}})\|_{v^{\prime}}^{[F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\colon F_{v}]}.

Proof.

(a)

A
\sigma\in\Gal({\mathbb{C}}_{v}/F_{v})
induces isomorphisms
V^{\prime(\sigma\circ\phi)}({\mathbb{C}}_{v})\to V^{\prime(\phi)}({\mathbb{C}}_{v}),P\mapsto P^{\sigma}
and
\Gamma(V^{\prime},{\mathcal{L}})^{(\phi)}\to\Gamma(V^{\prime},{\mathcal{L}})^{(\sigma\circ\phi)},s\mapsto\sigma(s)
. This gives an isomorphism
\sigma^{*}\colon{\mathcal{L}}(P^{\sigma}_{\phi})\to{\mathcal{L}}(P_{\phi})
which maps
s_{\phi}(P^{\sigma}_{\phi})
to
\sigma^{*}s_{\phi}(P_{\phi})
which have the same
v^{\prime}
-adic norm defined above. We conclude that

\|\sigma(t)(Q)\|_{v}=\|t(Q^{\sigma})\|_{v},

since
\sigma
just permutes the factors in the definition.

Similar to showing that the product of adelic metrics is an adelic metric, we can conclude that the collection of
v
-adic metrics
\{\|.\|_{v}\}
on
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}
is an adelic metric. Indeed, they define a product metric on
\prod_{j}V^{\prime(j)}
which is Galois invariant.

(b)

The conjugacy class of embeddings
\phi\colon F^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{C}}_{v}
which factor through
F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
, which has size
[F_{v^{\prime}}\colon F_{v}]
, correspond to the place
v^{\prime}
. Tracing through the isomorphisms we see that
P_{v^{\prime}}\colon F_{v^{\prime}}\to V^{\prime}
factors these
[F_{v^{\prime}}\colon F_{v}]
embeddings
\phi
. The section
\Res s
pulls back to
(\phi^{*}s)\in\prod_{\phi}\Gamma(V^{\prime(\phi)},{\mathcal{L}}^{(\phi)})
as
s\in\Gamma(V^{\prime},{\mathcal{L}})
is defined over the base field. Hence
[F_{v^{\prime}}\colon F_{v}]
of the factors
\|s_{\phi}(P_{\phi})\|_{v^{\prime}}
of the factors indexed by
\phi
equal
\|s(P_{v^{\prime}})\|_{v^{\prime}}
. The result follows.∎

Now, we compare the heights
\mathcal{H}
on
V^{\prime}
and
H
on
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}
induced by this adelic metric.

Proposition 3.10
.

A point
Q\in\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}(\bar{F})
, corresponding to
(P_{\phi})\in\prod_{\phi\colon F^{\prime}\to\bar{F}}V^{\prime}(\bar{F})=V^{\prime}(\bar{F}\otimes_{F}F^{\prime})
, satisfies

H(Q)=\prod_{\phi}\mathcal{H}(P_{\phi}).

In particular, for
Q\in\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}(F)
corresponding to
P\in V^{\prime}(F^{\prime})
we have

H(Q)=\mathcal{H}(P)^{[F^{\prime}\colon F]}.

Proof.

We compute the height on the restriction of scalars using a section
\Res s
and get

\displaystyle H(Q):=

\displaystyle\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}}\|\Res s(Q)\|_{v}^{-\frac{[F_{v}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}]}{[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}}=\prod_{v\in\Omega_{F}}\prod_{\phi}\prod_{v^{\prime}\mid v}\|s(P_{\phi})\|_{v^{\prime}}^{-\frac{[F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}]}{[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{\phi}\prod_{v^{\prime}\in\Omega_{F^{\prime}}}\|s(P_{\phi})\|_{v^{\prime}}^{-\frac{[F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}]}{[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}}=\prod_{\phi}\mathcal{H}(P_{\phi}).

In the case that
Q
is an
F
-point, we get
P=P_{\phi}
for all
\phi
.
∎

Thus, an adelic metric induces on
V^{\prime}
induces a natural one on
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}
. Since
\prod_{v^{\prime}\mid v}V^{\prime}(F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})
and
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}(F_{v})
are canonically in bijection, we can compare the local Tamagawa measures on both sides.

Theorem 3.11
.

Under the natural identification

\nu_{V^{\prime},v}\colon\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}(F_{v})\xrightarrow{\cong}V^{\prime}(F^{\prime}_{v})=\prod_{v^{\prime}\mid v}V^{\prime}(F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})

the image measure of
\omega_{v}
along
\nu
equals
\sqrt{|\Delta_{F^{\prime}/F}|_{v}}^{\dim V^{\prime}}\prod_{v^{\prime}\mid v}\omega_{v^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Let
\Res s\in\Gamma(\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime},\omega^{\vee}_{\Res V^{\prime}})
be a non-vanishing section at
Q\in\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}(F_{v})
, and let
s\in\Gamma(V^{\prime},\omega^{\vee}_{V^{\prime}})
and
(P_{v}^{\prime})\in\prod_{v^{\prime}\mid v}V^{\prime}(F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})
be the corresponding data on
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}
. We use
s
to construct the measures
\omega_{v^{\prime}}
and the section
\Res s
for
\omega_{v}
. From
3.9
(b) it is enough to prove that the image of
\omega_{v}
equals

\sqrt{|\Delta_{F^{\prime}/F}|_{v}}^{\dim V^{\prime}}\prod_{v^{\prime}\mid v}\omega^{[s]}_{v^{\prime}}.

We will do so by proving that under

\nu\colon\Res_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v}}V_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}}^{\prime}(F_{v})\xrightarrow{\cong}V^{\prime}(F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})

we have

\sqrt{|\Delta_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v}}|_{v}}^{\dim V^{\prime}}\omega_{v^{\prime}}^{[s]}=\nu_{*}\omega_{v}^{[\Res s]}.

(3.1)

We prove this statement in several steps.

First we establish (
3.1
) for
V^{\prime}=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{F^{\prime}}=\Spec F^{\prime}[y]
and
s=dy
. Let us write
r=[F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\colon F_{v}]
and fix an integral basis
e_{i}
for
\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}
over
\mathcal{O}_{v}
. In this way we can identify
{\mathbb{A}}_{F_{v}}^{r}=\Res{\mathbb{A}}_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}}^{1}
, such that the composition

F_{v}^{r}={\mathbb{A}}^{r}(F_{v})=\Res_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v}}{\mathbb{A}}^{1}(F_{v})\xrightarrow{\cong}{\mathbb{A}}^{1}(F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})=F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}

(3.2)

is given by
(y_{i})\mapsto\sum y_{i}e_{i}
. On the codomain the local Tamagawa measure equals
d\mu(y)
, as it is induced by
s=dy
.
To determine the measure on the domain, we will compute
\Res s
in the new coordinates on
\Res_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v}}{\mathbb{A}}_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}}^{1}
. Following the construction of the restriction of scalars on schemes and sections we get
{\mathbb{A}}^{r}=\Res{\mathbb{A}}^{1}\leftarrow\prod_{j}{\mathbb{A}}^{1(j)}\to{\mathbb{A}}^{1}
.
The section
s=dy
produces the section
\prod_{j}s^{(j)}=\prod_{j}dy^{(j)}
, which then descends to

\bigwedge_{j}\sum_{i}e_{i}^{(j)}dx_{i}=\det(e_{i}^{(j)})_{i,j}\bigwedge_{i}dx_{i}

since
y^{(j)}=\sum_{i}x_{i}e_{i}^{(j)}
. This produces the measure
|\Delta_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v}}|_{v}^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod d\mu(x_{i})
on
F_{v}^{r}
. To compare the measures along the identification (
3.2
) we noticed that because of our choice of integral basis, we have identified
\mathcal{O}_{v}^{r}
on the left with
\mathcal{O}_{v^{\prime}}
on the right. By our choice of Haar measures this proves the statement in this case.

Secondly, we show that if (
3.1
) holds for two varieties over
F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
, then it holds for their product with the expected factor. This proves the statement for
V^{\prime}=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{F^{\prime}}=\Spec F^{\prime}[y_{i}]
and
s=\bigwedge dy_{i}
, for any
n
.

Lastly we establish (
3.1
) for general
V^{\prime}
and
s
. Locally on an open
U^{\prime}
around
P\in V^{\prime}(F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})
we have an étale chart
c\colon U^{\prime}\to{\mathbb{A}}^{\dim V^{\prime}}
, which induces an étale chart
\Res c\colon\Res_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v}}V^{\prime}\to\Res_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v}}\left({\mathbb{A}}^{\dim V^{\prime}}\right)
around the associated point
Q\in\Res_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v}}V^{\prime}(F_{v})
. We have the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v}}U^{\prime}(F_{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U^{\prime}(F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res_{F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}/F_{v}}\left({\mathbb{A}}^{\dim V^{\prime}}\right)(F_{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}}^{\dim V^{\prime}}(F^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The measures on the top row are constructed by pulling back the measures on the bottom, which differ by the required discriminantal factor by the previous step. This proves (
3.1
) in general, and completes the proof.
∎

Note that the discriminantal factor in (
2.2
) could be absorbed into the Tamagawa measure if one would normalise the Haar measures to be self-dual. However, for our applications it will be convenient to separate the discriminant from the measure.

3.3.
Relation between restrictions of scalars and symmetric powers

The relevance of restriction of scalars is captured in the following proposition. We consider a variety
V/F
, and apply the results of the previous subsection to
V^{\prime}=V_{F^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
V
be a quasi-projective
F
-variety and
F^{\prime}/F
be an étale extension of degree
r
. There is a finite morphism of degree
r!

\eta\colon\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V_{F^{\prime}}\to\Sym^{r}V,

which sends an
A
-point in
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V_{F^{\prime}}(A)=V(A\otimes_{F}F^{\prime})
to the tuple
V(A\otimes_{F}\bar{F})^{r}
under the
r
embeddings
F^{\prime}\to\bar{F}
. The morphism
\eta
is étale on the complement of a closed subscheme of codimension
\dim V
.

Proof.

Let
L/F
be a normal field extension that contains
F^{\prime}
. We will construct the morphism over
L
and descend it back to
F
.

After base changing to
L
we have an
L
-morphism

\prod_{j}V^{\prime(j)}\to\prod V^{\prime}_{L},

but this morphism does not respect the effective descent data; on the left,
\Gamma_{F}
also permutes the factors while it does not on the right. Hence the composition

\prod_{j}V^{\prime(j)}\to\prod V^{\prime}_{L}\to\Sym^{r}V^{\prime}_{L}

does descend to the required morphism
\eta
.

The morphism
\eta
base changed to
L
is étale away from the total diagonal, i.e. those point on
V_{L}^{r}
for which at least
2
coordinates coincide, which has codimension
\dim V
. Since étale morphism are preserved under Galois descent the result follows.
∎

The proof here also shows that
\eta
is the twist of the map
V^{r}\to\Sym^{r}V
with its
S_{r}
-action, classified by the class of
F^{\prime}/F
in
\HH^{1}(F,S_{r})
.

Corollary 3.13
.

Let
X
be a smooth, geometrically integral, quasi-projective variety of dimension
d\geq 2
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, and
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
a quadratic extension. The natural morphism
\eta^{*}\omega_{\Sym^{2}X}\to\omega_{\Res_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}X_{K}}
is an isomorphism.
This isomorphism preserves the adelic metrics on
\Sym^{2}X
and
\Res_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}X_{K}
induced from the adelic metric on
X/{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Proof.

On the open subscheme where
\eta
is étale the natural morphism
\eta^{*}\omega_{\Sym^{2}X}\to\omega_{\Res_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}X_{K}}
is an isomorphism. Since the complement of this locus has codimension
2
and line bundles are determined up to such opens on normal schemes, we deduce that
\eta^{*}
induces an isomorphism.

Start with a section
s\in\Gamma(X,{\mathcal{L}})
and write
s_{K}\in\Gamma(X_{K},{\mathcal{L}}_{K})
for the corresponding section on the base change. We will first show that the natural section
s^{(2)}
on
\Sym^{2}X
pulls back to the natural section
\Res s_{K}
on
\Res_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}X_{K}
along
\eta
. Over
K
the morphism
\eta
is given by the composition

\prod_{j}X^{(j)}\to\prod_{j}X_{K}\to\Sym^{2}X_{K},

and by definition
s_{K}^{(2)}
pulls back to
\boxtimes_{j}s_{K}
on
\prod_{j}X_{K}
, which pulls back to
\boxtimes_{j}s^{(j)}
by definition of the twisted product. Since
\boxtimes_{j}s^{(j)}
descends to
\Res s_{K}
by definition, the result follows.

Now we will compute the pullback adelic metric
\|\Res s_{K}(Q)\|_{v}
at a section
\Res s_{K}
on
\Res_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}X_{K}
at a point
Q\in\Res_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}X_{K}({\mathbb{C}}_{v})
which corresponds to a pair
(P_{1},P_{2})\in X({\mathbb{C}}_{v})^{2}
.
The pullback metric of
\Res s_{K}(Q)=(\eta^{*}s^{(2)})(Q)
equals by definition

\displaystyle\|s^{(2)}(\eta(Q))\|_{v}

\displaystyle=\|s^{(2)}([P_{1},P_{2}])\|_{v}=\|(s\boxtimes s)(P_{1},P_{2})\|_{v}

\displaystyle=\|s(P_{1})\|_{v}\|s(P_{2})\|_{v}=\left(\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\mid v}\|s_{K}(P_{1})\|_{\mathfrak{v}}\|s_{K}(P_{2})\|_{\mathfrak{v}}\right)^{\frac{[K_{\mathfrak{v}}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}]}{[K\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}}\!\!\!\!\!\!=:\|\Res s_{K}(Q_{v})\|.\qed

Remark 3.14
.

The existence of the morphism
\eta
might make it logical to study the arithmetic of
\Sym^{r}X
through the
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}X_{F^{\prime}}(F)
instead of
X(F^{\prime})
, for all fields
F^{\prime}/F
of degree
r
. Indeed,
[
Lou15
]
showed that in reasonable situations all geometric invariants agree. Furthermore, one can show that if
\dim X\geq 2
the induced morphism
\eta^{*}
induces a homomorphism which makes the triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pic\Sym^{r}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br\Sym^{r}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pic X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pic\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}X_{F^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Br\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}X_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}\cdot\eta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{\prime}\cdot\eta^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}}\mapsto\Res{\mathcal{L}}_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}}\mapsto{\mathcal{L}}^{(r)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}\mapsto{\mathcal{A}}^{(r)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}}\mapsto\Res{\mathcal{A}}_{F^{\prime}}}

commute. Here
r^{\prime}=r!/\tilde{r}
, where
\tilde{r}
is the degree of the smallest normal extension over
F^{\prime}
.

In the case
r=2
these vertical morphisms are simply
\eta^{*}
. This allows us to compute
p
-adic integrals on
\Sym^{2}X
on the
\Res_{K/F}X_{F}
, for all quadratic fields. For
r>2
the situation is more complicated.

We also remark that only in the case that
\dim X=2
a general resolution of singularities is known, namely the Hilbert scheme of
r
points
\Hilb^{r}X
. However, by the discussion above this resolution will only be crepant in the case
r=2
, and hence for the symmetric square of a surface we can interpret the point count on
\Sym^{2}X
within Manin’s conjecture on
\Hilb^{2}X
.

3.4.
The Hilbert scheme of two points on a surface

In the case that
X
is a surface, we can describe a crepant desingularisation of
\Sym^{2}X
, namely the Hilbert scheme of two points
\Hilb^{2}X
. It is well known that
\Hilb^{2}X
can be obtained from the blowup
\epsilon_{X}\colon\Hilb^{2}X\to\Sym^{2}X
in the diagonal
\Delta^{(2)}
. Let us write
\Delta^{[2]}
for the exceptional divisor on
\Hilb^{2}X
. We could equivalently have constructed the Hilbert scheme by first blowing up and then taking the quotient. That is, the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bl_{\Delta}X^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hilb^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{2}X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\epsilon}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon_{X}}

where the vertical maps
\tilde{\pi}
and
\pi
are
S_{2}
-quotients, the horizontal maps
\tilde{\epsilon}_{X}
and
\epsilon_{X}
are blowups in respectively
\Delta
and
\Delta^{(2)}
.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
X
be a smooth surface over
F
.

(a)

The Hilbert–Chow morphism
