\epsilon_{X}
is a crepant resolution of singularities, that is
\epsilon_{X}^{*}\omega_{\Sym^{2}X}
is canonically isomorphic to
\omega_{\Hilb^{2}X}
.

(b)

The map
\Pic X\to\Pic(\Hilb^{2}X)
,
{\mathcal{L}}\mapsto{\mathcal{L}}^{[2]}:=\epsilon_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}
is a homomorphism of groups, and induces an isomorphism

\Pic(\Hilb^{2}X)\cong\Pic X\oplus\mathbb{Z}\Delta^{[2]}.

(c)

Assume
X
is weak Fano and
\Hilb^{2}X(F)\neq\emptyset
. The morphism
\Br X\to\Br(\Hilb^{2}X)
,
{\mathcal{A}}\mapsto{\mathcal{A}}^{[2]}:=\epsilon_{X}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}^{(2)}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(a)

See
[
LR14
, Prop. 3.3]
.

(b)

This follows from tracing through the maps given in
[
LR14
, §3.1.1]
with the conclusion being in Proposition 3.1
loc. cit.
. In particular, we note that the map

\rho^{*}\colon\Pic(\Hilb^{2}X)\to\Pic(\widetilde{X}^{2})^{S_{2}},

which is described by pulling back an
S_{2}
-invariant line bundle on
\widetilde{X^{2}}
to
\Hilb^{2}X=\widetilde{X^{2}/S_{2}}

coincides with taking the norm of a line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}\in\Pic X
and pulling back; that is,
{\varepsilon}_{X}^{*}N_{\pi}({\mathcal{L}})=\rho^{*}({\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes m})
.

(c)

Defining the usual filtration for any variety over a field
F

\Br_{0}V\subseteq\Br_{1}V\subseteq\Br V

with
\Br_{0}V:=\im[\Br F\to\Br V]
and
\Br_{1}V:=\ker[\Br V\to\Br{\overline{V}}]
, we begin by proving that

\Br_{1}X/\Br_{0}X\cong\Br_{1}\Hilb^{2}X/\Br_{0}\Hilb^{2}X.

By
[
CTS21
, Rmk. 5.4.3(3)]
, the above isomorphism is equivalent to showing that

\HH^{1}(F,\Pic{\overline{X}})\cong\HH^{1}(F,\Pic(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})).

This isomorphism is clear from part (b) since

\HH^{1}(F,\Pic(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}}))\cong\HH^{1}(F,\Pic{\overline{X}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}})\cong\HH^{1}(F,\Pic{\overline{X}}).

Next we will show that the transcendental Brauer groups of
X
and
\Hilb^{2}X
are trivial, i.e.
\Br_{1}X=\Br X
and
\Br_{1}(\Hilb^{2}X)=\Br(\Hilb^{2}X)
.

The first equality follows since
X
being a weak Fano surface implies that
X
is rational. Since
\Br
is a birational invariant for proper, smooth varieties over a field of characteristic
0
, we have that
\Br{\overline{X}}=\Br{\mathbb{P}}^{2}_{\overline{F}}=0
.

So it remains to show that
\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})=0
. By the exact sequence (
[
CTS21
, Prop. 5.4.8]
) we have

\coker(\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})_{\divv}^{\Gamma}\to\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})^{\Gamma})=\ker(\HH^{2}(F,\Pic(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}}))\to\HH^{2}(F,\NS(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})/\tors)).

As
{\overline{X}}
is rational, we have that
\Pic(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})=\Pic{\overline{X}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}
is torsion-free, so
\Pic(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})=\NS(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})
. Hence the kernel is trivial, and we obtain the truncated exact sequence

\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})_{\divv}^{\Gamma}\to\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})^{\Gamma}\to 0.

Finally, we claim that
\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})
is a
2
-group. Recall the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bl_{\Delta}X^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hilb^{2}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym^{2}X}

By
[
CTS21
, Prop. 11.2.1]

\Br({\overline{X}}^{2})
is generated by
\Br{\overline{X}}=\Br({\mathbb{P}}^{2}_{\overline{F}})=0
, so
\Br({\overline{X}}^{2})=0
, and by the birational invariance of the Brauer groups we have
\Br(\Bl_{\Delta}{\overline{X}}^{2})=0
. Since
\Bl_{\Delta}{{\overline{X}}^{2}}\to\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}}
is a finite Galois morphism of degree
2
of smooth varieties, we have that the map

\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})[n]\to\Br(\Bl_{\Delta}{{\overline{X}}^{2}})^{S_{2}}[n]=0

is an isomorphism for odd
n
, see
[
CTS21
, Thm. 3.8.5]
. Thus,
\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})
is a
2
-group, as claimed.

Hence
\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})_{\divv}=0
and
\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})^{\Gamma}=0
from the above exact sequence. Then since the transcendental part of the Brauer group embeds as

\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})/\Br_{1}(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})\hookrightarrow\Br(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})^{\Gamma}=0,

we can conclude that the transcendental part is trivial, i.e.
\Br_{1}\Hilb^{2}X=\Br(\Hilb^{2}X)
. Hence, the induced morphism
\Br X\to\Br\Hilb^{2}X
is an isomorphism on the constant subgroups, and on the quotients
\Br X/\Br_{0}X
and
\Br\Hilb^{2}X/\Br_{0}\Hilb^{2}X
. By the snake lemma we conclude that
\Br X\to\Br\Hilb^{2}X
is an isomorphism, where we have used that
\Br F\to\Br(\Hilb^{2}X)
is injective due to the presence of a rational point. ∎

Since the elements
\mathcal{A}^{[2]}
are pullbacks of the
\mathcal{A}^{(2)}
on
\Sym^{2}X
we can use the expressions above to compute invariant maps at a subscheme
Z\subseteq X
of dimension
0
and degree
2
.
We can also pullback adelic metrics along
\epsilon_{X}
to produce height functions on
\Hilb^{2}X
, which will satisfy the Northcott property away from
\Delta^{[2]}
. Since
\Hilb^{2}X\setminus\Delta^{[2]}\cong\Sym^{2}X\setminus\Delta^{(2)}
, we will be able do many computations on the Hilbert scheme on the symmetric square instead.

4.
The contribution from a single field to quadratic Manin–Peyre

We are interested in establishing asymptotics for

N_{{\mathcal{L}}^{(2)}}({\mathcal{U}},B):=\{Q\in\mathcal{U}\colon H^{(2)}(Q)\leq B\}

for an appropriate cothin set
{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}})
for
X
a weak Fano variety. We note that weak Fano varieties are rationally connected, see
[
Zha06
]
, and so their geometric Picard groups are free and finitely generated as
{\mathbb{Z}}
-modules and their Brauer groups modulo constants are finite.

In this section we will first examine thin sets on symmetric squares and where we expect accumulating subsets with too many points.
When
X
is a surface, and thus
\Hilb^{2}X
is in the domain of the Manin–Peyre conjecture, one must identify and remove such sets in order to achieve the expected growth of the counting function. Even in higher dimensions it is necessary to identify fields whose contribution to the count can be of a greater order of magnitude than the typical quadratic field and which would thus dominate the count.

Once these thin sets are identified, we will count the number of points in a cothin subset
\mathcal{U}_{K}
of
X(K)
for (almost) every quadratic extension
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
, making completely explicit how the predicted leading term in the asymptotic formula for
N_{K}(\mathcal{U}_{K},B)
depends on
K
. This will then allow us to sum this term over all quadratic fields containing a point of bounded height.

4.1.
Accumulating subsets of
\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}})

Lemma 4.1
.

The following sets are thin sets of
\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}})
:

(a)

\{[P_{1},P_{2}]\colon P_{1},P_{2}\in X({\mathbb{Q}})\}
;

(b)

\{[P,\bar{P}]\colon P\in X(K)\}
for any fixed quadratic field
K
;

(c)

\{[P_{1},P_{2}]\colon P_{1}\in Z({\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}})\text{ or }P_{2}\in Z({\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}})\}

for a proper closed subscheme
Z\subseteq V
, and

(d)

\{[P_{1},P_{2}]\colon P_{1}\in f(W({\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}))\text{ or }P_{2}\in f(W({\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}}))\}

for a generically finite morphism
f\colon W\to X
of degree larger than
1
.

Proof.

(a)

This follows from the existence of the finite morphism
X\times X\to\Sym^{2}X
.

(b)

This follows from Proposition
3.12
.

(c),(d)

Any morphism
X^{\prime}\to X
gives a morphism
(X^{\prime}\times X)\coprod(X\times X^{\prime})\to X\times X
which descends to a morphism to
\Sym^{2}X
. For
X^{\prime}=Z
this gives a proper closed immersion, and for
X^{\prime}=W
we obtain a generically finite morphism of degree at least
1
. ∎

Now consider a weak Fano variety
X
with an anticanonical height function. From Manin’s conjecture we see that the sets

\{[P_{1},P_{2}]\colon P_{1},P_{2}\in X({\mathbb{Q}})\}

and

\{[P,\bar{P}]\colon P\in X(K)\setminus X({\mathbb{Q}})\}

for fields with
\rho(X_{K})>\rho(X)+1
are expected to be accumulating, since
\rho(\Hilb^{2}X)=\rho(X)+1
. The pairs of conjugate
K
-points for the quadratic fields
K
with
\rho(X_{K})=\rho(X)+1
contribute the expected order of growth, but we will see that we will need to remove those too. For other quadratic fields the contribution has a smaller order of magnitude individually than the expected total and we should be able to remove any finite number of them without changing the asymptotics.

In the higher dimensional setting, any single field with for which the rank of the Picard group jumps will dominate the count completely and it seems reasonable to want to remove the contribution from such fields.
For simplicity we define a finite collection of quadratic fields to exclude at this point.

Definition 4.2
.

For a weak Fano variety
X/{\mathbb{Q}}
, let
L
be the splitting field of
\Pic{\overline{X}}
(we remark that
\Pic{\overline{X}}
is finitely generated and free as a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-module, and thus
L/{\mathbb{Q}}
is a finite Galois extension). Let
{\mathcal{K}}:={\mathcal{K}}(X)
be the collection of quadratic fields
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
which are linearly disjoint from
L
.

Lemma 4.3
.

There are finitely many quadratic fields not in
\mathcal{K}(X)
.

Proof.

A quadratic field is linearly disjoint from the splitting field
L
if and only if it is not contained in
L
. Since
\Pic{\overline{X}}
is finitely generated, we have that
L/{\mathbb{Q}}
is a finite extension. Since
L/{\mathbb{Q}}
is moreover separable, it has finitely many subfields, in particular of degree
2
.
∎

4.2.
Peyre constants under quadratic extensions

We will need to understand the expected order of growth in Manin’s conjecture
2.9
for
X_{K}
for all but finitely many quadratic fields
K
. Thanks to our equivalent reformulation of the Manin–Peyre invariants (c.f. Lemma
2.11
), we can easily understand how
\alpha
and
\beta
appearing in Peyre’s constant as well as
\rho
behave under finite extensions disjoint from the splitting field of the geometric Picard group.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
X
be a weak Fano variety over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. Then, for any
K\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, we have
\rho(X_{K})=\rho(X)
,
\alpha(X_{K})=\alpha(X)
, and
\beta(X_{K})=\beta(X)
.

Proof.

Let
K\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
and let
L/{\mathbb{Q}}
be the splitting field of
\Pic X
, then
L
and
K
are linearly disjoint over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. It follows that
\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}=\Gamma_{L}\Gamma_{K}
. Since
\Gamma_{L}
acts trivially on
\Pic{\overline{X}}
, it follows that the natural map
(\Pic{\overline{X}})^{\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}}\to(\Pic{\overline{X}})^{\Gamma_{K}}
is an isomorphism. By Lemma
2.11
, this shows that
\rho(X_{K})=\rho(X)
and
\alpha(X_{K})=\alpha(X)
.
Since we can identify
\Pic({\overline{X}})
with
\Pic X_{L}
and as
\Gamma_{L}
is profinite and
\Pic{\overline{X}}
is torsion-free we have
\HH^{1}(L,\Pic{\overline{X}})=0
. It follows that
\HH^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},\Pic{\overline{X}})
can be identified with
\HH^{1}(\Gal(L/{\mathbb{Q}}),\Pic X_{L})
. Similarly, we can make the identification
\HH^{1}(K,\Pic{\overline{X}})=\HH^{1}(\Gal(LK/K),\Pic X_{LK})
. But
\Gal(LK/K)=\Gal(L/{\mathbb{Q}})
and
\Pic X_{L}=\Pic X_{LK}
, since
K\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
and
L
is the splitting field for
\Pic X
. Hence,

\HH^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},\Pic{\overline{X}})=\HH^{1}(\Gal(L/{\mathbb{Q}}),\Pic X_{L})=\HH^{1}(\Gal(LK/K),\Pic X_{LK})=\HH^{1}(K,\Pic{\overline{X}}),

meaning that
\beta(X)=\beta(X_{K})
.
∎

The Tamagawa number of
X_{K}
will have a much greater dependence on
K
. While almost all local Tamagawa numbers are easily computed by point counting over finite fields, there are some exceptional places.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
X
be an anticanonically adelically metrised weak Fano variety over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. We let
\mathbb{S}:=\mathbb{S}_{X,{\mathbb{Q}}}
be the finite set of places of
{\mathbb{Q}}
consisting of

(i)

the places
\infty
,
2
,

(ii)

all places of bad reduction of
X
,

(iii)

all places for which
\|.\|_{v}
is not induced by a model, and

(iv)

all places
v
for which
X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})=\emptyset
.

If
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
is a finite field extension, we let
\mathbb{S}_{K}
be the places of
K
above the places in
\mathbb{S}
.

Remark 4.6
.

(1)

By spreading out and Lang–Weil, we know that for places
v
with
q_{v}\gg_{X}1
there is a model
{\mathcal{X}}/{\mathcal{O}}_{v}
such that
{\mathcal{X}}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{v}}/{\mathbb{F}}_{v}
is smooth and has an
{\mathbb{F}}_{v}
-point, which lifts to an
{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}
-point using Hensel’s lemma. Hence,
\mathbb{S}
is indeed a finite set.

(2)

In practice, for the purposes of this paper, we can and often do work with any finite set of places containing
\mathbb{S}
.

(3)

Let
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
be a quadratic extension. It is easily deduced that if
\mathfrak{v}\notin\mathbb{S}_{K}
, then
\mathfrak{v}
is a finite odd place of
K
of good reduction for
X_{K}
, the adelic metric at
\mathfrak{v}
is induced by a model, and
X_{K}(K_{\mathfrak{v}})\neq\emptyset
.

To compute the Tamagawa number over a quadratic field, we begin by understanding the Brauer set of
X
over a quadratic field.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
K\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
. Then we have the equality

X_{K}({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br X_{K}}=X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br X}.

When
K\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, we will write
X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br}
for this set.

Proof.

We obtain

\Br X/\Br_{0}X=\HH^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},\Pic{\overline{X}})=\HH^{1}(K,\Pic{\overline{X}})=\Br X_{K}/\Br_{0}X_{K},

where the middle equality follows from
K
being linearly disjoint to the splitting field of
\Pic{\overline{X}}
, and the extremal identifications come from the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence.
Now pick elements
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}\in\Br X
, which generate the quotient
\Br X/\Br_{0}X
, then their restrictions
{\mathcal{A}}_{i,K}
to
\Br X_{K}
generate
\Br X_{K}/\Br_{0}X_{K}
.
We get

X_{K}({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br X_{K}}=\left\{(y^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{v}})\in X_{K}({\mathbb{A}}_{K}):\sum_{\mathfrak{v}}\inv_{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathcal{A}}_{i,K}(y^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{v}})=0\right\}

and

X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br X}=\left\{(y_{\mathfrak{v}})\in X({\mathbb{A}}_{K}):\sum_{\mathfrak{v}}\inv_{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathcal{A}}_{i}(y_{\mathfrak{v}})=0\right\}.

Since
y_{\mathfrak{v}}\colon\Spec K_{\mathfrak{v}}\to X
decomposes as
\Spec K_{\mathfrak{v}}\xrightarrow{y^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{v}}}X_{K}\to X
we see that the sets agree.
∎

Definition 4.8
.

For any
K\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, we define

\biggl(\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{v})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}:=\textrm{pr}_{\mathbb{S}_{K}}\biggl(X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br}\biggr),

where
\textrm{pr}_{\mathbb{S}_{K}}:X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})\to\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})
is the projection map, and we are viewing
X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br}
as a subset of
X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})
.

Note that by the assumption that
X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\neq\emptyset
for
v\notin\mathbb{S}
we see that the projection
\textrm{pr}_{\mathbb{S}_{K}}
is surjective for all
K
.

Lemma 4.9
.

For any
K\in{\mathcal{K}}(X)
, we have

X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br}=\biggl(\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})\biggr)^{\Br}\times\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\not\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{v}).

Proof.

Since
X
is (geometrically) rationally connected (see
[
Zha06
]
), we have
\Br{\overline{X}}=0
,
\HH^{1}({\overline{X}},{\mathcal{O}}_{{\overline{X}}})=0
, and
\Pic{\overline{X}}=\NS{\overline{X}}
is a finitely generated, torsion-free abelian group. Hence, for any quadratic extension
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
, we have that
\Br_{1}X_{K}=\Br X_{K}
and that
\Br X_{K}/\Br_{0}X_{K}=\HH^{1}(K,\Pic{\overline{X}})
is finite. Since
\mathbb{S}_{K}
contains all the places of bad reduction of
X_{K}
, we can fix an integral model
{\mathcal{X}}_{K}
proper and smooth over
\Spec({\mathcal{O}}_{K,\mathbb{S}_{K}})
. The proof of
[
CTS21
, Thm. 13.3.15]
then yields the required result.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

We warn the reader that the notation
\left(\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})\right)^{\Br}
on the right hand side of the equation in the previous lemma is non-standard. This is merely a label which we have chosen to give to a certain subset of
\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})
. In particular, it is not necessarily the subset of points whose invariants at
\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}
sum to
0
.

Understanding the behaviour of
X(K_{v})
with
v\in\mathbb{S}_{K}
is crucial in computing the Tamagawa number of
X
. We begin by partitioning the set of quadratic fields by their splitting behaviours at the places over
\mathbb{S}
.

Definition 4.11
.

For a place
v\in\Omega_{\mathbb{Q}}
we let
{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}}
be the quadratic étale algebra indexed by
\xi_{v}\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times,2}
, that is

{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}}=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}\times{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}&\text{ if }\xi\equiv 1;\\
{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}(\sqrt{\xi}_{v})&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

For an element
\Xi:=(\xi_{v})_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\in\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times,2}
, define

\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}:=\{K/{\mathbb{Q}}\colon[K\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]=2,\forall v\in\mathbb{S},K_{v}\cong{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}}\},

where
K_{v}:=\prod_{{\mathfrak{v}}\mid v}K_{\mathfrak{v}}
, and we let
\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}^{\circ}:=\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}\cap\mathcal{K}(X)
. We also denote by either
\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}
or
\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}^{\circ}
the associated set of fundamental discriminants, while the set of all fundamental discriminants associated to fields in
\mathcal{K}(X)
is denoted by
\mathcal{F}^{\circ}
.

Furthermore, letting
\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}
be the unique place above
v\in\mathbb{S}
in the local field extension
{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}}
of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}
, we have the natural idenification

\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})=\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}}),

which allows us to compare the
K_{\mathfrak{v}}
-points of
X
as we range over
K\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\Xi}^{\circ}
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
\Xi\in\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times 2}
be a fixed element. The subset

\biggl(\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})\biggr)^{\!\Br}\subseteq\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})=\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}})

is independent of the choice of
K\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\Xi}^{\circ}
, and it is henceforth denoted
\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}
.

Proof.

By definition of
\mathbb{S}
, we have
X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\neq\emptyset
for any
v\notin\mathbb{S}
, we fix some
x_{v}\in X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})
for all
v\notin\mathbb{S}
.
Let us start with an adelic point
(y_{\mathfrak{v}})_{\mathfrak{v}}\in X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})
, and consider the adelic point
(y^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{v}})_{\mathfrak{v}}
constructed as

y^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{v}}:=\begin{cases}y_{\mathfrak{v}}&\makebox[36.90045pt][l]{ }\textrm{if ${\mathfrak{v}}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}$,}\\
x_{v}&\textrm{ for ${\mathfrak{v}}\mid v$ if ${\mathfrak{v}}\notin\mathbb{S}_{K}$.}\end{cases}

For any
{\mathcal{A}}\in\Br X
, Lemma
4.9
yields that
{\mathcal{A}}
is constant on
X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})
for
{\mathfrak{v}}\notin\mathbb{S}_{K}
. Hence,
X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br}
contains
(y_{\mathfrak{v}})_{\mathfrak{v}}
if and only if it contains
(y^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{v}})_{\mathfrak{v}}
.
We conclude that any point
(y_{\mathfrak{v}})_{{\mathfrak{v}}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}\in\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})
is the projection of an adelic point, and it lies in
\biggl(\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})\biggr)^{\!\Br}
if and only if

\sum_{{\mathfrak{v}}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}\inv_{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathcal{A}}(y_{\mathfrak{v}})=-\sum_{{\mathfrak{v}}\not\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}\inv_{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathcal{A}}(x_{v})=-[K\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]\sum_{v\not\in\mathbb{S}}\inv_{v}{\mathcal{A}}(x_{v}).

Since
\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})
only depends on
\Xi
and the latter condition is independent of
K
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Definition 4.13
.

Let
v
be a place of
{\mathbb{Q}}
, and choose
\xi_{v}\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times 2}
. Define
\omega_{v}
to be the measure on
X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}})
given by

\begin{cases}\omega_{v}\times\omega_{v}\text{ on }X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}})=X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\times X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})&\text{ if }\xi_{v}=1,\\
\makebox[31.95142pt][l]{$\omega_{\mathfrak{v}}$}\text{ on }X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}})&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

where
\mathfrak{v}
is the unique place of the field
{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}}
for
\xi_{v}\not\neq 1
.
We define

\omega_{\Xi}(X):=\left(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\omega_{v}\right)\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}.

Now we study the local Tamagawa numbers at primes
p
away from places in
\mathbb{S}_{K}
. These depend on the splitting of
p
in
K
, which we encode in the quadratic character associated to
K
.

Definition 4.14
.

Denote by
\chi_{K}
both the Galois representation associated to the quadratic field
K
and the associated quadratic character. For
p\not\in\mathbb{S}
and
\mathcal{X}/{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
a smooth model, define

(a)

the local Tamagawa numbers

w_{p}=\frac{\#{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})}{p^{\dim X}}\quad\text{ and }\quad w_{p^{2}}=\frac{\#{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p^{2}})}{p^{2\dim X}},

(b)

the modified convergence factor

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}_{p}=

\displaystyle L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}(1,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K}).

Note that this should be compared with (and will occasionally appear alongside) the traditional convergence factors

\lambda_{p}=L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}(1,\Pic\overline{X})\quad\text{ and }\quad\lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}=L_{K,\mathfrak{p}}(1,\Pic\overline{X}).

Note that if we write
\lambda_{p}
as a rational function in the variable
\tfrac{1}{p}
, then
\bar{\lambda}_{p}
is the same function in
\chi_{K}(p)/p
. Then
\bar{\lambda}_{p}=1
for ramified primes, and
\bar{\lambda}_{p}=\lambda_{p}
for split primes.

The following generalises the fact that
\zeta_{K}(s)=\zeta_{\mathbb{Q}}(s)L_{{\mathbb{Q}}}(s,\chi_{K})
for quadratic fields
K
.

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
be a quadratic extension and let
p
be a prime number. Then

\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid p}L_{K,\mathfrak{p}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})=L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K}).

Proof.

We note that since
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
is quadratic, if
K\subseteq L
then
LK=L
and so
LK/K
is Galois since
L/{\mathbb{Q}}
is Galois by definition. If
K
is not contained in
L
, then
L
and
K
are linearly disjoint over
{\mathbb{Q}}
and so
LK/K
is again Galois.
Let
(\rho,V)
denote the
\Gal(LK/K)
representation associated to
\Pic X_{LK}=\Pic\overline{X}
. There is the induced
\Gal(LK/{\mathbb{Q}})
representation
(\text{Ind}^{\Gal(LK/K)}_{\Gal(LK/{\mathbb{Q}})}\rho,V^{\prime})
where
V^{\prime}
can be written explicitly as
V\oplus\sigma V
for
\sigma\in\Gal(LK/{\mathbb{Q}})
representing the non-trivial element of
\Gal(K/{\mathbb{Q}})
. By the inductivity property of Artin
L
-functions

L_{K}(s,\rho)=L_{\mathbb{Q}}\left(s,\text{Ind}^{\Gal(LK/K)}_{\Gal(LK/{\mathbb{Q}})}\rho\right),

and so in particular the local factor of the Euler product at any prime
p
is the same for both
L
-functions.
By definition, the local factor of
L_{K}(s,\rho)
is
\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid p}L_{K,\mathfrak{p}}(s,\rho)
. Moreover,
\text{Ind}^{\Gal(LK/K)}_{\Gal(LK/{\mathbb{Q}})}\rho
can be written as
\rho\oplus(\rho\otimes\chi_{K})
, with the associated module being
V^{\prime}=V\oplus(V\otimes\chi_{K})
.
Therefore,

L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}\left(s,\text{Ind}^{\Gal(LK/K)}_{\Gal(LK/{\mathbb{Q}})}\rho\right)=L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}(s,\rho\oplus(\rho\otimes\chi_{K}))=L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}(s,\rho)L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}(s,\rho\otimes\chi_{K}),

which gives the desired equality.
∎

Proposition 4.16
.

Let
K\in\mathcal{K}(X)
be a quadratic extension and
p\not\in\mathbb{S}
.
We have

\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid p}\lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}=\lambda_{p}\bar{\lambda}_{p}=\begin{cases}\lambda_{p}&\textup{ if }p\text{ ramifies in }K;\\
\lambda^{2}_{p}&\textup{ if }p\text{ splits completely in }K;\\
\lambda_{p}\bar{\lambda}_{p}&\textup{ if }p\text{ is inert in }K,\end{cases}

and

\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid p}\omega_{\mathfrak{p}}(X(K_{\mathfrak{p}}))=\begin{cases}w_{p}&\textup{ if }p\text{ ramifies in }K;\\
w^{2}_{p}&\textup{ if }p\text{ splits completely in }K;\\
w_{p^{2}}&\textup{ if }p\text{ is inert in }K.\end{cases}

Proof.

The statement concerning the convergence factors
\lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}
follows almost immediately from the previous proposition. Indeed, for inert primes there is nothing to prove. For split primes
p
, note that
\chi_{K}(p)=1
and therefore
L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}(s,\rho\otimes\chi_{K})=L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}(s,\rho)
, and for ramified primes
p
, we have
\chi_{K}(p)=0
and thus
L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}(s,\rho\otimes\chi_{K})=1
.

Now suppose
p
is a rational prime which ramifies in
K
and that
p\mathcal{O}_{K}=\mathfrak{p}^{2}
. Since in this case
K_{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
, we have

\omega_{\mathfrak{p}}(X(K_{\mathfrak{p}}))=\omega_{p}(X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})).

Hence by
[
Pey95
, Lem. 2.2.1]
,
\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid p}\omega_{\mathfrak{p}}(X(K_{\mathfrak{p}}))=\omega_{p}(X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}))=w_{p}
. Similarly, if
p

splits in
K
then for every
\mathfrak{p}
above
p

\omega_{\mathfrak{p}}(X(K_{\mathfrak{p}}))=\omega_{p}(X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}))=w_{p}.

Hence
\prod_{\mathfrak{p}\mid p}\omega_{\mathfrak{p}}(X(K_{\mathfrak{p}}))=w_{p}^{2}
. Finally, if
p
is inert then the residue field associated to
K_{\mathfrak{p}}
has order
p^{2}
. Since
p\not\in\mathbb{S}
and thus is of good reduction,
[
Pey95
, Lem. 2.2.1]
implies that
\omega_{\mathfrak{p}}(X(K_{\mathfrak{p}}))=w_{p^{2}}
.
∎

Theorem 4.17
.

For
K\in\mathcal{K}(X)
the Tamagawa number of
X_{K}
is given by

\tau(X_{K})=|\Delta_{K}|^{-\frac{\dim X_{K}}{2}}\left(\lim_{s\to 1}(s-1)^{\rho(X)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})\right)L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(1,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K})\hskip 85.35826pt

\hskip 85.35826pt\times\ \omega_{\Xi}(X)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}\begin{cases}\lambda^{-1}_{p}w_{p}&\textup{ if }p\text{ ramifies in }K;\\
\lambda^{-2}_{p}w^{2}_{p}&\textup{ if }p\text{ splits completely in }K;\\
\lambda^{-1}_{p}\bar{\lambda}^{-1}_{p}w_{p^{2}}&\textup{ if }p\text{ is inert in }K.\end{cases}

Proof.

By the definition of the Tamagawa number

\displaystyle\tau(X_{K})

\displaystyle=\omega(X_{K}({\mathbb{A}}_{K}))^{\Br}

\displaystyle=|\Delta|^{-\frac{\dim X_{K}}{2}}\lim_{s\rightarrow 1^{+}}[(s-1)^{\rho(X_{K})}L_{K,\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic\overline{X_{K}})]\left[\prod_{\mathfrak{v}}\lambda^{-1}_{\mathfrak{v}}\omega_{\mathfrak{v}}\right]\left(X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br}\right).

By Proposition
4.15
, we see that

L_{K,\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic\overline{X_{K}})=L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K}).

Note that for
K\in\mathcal{K}(X)
, the
L
-function
L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K})
converges absolutely at
s=1
. By Lemma
4.9
, the influence of the Brauer–Manin obstruction factors through the places in
\mathbb{S}
and so we have

\left[\prod_{\mathfrak{v}}\lambda^{-1}_{\mathfrak{v}}\omega_{\mathfrak{v}}\right]\left(X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br}\right)=\left[\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\not\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}\lambda_{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}\omega_{\mathfrak{v}}(X(K_{\mathfrak{v}}))\right]\left(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\omega_{v}\right)\left(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}})\right)^{\Br}.

The factor on the far right is precisely
\omega_{\Xi}(X)
by definition. For the remaining product, we apply Proposition
4.16
.
∎

5.
The Peyre constant for the Hilbert scheme of
2
points on a surface
X

If
X
is a surface, then the crepant desingularisation
\Hilb^{2}X
of
\Sym^{2}X
is a weak Fano variety. Hence, Manin’s conjecture applies to the point count on the symmetric square and the result depends on the invariants of the Hilbert scheme. In this section, we study the Peyre constant of the
\Hilb^{2}X
in relation to the Peyre constant of the surface
X
.

5.1.
Cohomological invariants

From the description of the Picard group of
\Hilb^{2}X
in Proposition
3.15
we have the following result.

Proposition 5.1
.

We have

\rho(\Hilb^{2}X)=\rho(X)+1\quad\text{ and }\quad\beta(\Hilb^{2}X)=\beta(X).

Proof.

The isomorphism
\Pic(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})\cong\Pic{\overline{X}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\Delta^{[2]}
of Galois modules proves the first statement. The second statement follows from
\HH^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},{\mathbb{Z}})=0
.
∎

We note in particular that the equality
\beta(\Hilb^{2}X)=\beta(X)
is induced by the isomorphism

\HH^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},\Pic{\overline{X}})\xrightarrow{\cong}\HH^{1}({\mathbb{Q}},\Pic(\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}}))\cong\Br(\Hilb^{2}X)/\Br_{0}(\Hilb^{2}X)

which is compatible with the homomorphism

\Br X\to\Br(\Hilb^{2}X),\quad\mathcal{A}\mapsto\mathcal{A}^{[2]},

from Proposition
3.15
, and invariant maps of
\mathcal{A}^{[2]}
can be computed as in Proposition
3.6
, since the homomorphism factors through
\Br(\Sym^{2}X)
.

The effective cone of
\Hilb^{2}X
, and hence
\alpha(\Hilb^{2}X)
, are more mysterious even for the case of
X
being a surface, and were only successfully studied in a few cases
[
LR14
,
BC13
]
. For us there will be relation to a combination of arithmetical and geometrical properties, which are studied in depth in §
7
.

5.2.
The Tamagawa number

In this section, we relate the Tamagawa number of
\Hilb^{2}X
to the Tamagawa numbers of the
X_{K}
for all quadratic extensions
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
. For the good places
p\not\in\mathbb{S}
the local Tamagawa number and convergence factor for
\Hilb^{2}X
and are directly described in terms of the invariants of
X
from Definition
4.5
, as these places play no role in the Brauer–Manin obstruction. We will show, see Proposition
5.11
and Proposition
5.14
, that the following definition for
\widetilde{w}_{p}
and
\widetilde{\lambda}_{p}
correspond to
\omega_{p}(\Hilb^{2}X)
and the convergence factors for
\Hilb^{2}X
.

Definition 5.2
.

For
p\not\in\mathbb{S}
we define

\widetilde{w}_{p}:=\frac{w_{p}}{p}+\frac{w_{p}^{2}+w_{p^{2}}}{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{\lambda}_{p}:=\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)^{-1}\lambda_{p}

When considering the effect of the Brauer–Manin obstruction at the places
v\in\mathbb{S}
we will need to sort the quadratic extensions by splitting type using the
\Xi=(\xi_{v})_{v}\in\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times,2}
as in §
4.2
. We will see that the local factor of
\Hilb^{2}X
at
v\in\mathbb{S}
is intimately connected to the distribution of quadratic extensions
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
with splitting type
\Xi
. Therefore, we introduce succinct notation for a local factor involving the local part of the discriminant of such extensions.

Definition 5.3
.

For a finite place
p\in\mathbb{S}\setminus\{\infty\}
and
\xi_{p}\in{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{\times,2}
we define

\delta_{\xi_{p},p}=\frac{1}{2}|\Delta_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p,\xi_{p}}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}|_{p}.

In the case of
v=\infty
we define the local factor as
\delta_{\xi_{\infty},\infty}=\frac{1}{2}
, for both quadratic étale extensions of
{\mathbb{R}}
.

For
\Xi=(\xi_{v})_{v}\in\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times,2}
we collect these terms at all bad places in

\delta_{\Xi}=\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\delta_{\xi_{v},v}\quad\text{ and }\quad\widetilde{\delta}_{\Xi}=\delta_{\Xi}\prod_{p\in\mathbb{S}\setminus\{\infty\}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right).

Finally, we combine the above ingredients to complete the description of the Tamagawa measure on
\Hilb^{2}X
, which should be compared to our expression of
\tau(X_{K})
in Theorem
4.17
.
In that Theorem, the Tamagawa number at bad places captured information about the specific local behaviour of the extension
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
. On the Hilbert scheme, we are interested in all quadratic points and this is reflected in the Tamagawa number taking the form of a sum over all possible local factors in
\tau(X_{K})
weighted by the local discriminant of the corresponding extension.
The existence of such a description is in no way obvious and is also completely pivotal for the strategy to counting points on
\Hilb^{2}X
outlined in §
1.1.1
.

Theorem 5.4
.

If
X
is a smooth weak Fano surface over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, we have

\tau(\Hilb^{2}X)=\lim_{s\to 1}\left[(s-1)^{\rho(X)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})\right]\left[\sum_{\Xi}\widetilde{\delta}_{\Xi}w_{\Xi}(X)\right]\left[\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{p}\widetilde{w}_{p}\right].

This result implies the following.

Corollary 5.5
.

The following are equivalent.

(i)

\tau(\Hilb^{2}X)>0
.

(ii)

\tau(X_{K})>0
for a quadratic extension
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
.

(iii)

\tau(X_{K})>0
for infinitely many quadratic extensions
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Remark 5.6
.

Colliot-Thélène
[
CT03
]
conjectured that, for any rationally connected variety
V
over a number field
K
, the set of rational points
V(K)
has dense image in
V({\mathbb{A}}_{K})^{\Br}
. This conjecture applies to both the rational surface
X
and the unirational variety
\Hilb^{2}X
. So, under this conjecture, the equivalent conditions in Corollary
5.5
could be replaced by “
X(K)
is non-empty for a quadratic field
K
”, or “
\Hilb^{2}X
has a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-point”.

For the proof we first write the Brauer–Manin set of
\Hilb^{2}X
as a disjoint union over all
\Xi
, and then compute the Tamagawa measure of each of the finitely many parts.

5.2.1.
The Brauer–Manin set

To compute the Tamagawa number, we need to first understand the Brauer–Manin set
\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{A}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}})^{\Br}\subseteq\prod_{p}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
. When
p\notin\mathbb{S}
, there is no constraint imposed on the factor
\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
by the Brauer group.

Proposition 5.7
.

(a)

For
p\not\in\mathbb{S}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in\Br(\Hilb^{2}X)
, the map

\inv_{p}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}\colon\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\to{\mathbb{Q}}/{\mathbb{Z}}

is constant.

(b)

The set
\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbb{Q}})^{\!\textup{Br}}
is a product with
\prod_{p\not\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
as one of the factors.

Proof.

(a)

Proposition
3.15
gives an isomorphism
\Br X\rightarrow\Br(\Hilb^{2}X)
so let
\mathcal{A}\in\Br X
be such that
\mathcal{A}^{[2]}={\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
. Recall that we have constructed
{\mathcal{A}}^{(2)}=\cores_{\pi}(\pi_{i}^{*}{\mathcal{A}})\in\Br(\Sym^{2}X)
in Definition
3.5
, which pulls back to
{\mathcal{A}}^{[2]}
along the Hilbert–Chow morphism. Then by Proposition
3.6
, the evaluation maps of
\mathcal{A}^{[2]}
on
\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})
is expressed in terms of the evaluation maps of
\mathcal{A}
on
X(K_{v})
for
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
some quadratic extension and
v
a place above
p
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}_{p}
-model for
X_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
. By
[
CTS13
, Prop. 2.3(i)]
, the image of
\Br X\rightarrow\Br X_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
is generated by
\Br{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and
\Br\mathcal{X}
. Thus, the image of
\Br X_{K}\rightarrow\Br X_{K_{v}}
is also generated by
\Br{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
and
\Br\mathcal{X}
.
The evaluation maps are constant on the constant classes in
\Br{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
; The classes in
\Br X_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}}
coming from
\Br\mathcal{X}
can be pulled back there and since
\Br\mathcal{O}_{K_{v}}
is trivial, these classes have trivial evaluation maps. Thus
\inv_{v}\mathcal{A}(P)
is constant on
X(K_{v})
for all
K_{v}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
with value independent of the specific field
K
.

(b)

This follows directly from (a).∎

Remark 5.8
.

The work of
[
CTS13
, Cor. 3.2]
directly shows that
\inv_{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathcal{A}}
is constant on
X(K_{\mathfrak{p}})
for every
K
and
\mathfrak{p}\mid p
for
p\notin\mathbb{S}
. Then using the assumption that
X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})\neq\emptyset
we can conclude that this value is independent of
K
, and hence only depends on
p
. However, the above proof does not rely on this last assumption and is true for a smaller set of exceptional primes
\mathbb{S}
.

As a consequence of the above theorem, we have the following factorisation of
\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbb{Q}})^{\text{Br}}
.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}
be the subset of
\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})
such that

\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbb{Q}})^{\text{Br}}=\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}\times\prod_{p\not\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}).

5.2.2.
The local Tamagawa numbers of the Hilbert scheme

To compute the Tamagawa measure of local and adelic sets on the Hilbert scheme we will use the natural morphisms
\Res_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}X_{K}\to\Sym^{2}X\leftarrow\Hilb^{2}X
, both preserving the anticanonical line bundle and adelic metrics. For the first morphism, constructed in Proposition
3.12
, this follows from Corollary
3.13
, and for Hilbert–Chow morphism this follows from Proposition
3.15
(a). These morphism allow us to translate local integrals on
\Hilb^{2}X
to
\Res_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}X_{K}
and in turn back to
X_{K}
using Theorem
3.11
.

By the factorisation above the Tamagawa number of
\Hilb^{2}X
will be a product of the local Tamagawa number at each
p\not\in S
, and a single factor which will incorporate the effect of the Brauer–Manin obstrution at the bad places
v\in S
. For both cases we will use the following partition of
\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})
for a single place
v
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Consider the morphism
\vartheta_{v}
constructed as the composition

\vartheta_{v}\colon\coprod_{\xi\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}_{v}/{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times,2}_{v}}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi})\xrightarrow{\nu^{-1}_{X,v}}\coprod_{\xi\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}_{v}/{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times,2}_{v}}\left[\Res_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}}X_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi}}\right]({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\xrightarrow{\eta_{X,v}}\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v}).

(a)

The morphism
\vartheta_{v}
is a surjective degree
2
local diffeomorphism away from measure zero loci.

(b)

Recall the measure
\omega_{v}
from Definition
4.13
on
X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi})
. The pushforward of the measure

\sum_{\xi\in{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times}_{v}/{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times,2}_{v}}\delta_{\xi,v}\omega_{v}

along
\vartheta_{v}
equals the Tamagawa measure
\omega_{v}^{(2)}
on
\Sym^{2}X
.

Proof.

(a)

The map
\nu_{X,v}
is a bijection and the maps
\eta_{X,v}
are degree 2 étale maps away from the diagonal according to Proposition
3.12
so one gets a degree 2 map. The surjectivity does not hold for an individual map
\eta_{X,v}
but follows from taking the disjoint union over all possible quadratic extensions of
\mathbb{Q}_{v}
.

(b)

As
\vartheta_{v}
is defined as a composition, let us compute the effect of pushing forward along each of the parts. Let us write
W_{v}
for the Tamagawa measure on the middle scheme in the definition of
\vartheta_{v}
. As
X
is of the dimension
2
Theorem
3.11
says that
\sum_{\xi}\delta_{\xi,v}\omega_{v}
pushes forward to
\frac{1}{2}W_{v}
. For the final step, we remark that
\prod_{\xi}\Res_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}}X_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi}}\to\Sym^{2}X_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}}
is crepant, see Corollary
3.13
, and étale away from the diagonal, and hence locally the Tamagawa measures on
\coprod_{\xi}\left[\Res_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}}X_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi}}\right]({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})
and
\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})
agree. To compute the image measure, let
U
be an open subset of
\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})
contained in the complement of
\Delta
, then
\eta_{X,v}^{-1}(U)=U_{1}\coprod U_{2}
with
U
diffeomorphic to
U_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
and

\int_{U_{i}}\eta_{X,v}^{\ast}\omega_{v}^{(2)}=\int_{U}\omega_{v}^{(2)}.

Therefore, by definition of the image measure

\eta_{X,v*}W_{v}(U)=\sum_{i}W_{v}(U_{i})=2\omega_{v}(U).

Hence the image measure of
\frac{1}{2}W_{v}
equals
\omega_{v}
. ∎

This yields the following result at a good place.

Proposition 5.11
.

For
p\not\in\mathbb{S}
we have

\omega_{p}(\Hilb^{2}X(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))=\widetilde{w}_{p}.

Proof.

In this case, by Proposition
5.10
we see that

\omega_{p}(\Hilb^{2}X(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))=\sum_{\xi}\delta_{\xi,p}\omega_{p}(X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p,\xi}))=2\frac{w_{p}}{2p}+\frac{w_{p^{2}}}{2}+\frac{w^{2}_{p}}{2},

as
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
has two ramified quadratic extensions, an inert one and one split extension
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}^{2}
.
∎

Remark 5.12
.

Equivalently, one could prove that the adelic metric on
\Hilb^{2}X
at
p\not\in\mathbb{S}
is defined by a model, and hence we could count
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-points on
\Hilb^{2}{\mathcal{X}}
, which correpond to zero-dimensional degree
2
subschemes on a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-model
{\mathcal{X}}
of
X
. These are either pairs of disjoint points in
{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
, conjugate pairs in
{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p^{2}})\setminus{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
or a point in
{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})
with one of the
p+1
tangent directions. This yields the same result.

We now describe and compute the measure of the remaining factor

\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}},

which is more complicated, since there might be non-trivial reciprocity conditions imposed by the Brauer group. We generalise Proposition
5.10
to include all primes appearing in the Brauer–Manin obstruction.

Proposition 5.13
.

Consider the morphism

\vartheta^{{{Br}}}\colon\coprod_{\Xi}\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}\subseteq\coprod_{\Xi}\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}})\biggr)=\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\biggl(\coprod_{\xi_{v}}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}})\biggr)\xrightarrow{\prod_{v}\vartheta_{v}}\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v}),

where
\Xi
runs over all tuples
(\xi_{v})_{v}\in\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}^{\times,2}_{v}
.

(a)

The image of
\vartheta^{\text{Br}}
coincides up to a measure 0 locus with the image of

\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}\to\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})

induced by the Hilbert–Chow morphism
\epsilon_{X}\colon\Hilb^{2}X\to\Sym^{2}X
.

(b)

We have the following equality

\left(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\omega_{v}^{[2]}\right)\left[\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}\right]=\sum_{\Xi}\delta_{\Xi}w_{\Xi}(X),

where
w_{\Xi}(X)=\left(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\omega_{v}\right)\left[\left(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}})\right)^{\Br}\right]
as in Definition
4.13
.

Proof.

(a)

We remove here and in what follows the measure zero loci corresponding to the diagonal
\Delta^{(2)}\subseteq\Sym^{2}X
and its preimages.

It also suffices to compute the Brauer–Manin set with respect to the elements
{\mathcal{A}}\in\Br X
for which
\sum_{p\notin\mathbb{S}}\inv_{p}
is identically zero on
X({\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbb{Q}})
, since every element of the Brauer group is of this form modulo a constant class. For these classes we see that
\sum_{p\notin\mathbb{S}}\inv_{p}{\mathcal{A}}^{[2]}
is also identically zero on
\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbb{Q}})
by Proposition
3.6
. It is also immediate that
\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\notin\mathbb{S}_{K}}\inv_{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathcal{A}}
is identically zero on
X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})
for every quadratic field
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Let us start from a point
(Q_{v})_{v}\in\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})
. Each
Q_{v}
corresponds to a
{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi_{v}}
-point on
X
for some
\xi_{v}
. Let
K
be a quadratic field such that for all
v\in\mathbb{S}
we have
K_{v}\cong{\mathbb{Q}}_{v,\xi}
. Then
Q_{v}
corresponds to
(P_{\mathfrak{v}})_{\mathfrak{v}\mid v}\in\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\mid v}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})
and by Proposition
3.6
we have
\inv_{v}{\mathcal{A}}^{[2]}(Q_{v})=\sum_{\mathfrak{v}\mid v}\inv_{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathcal{A}}(P_{\mathfrak{v}})
.
We conclude that for
(P_{\mathfrak{v}})\in\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})
we have

\sum_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\inv_{v}{\mathcal{A}}^{[2]}(Q_{v})=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\sum_{\mathfrak{v}\mid v}\inv_{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathcal{A}}(P_{\mathfrak{v}}).

Since
\sum_{\mathfrak{p}\notin\mathbb{S}_{K}}\inv_{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\sum_{p\notin\mathbb{S}}\inv_{p}
are identically zero on
X({\mathbb{A}}_{K})
and
\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbb{Q}})
respectively, we see that

(Q_{v})\in\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}\quad\text{ if and only if }\quad(P_{\mathfrak{v}})\in\biggl(\prod_{\mathfrak{v}\in\mathbb{S}_{K}}X(K_{\mathfrak{v}})\biggr)^{\!\Br}.

(b)

Since the Hilbert–Chow morphism is a crepant resolution of singularities with exceptional locus the preimage of the diagonal,

\left(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\omega_{v}^{[2]}\right)\left[\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}\right]

is the measure of the image inside
\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Sym^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})
. We then use Proposition
5.10
to conclude. ∎

5.2.3.
The proof of Theorem
5.4

There is just one things left, before you can prove the results stated at the beginning of the subsection.

Proposition 5.14
.

The quantity
\widetilde{\lambda}_{p}
is the convergence factor for
\Hilb^{2}X
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the isomorphism of Galois modules
\Pic(\Hilb^{2}X)\cong\Pic X\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}
from Proposition
3.15
(b).
∎

We can now put everything together and compute the Tamagawa number of
\Hilb^{2}X
.

Proof of Theorem
5.4
.

Using the definition of the Tamagawa number and
L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Hilb^{2}{\overline{X}})=\zeta_{\mathbb{S}}(s)L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})
we get

\tau(\Hilb^{2}X)=\lim_{s\rightarrow 1^{+}}[(s-1)^{\rho(X)+1}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})\zeta(s)]\prod_{p\in\mathbb{S}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(\prod_{v}\widetilde{\lambda}_{v}^{-1}\omega^{[2]}_{v}\right)\biggl[\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbb{Q}})^{\Br}\biggr].

Since
\lim_{s\rightarrow 1^{+}}(s-1)\zeta(s)=1
, we may drop this term producing an expression more easily comparable to that in Theorem
4.17
.

By virtue of Proposition
5.7
and Definition
5.9
, we know that we can write

\left(\prod_{v}\widetilde{\lambda}_{v}^{-1}\omega^{[2]}_{v}\right)\biggl[\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{A}}_{\mathbb{Q}})^{\Br}\biggr]=\left(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\omega_{v}^{[2]}\right)\left[\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}\right]\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}\widetilde{\lambda}_{p}^{-1}\omega_{p}(\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p})).

Moreover, by Corollary
5.11
,

\omega_{p}(\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{p}))=\widetilde{w}_{p},

and by the previous proposition we have

\left(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\omega_{v}^{[2]}\right)\left[\biggl(\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}\Hilb^{2}X({\mathbb{Q}}_{v})\biggr)^{\!\text{Br}}\right]=\sum_{\Xi}\delta_{\Xi}w_{\Xi}(X).

This last sum becomes
\sum_{\Xi}\widetilde{\delta}_{\Xi}w_{\Xi}(X)
upon incorporating the term
\prod_{p\in\mathbb{S}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
5.5
.

By the inverse function theorem, a smooth variety over a number field has a positive Tamagawa measure if and only if its Brauer–Manin set is non-empty.
Theorem
5.4
shows that (i) is equivalent to
w_{\Xi}>0
for some
\Xi
. By Theorem
4.17
we see that
\tau(X_{K})>0
for
K\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\Xi}
holds precisely if
w_{\Xi}>0
.
∎

6.
A formal sum of quadratic Tamagawa numbers

The aim of the present section is to establish Theorem
1.2
on the sum of the Tamagawa numbers
\tau(X_{K})
over quadratic fields
K
of bounded discriminant, with
X
a weak Fano variety of dimension at least
2
. That is, if
\dim X\geq 3
then we show that the sum

\sum\limits_{K\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}(Y)}\tau(X_{K})

converges absolutely. If
X
is a surface then we obtain

\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)}\tau(X_{K})=\tau(\Hilb^{2}X)\log Y+O(1),

with the leading constant matching the expression developed in the previous section.

By Theorem
4.17
the Tamagawa number
\tau(X_{K})
over a quadratic extension
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
is the product of the following three components:

(1)

the discriminant factor
|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{-\dim X/2}
;

(2)

the Haar volume of
X
over the good primes along with their convergence factors coming from the twisted Artin
L
-function, given by

\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K}):=L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(1,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K})\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}\begin{cases}\lambda^{-1}_{p}w_{p}&\textup{ if }\chi_{\Delta}(p)=0;\\
\lambda^{-2}_{p}w^{2}_{p}&\textup{ if }\chi_{\Delta}(p)=1;\\
\lambda^{-1}_{p}\bar{\lambda}^{-1}_{p}w_{p^{2}}&\textup{ if }\chi_{\Delta}(p)=-1;\end{cases}

(3)

the weighted local volume over the bad primes, given by

\kappa_{\Xi}=\lim_{s\to 1}\left[(s-1)^{\rho(X)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})\right]w_{\Xi}(X).

We note that by Proposition
4.12
, this factor depends only on the splitting types of
K
over the primes lying above
\mathbb{S}
.

The main challenge of summing the Tamagawa number is summing the factor
\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})
over quadratic fields with a fixed splitting type
\Xi
. The factor
\kappa_{\Xi}
is constant among those fields, and we can work in the discriminant factor using partial summations. In order to accomplish the summing of
\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})
, we will re-arrange the order of summation to bring the
K
sum inside. The result will be a sum of the shape

\sum_{K}\chi_{K}(m)g(\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}),

where
g
is a multiplicative function.
Sums of this form are treated in §
6.1
. In order to apply these results to the sum of
\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})
, we need to express the
L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(1,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K})
factor as a function of
\chi_{K}
and
\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}
.
To accomplish this we prove very general on-average truncation results for such
L
-values in §
6.2
. The remaining sections are devoted to proving Theorem
1.3
and deducing some relevant and interesting corollaries to the developed theory in this section.

6.1.
Sums of multiplicative functions over fundamental discriminants

The purpose of this section is to establish Proposition
6.2
which allows us to sum multiplicative functions of the discriminant of quadratic fields. The key ingredient is the following result on mean values of multiplicative functions on squarefree integers.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and let
g
be a multiplicative function such that
g(p)=1+O(p^{-1})
. Then, for
Y\geq 2
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<d\leq Y\\
\gcd(d,m)=1\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(d)g(d)=Y\prod_{p\mid m}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\prod_{p\nmid m}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)+O_{\epsilon}\left(m^{\epsilon}Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right).

With the error term
O_{\epsilon}\left(m^{\epsilon}Y^{\frac{5}{6}+\epsilon}\right)
this would be a standard consequence of the contour integration method in analytic number theory. We record the proof here since a judicious application of fractional moments bounds allows us to produce an error term that is best possible, which will be important for a later application.

Proof.

We proceed by the Selberg–Delange method. The Dirichlet series associated to the sum is

F(s):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d=1\\
\gcd(d,m)=1\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{\mu^{2}(d)g(d)}{d^{s}}=\prod_{p\nmid m}\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p^{s}}\right),

for
\Rea(s)>1
. The assumption on our function
g
implies that
F(s)=\zeta(s)G(s)
for
G
some function which is holomorphic and absolutely convergent for
\Rea(s)>\frac{1}{2}
. The truncated Perron formula (e.g.
[
Tit86
, Lem. 3.19]
) gives

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\leq Y\\
\gcd(d,m)=1\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(d)g(d)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{1+\frac{1}{\log Y}-iT}^{1+\frac{1}{\log Y}+iT}F(s)\frac{Y^{s}}{s}\mathrm{d}s+O\left(\tau(m)\frac{Y\log Y}{T}\right)

for any
T\geq 1
, which will be chosen at the end of the proof. We now move the line of integration back to the line
\Rea(s)=\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
, using a rectangular contour with a keyhole around
s=1
. The contribution from the circle around
s=1
will be the residue of the integrand at the pole, namely
YG(1)
. It remains to bound the contribution from the rest of the contour.
Along the horizontal strips, applying Weyl’s subconvexity bound for
\zeta(s)
, we have the contribution

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}^{1+\frac{1}{\log Y}}F(\sigma\pm iT)\frac{Y^{\sigma\pm iT}}{\sigma\pm iT}\mathrm{d}\sigma\ll m^{\epsilon}\frac{1}{T}\int_{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}^{1+\frac{1}{\log Y}}T^{\frac{1-\sigma}{3}}Y^{\sigma}\mathrm{d}\sigma\ll m^{\epsilon}\frac{Y}{T\log(Y/T^{1/3})}.

For the contribution from the vertical strip, we break into dyadic intervals

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle\int_{-T}^{T}F\left(\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon+it\right)\frac{Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon+it}}{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon+it}\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}}{2\pi}\left(2\sum_{\ell=0}^{\log_{2}T}\int_{T/2^{\ell+1}}^{T/2^{\ell}}\left|F\left(\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon+it\right)\right|\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{1+|t|}+\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left|F\left(\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon+it\right)\right|\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{1+|t|}\right)

\displaystyle\ll m^{\epsilon}Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\log_{2}T}\frac{1}{T/2^{\ell}}\int_{T/2^{\ell+1}}^{T/2^{\ell}}\left|\zeta\left(\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon+it\right)\right|\mathrm{d}t.

The innermost integral is a small fractional moment of zeta on the critical line which is known to be
\ll\frac{T}{2^{\ell}}\log(T/2^{\ell})^{1/4}
, by work of Heath-Brown
[
HB81
]
. Computing the summation over
\ell
gives the bound
\ll Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}m^{\epsilon}T^{\epsilon}
.
The combined error is minimised by taking
T=Y^{\frac{1}{2}}
, from which the lemma follows.
∎

Recall from Definition
4.11
, that
\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}
is the family of quadratic extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}
with splitting type over the primes in
\mathbb{S}
specified by
\Xi=(\xi_{v})_{v}\in\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times,2}
. For any
K\in\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}
, we denote by
\Delta_{K}
the associated fundamental discriminant.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
be a finite set of places of
{\mathbb{Q}}
and set
S:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathbb{S}\\
p<\infty\end{subarray}}p
.
Let
g
be a multiplicative function which is
1
on primes in
\mathbb{S}
and otherwise
g(p)=1+O(p^{-1})
. Let
m
be an integer coprime to
S
. Then we have

\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}(Y)}\!\!\chi_{\Delta_{K}}(m)g(\Delta_{K})=\begin{cases}\makebox[27.77783pt][l]{ }O\left(m^{1/4}\sqrt{\log(4mS)}\sqrt{Y}\right)&\hskip-34.14322pt\text{ if $m\neq\square$};\\
\delta_{\Xi}Y\prod_{p\in\mathbb{S}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\prod^{S}_{p\mid m}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\prod^{S}_{p\nmid m}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)&\\
\quad\quad+O_{\epsilon}\left((mS)^{\epsilon}Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right)&\hskip-34.14322pt\text{ if $m=\square$}.\end{cases}

Here
\delta_{\Xi}
is the constant defined in Definition
5.3
.

The case
g\equiv 1
and
\mathbb{S}=\{\infty\}
was handled previously by Schmidt
[
Sch95
, Lem. 15]
.

Proof.

Denote
S^{\prime}:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\neq 2\\
p\in\mathbb{S}\end{subarray}}p
.
We will break the proof into cases depending on the splitting types specified by
\Xi
. We have two subcases depending on whether we choose
+1
or
-1
at the place
\infty
, let us suppose for now that we have chosen
+1
; the other case has a similar proof replacing
d
by
-d
.

We first deal with the case when each quadratic extension
{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}
is unramified, so we start here. We will represent fundamental discriminants by positive integers
d
with
d\equiv 1\mod 4
or
4d
where
d\equiv 2
or
3\mod 4
. The other splitting conditions for each
p\in\mathbb{S}
can be expressed as a congruence condition

\text{i.e. }\exists H\subseteq{\mathbb{Z}}/8S^{\prime}{\mathbb{Z}}\text{ such that for }K\in\mathcal{F}(Y),\Delta_{K}\in\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}(Y)\iff\Delta_{K}\in H\bmod{8S^{\prime}}.

If
\xi_{2}
corresponds to an unramified extension then
\Delta_{K}
is equal to a power of 4 times a number which is congruent to 1 mod 8. The only way this can happen is if
\Delta_{K}\equiv 1\mod 8
.
At odd primes, the assumption that each extension associated to
\Xi
is unramified means that each residue class in
H
is primitive.

Hence, the sum we are left to evaluate is

\sum_{a\in H}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<d\leq Y\\
d\equiv a\bmod{8S^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(d)\chi_{d}(m)g(d).

We will remove the congruence condition with a sum of Dirichlet characters (here it is important that our congruences are primitive), giving

\frac{1}{4\phi(S^{\prime})}\sum_{a\in H}\sum_{\psi\bmod{8S^{\prime}}}\overline{\psi(a)}\sum_{0<d\leq Y}\mu^{2}(d)\chi_{d}(m)\psi(d)g(d).

When the product
\chi_{d}(m)\psi(d)
is a non-principal character there will be oscillation and the contribution will be negligible. The main contribution comes when the product corresponds to the principal character. On such an occasion, the inner sum above becomes after using Lemma
6.1

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<d\leq Y\\
\gcd(d,mS)=1\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(d)g(d)=Y\prod_{p\mid mS}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\prod_{p\nmid mS}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)+O_{\epsilon}\left((mS)^{\epsilon}Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right).

If
m\neq 1
then, since
m
is coprime to
S
, the product of characters is only principal when both characters are principal. Otherwise, the product is principal precisely when
\psi
is the unique character mod
S
which is induced by the non-principal character mod 4, and by the principal character modulo all other primes in
\mathbb{S}
. Either way, there is a single unique
\psi
mod
S
which contributes to the main term. Therefore, the total sum in question is equal to

\frac{\#H}{4\phi(S^{\prime})}Y\prod_{p\mid mS}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\prod_{p\nmid mS}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)+O_{\epsilon}\left((mS)^{\epsilon}Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right).

All that remains is to observe that this leading term is equal to
\frac{1}{4}\prod_{p\mid S^{\prime}}\frac{\#H_{p}}{p-1}
, where
H_{p}
is the image of
H
under the map
{\mathbb{Z}}/S{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}/p{\mathbb{Z}}
. For odd primes
p
,
H_{p}
consists either of all the quadratic residues or of the quadratic non-residues modulo
p
, hence we get a factor of
\frac{1}{2}
at each place in
S
.
In the case, that
\chi_{d}(m)\psi(d)
is a non-principal character, say
\sigma
, it has modulus at most
4mS
. Applying the identity
\mu^{2}(n)=\sum_{d^{2}\mid n}\mu(d)
, we have

\sum_{0<d\leq Y}\mu^{2}(d)\sigma(d)g(d)=\sum_{\ell\leq\sqrt{Y}}\mu(\ell)\sigma(\ell)^{2}g(\ell^{2})\sum_{d\leq Y/\ell^{2}}\sigma(d)g(d).

We will write
g=\mathbf{1}\ast h
for
h(n)
a multiplicative function which, by virtue of our assumption on
g
, satisfies
h(p)=O\left(\frac{1}{p}\right).
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{0<d\leq Y}\mu^{2}(d)\sigma(d)g(d)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\leq\sqrt{Y}}\mu(\ell)\sigma(\ell)^{2}g(\ell^{2})\sum_{ab\leq Y/\ell^{2}}\sigma(ab)h(a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\leq Y}\sigma(a)h(a)\sum_{\ell\leq\sqrt{Y/a}}\mu(\ell)\sigma(\ell)^{2}g(\ell^{2})\sum_{b\leq Y/\ell^{2}a}\sigma(b).

This inner sum is at most
\max\{\sqrt{4mS}\log 4mS,Y/\ell^{2}a\}
by the Pólya–Vinogradov inequality. Thus

\sum_{0<d\leq Y}\mu^{2}(d)\sigma(d)g(d)\ll\sqrt{mS}\log(4mS)\sum_{a\leq\sqrt{mS}\log(4mS)}|h(a)|\sum_{\ell\leq\sqrt{Y}(mS)^{-1/4}(\log(4mS)^{-1}a^{-1/2}}\mu^{2}(\ell)|g(\ell)|

+Y\sum_{a<Y}\frac{|h(a)|}{a}\sum_{\sqrt{\sqrt{mS}\log(4mS)}/a<\ell<\sqrt{Y/a}}\mu^{2}(\ell)\frac{|g(\ell)|}{\ell^{2}}

By Lemma
6.1
, this is

\ll_{\epsilon}(mS)^{\frac{1}{4}+\epsilon}\sqrt{Y}\sum_{a\leq Y}\frac{|h(a)|}{\sqrt{a}}.

The inner sum converges due to the conditions on
h
.

Now, we must allow for wild ramification, and thus non-primitive congruences. As before, the splitting conditions can be expressed as congruences, but at odd primes
p
where the extension corresponding to
\Xi
is ramified we use a non-primitive congruence modulo
p^{2}
. Let
H
be the set of permissible congruences to the modulus
16S^{2}
. Let
a\in H
and denote
k=\gcd(a,S^{\prime})
. Note that this definition is independent of the choice of
a
. We begin at the same starting point as before then remove a factor of
k
to make all the congruences modulo odd primes primitive. Moreover, if the extension at 2 is ramified then we must remove a factor of 4. This gives

\sum_{a\in H}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<d\leq Y/4^{\delta}\\
d\equiv a\bmod S^{2}\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(d)\chi_{d}(m)g(d)=\chi_{4k}(m)g(4k)\sum_{b\in\widetilde{H}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<d\leq Y/4^{\delta}k\\
d\equiv b\bmod 16S\\
\gcd(d,k)=1\end{subarray}}\chi_{d}(m)g(d),

where
\widetilde{H}
is a set of primitive congruence classes modulo
S
capturing the splitting information of
H
and
\delta
is either 1 or 0, depending on the splitting condition at 2. For instance, at an odd prime
p
, the extension specified by
\xi_{p}
is generated either by
\sqrt{p}
or by
\sqrt{ap}
for
a
a quadratic non-residue mod
p
. The two ramified extensions of
{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}
correspond to congruence classes
ap
mod
p^{2}
where
a
is either a quadratic residue or quadratic non-residue depending on the desired extension.
{\mathbb{Q}}_{2}
has 7 unique quadratic extensions: 3 generated by pairs of primitive residue classes mod 16 (although it is impossible in our situation for
\Delta_{K}
to be congruent to 5 mod 16) and 4 generated by a unique even residue mod 16.
We will break our sum over
b\in\widetilde{H}
into two cases corresponding to whether the residue classes specified mod 16 are even or odd, denoted
b\in\widetilde{H}^{e}
or
d\in\widetilde{H}^{o}
. If
\xi
corresponds to the trivial extension at 2 then all the residue classes are odd (namely 1 and 9 mod 16).
If
b\in\widetilde{H}^{o}
, then the analysis may now proceed as before. Again, when
m
is non-square we get the same error term. When
m
is a square, we have
\chi_{k}(m)=1
and since
g
is defined to be 1 on all primes in
S
the main term becomes

\frac{1}{8\phi(S)}\sum_{b\in\widetilde{H}^{e}}\sum_{0<d\leq Y/4^{\delta}k}\mu^{2}(d)g(d).

If only odd primes ramify then we see that the only difference between this and the previous case is the presence of
k
on the denominator, as explained in the factor
\delta_{\Xi}
. If the splitting condition specified by
\xi_{2}
corresponds to the extensions
{\mathbb{Q}}_{2}(\sqrt{3})
or
{\mathbb{Q}}_{2}(\sqrt{7})
then 2 ramifies and so
\delta=1
and then there are two possible residue classes mod 16 which
b
may occupy (and
\widetilde{H}^{e}
is empty). This corresponds to dividing by precisely the factor expected in the definition of
\delta_{\Xi}
.

If
\xi_{2}
corresponds to any of the extensions
{\mathbb{Q}}_{2}(\sqrt{2}),{\mathbb{Q}}_{2}(\sqrt{6}),{\mathbb{Q}}_{2}(\sqrt{10})
or
{\mathbb{Q}}_{2}(\sqrt{14})
, then
\widetilde{H}^{o}
is empty. To handle the sum over
b\in\widetilde{H}^{e}
, we must divide through by 2. Now the splitting condition at 2 corresponds to a choice of primitive residue class mod 8. Hence the main term contribution is

\frac{1}{4\phi(S)}\sum_{b\in\widetilde{H}^{e}}\sum_{0<d\leq Y/(2\times 4k)}\mu^{2}(d)g(d).

Again we see that the factor out front after applying Lemma
6.1
will correspond precisely to the definition of
\delta_{\Xi}
, completing the proof.
∎

6.2.
Sums of twists of
L
-functions

We will require a truncation estimate for special values of quadratic twist
L
-functions. Unfortunately, the pointwise bounds that one is able to provide (without assuming GRH) are insufficient. However, the following on-average result will suffice for our application.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\rho
be a Galois representation which factors through a finite extension
L/{\mathbb{Q}}
. For a real parameter
Y\geq 1
, recall the notation
\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)
for the set of fundamental discriminants
\Delta
absolutely bounded by
Y
whose associated quadratic field
K
is linearly disjoint from
L
.
Let
Z\geq Y^{\delta}
be a real parameter, for some
\delta>0
, and let
c_{\Delta}
be an absolutely bounded sequence of complex numbers, supported on fundamental discriminants
\Delta
. Then, for any
\epsilon>0
, we have

\sum_{\Delta\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)}c_{\Delta}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(1,\rho\otimes\chi_{\Delta})=\sum_{\Delta\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)}c_{\Delta}\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{n\leq Z}\frac{\psi(n)\chi_{\Delta}(n)}{n}+O_{\mathbb{S},\rho,\epsilon}\left(Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}Z^{\epsilon}+Y^{1+\epsilon}Z^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right),

where
\psi(n)
is the multiplicative function defined by

L_{{\mathbb{Q}},p}(s,\rho)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{\psi(p^{j})}{p^{js}}.

In the above statement,
\mathbb{S}
can be taken to be any finite set of places and we remind the reader of our conventions that
S=\prod_{p\in\mathbb{S},p<\infty}p
,
\sum^{S}_{n}
denotes a sum over integers coprime to
S
, and
\prod^{S}_{p}
denotes a product indexed by primes not in
\mathbb{S}
, respectively.
We will ultimately be applying this result in the case that
\rho
is the Galois representation associated to
\Pic{\overline{X}}
. In this case,
L
is the splitting field of
\Pic{\overline{X}}
, as in §
4
.

Remark 6.4
.

In the work of Schmidt and Le Rudulier on
\Hilb^{2}{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}
and
\Hilb^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1})
, it was also necessary to truncate
L
-series. In Schmidt, the
L
-series is simply
L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\emptyset}(1,\chi_{\Delta})
and in Le Rudulier
L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\emptyset}(1,\chi_{\Delta})^{2}
,
[
Sch95
, Lem. 16]
and
[
LR14
, Lem. 5.3]
, respectively. In these cases, it is possible to truncate each individual
L
-series with an acceptable error term. However, for more general
L
-functions (even for moments of
L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\emptyset}(1,\chi_{\Delta})
beyond the third) this simple approach fails unconditionally hence the need for an on-average truncation.

The key tool that will allow us to truncate on average is a variation on the celebrated quadratic large sieve of Heath-Brown.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\alpha_{m}
and
\beta_{n}
be complex sequence satisfying
|\alpha_{m}|\ll 1
for all
m
and
|\beta_{n}|\ll\frac{\tau_{r}(n)^{C}}{n}
for all
n
, for absolute constants
C
and
r
. Then, for all
M,N\geq 1
and
\epsilon>0
, we have

(a)

if
N\leq M^{2}
then

\sum_{m\leq M}\sum_{N\leq n<2N}\alpha_{m}\beta_{n}\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)\ll_{\epsilon,C}M^{1+\epsilon}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}+M^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon},\quad\text{ and if }

(b)

if
N>M^{2}
then

\sum_{m\leq M}\sum_{N\leq n<2N}\alpha_{m}\beta_{n}\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)\ll_{r,C}M^{\frac{1}{2}}(\log N)^{\frac{r^{2C}-1}{2}}.

Proof.

(a)

This is a variation on
[
HB95
, Cor. 4]
. Let
\gamma_{n}=\beta_{n}n^{1-\epsilon}
, which by assumption is bounded. Then the sum in question is

\sum_{m\leq M}\sum_{N\leq n<2N}\alpha_{m}\frac{\gamma_{n}}{n^{1-\epsilon}}\left(\frac{m}{n}\right).

We write this as

\sum_{N\leq n<2N}\frac{\delta_{n}}{n^{1-\epsilon}},

where
\delta_{n}=\gamma_{n}\sum_{m\leq M}\alpha_{m}\left(\frac{m}{n}\right).

The bound now follows from partial summation using
[
HB95
, Cor. 4]
and the fact that
N^{\epsilon}\ll M^{2\epsilon}
.

(b)

Now suppose that
N>M^{2}
. By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, the left hand side can be bounded by

\ll M^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{m\leq M}\left|\sum_{N\leq n<2N}\beta_{n}\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We now apply the standard large sieve for primitive characters
[
HB95
, Eqn. (3)]
producing the bound

\ll M^{\frac{1}{2}}\left((M^{2}+N)\sum_{N\leq n<2N}|\beta_{n}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

This becomes

\ll M^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\left(\frac{M^{2}}{N}+1\right)(\log N)^{r^{2C}-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

using the standard estimate
\sum_{N\leq n<2N}\tau_{r}(n)^{A}\ll N(\log N)^{r^{A}-1}
. ∎

We will also require a bound on the growth of
\psi
.

Lemma 6.6
.

For the multiplicative function
\psi
associated to a Galois representation
\rho
there are constants
C>0
and
r>0
only depending on
\dim\rho
such that

\psi(n)\ll\tau_{r}(n)^{C}.

We need the bound in terms of divisor functions to be able to apply Lemma
6.5
. For all other applications the bound
\psi(n)\ll n^{\epsilon}
will suffice.

Proof.

Consider all cyclotomic polynomials
\Phi_{n}
of degree at most
\dim\rho
, of which there are finitely many. Since
(X^{n}-1)/\Phi_{n}(X)
is a polynomial we see that the expansion of
\Phi_{n}(X)^{-1}
has only finitely many different coefficients. So all their coefficients are bounded by an absolute constant
C^{\prime}
depending only on
\dim\rho
.

We will first bound
\psi(p^{k})
for a prime number
p
. Since
\Frob_{p}
has finite order the characteristic polynomial is the product of at most
\dim\rho
cyclotomic polynomials of degree at most
\dim\rho
. We conclude that
\psi(p^{k})\leq C^{\prime\dim\rho}\tau_{\dim\rho}(p^{k})
. Now we find
\psi(n)=\prod_{p^{k}\mid\!\mid n}\psi(p^{k})\leq C^{\prime\omega(n)\dim\rho}\tau_{\dim\rho}(n)
. Using
2^{\omega(n)}\leq\tau_{2}(n)
and taking
r=\max\{2,\dim\rho\}
we find
\psi(n)\ll\tau_{r}(n)^{C}
for
C>0
only depending on
\dim\rho
.
∎

We now have all the necessary prerequisites to prove our on-average truncation result.

Proof of Proposition
6.3
.

We start by observing that
L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\rho\otimes\chi_{\Delta})
is the
L
-function attached to the Galois representation associated to
\rho
twisted by the quadratic character
\chi_{\Delta}
. Writing the local factors
L_{p}(s,\rho)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\psi(p^{k})p^{-ks}
, we can express the twisted local factor as
\sum_{k\geq 0}\psi(p^{k})\chi_{\Delta}(p^{k})p^{-ks}.
Expanding the resulting Euler product gives the series appearing in the main term of the statement.

We break the sum in the main term into three parts

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leq Z}\frac{\psi(n)\chi_{\Delta}(n)}{n}+\sum_{Z<n\leq e^{Z^{\epsilon}}}\frac{\psi(n)\chi_{\Delta}(n)}{n}+\sum_{n>e^{Z^{\epsilon}}}\frac{\psi(n)\chi_{\Delta}(n)}{n}

\displaystyle=:\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}+\Sigma_{3}.

Substituting this into the original sum will yield 3 corresponding sums,
S_{1},S_{2}
and
S_{3}
with

S_{i}=\sum_{\Delta\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)}c_{\Delta}\Sigma_{i}.

The sum
S_{1}
is the main term and hence it suffices to bound
S_{2}
and
S_{3}
. We start with
S_{3}
which can be bounded using partial summation.
We write

\sum_{n>e^{Z^{\epsilon}}}\frac{\psi(n)\chi_{\Delta}(n)}{n}=\sum_{n>e^{Z^{\epsilon}}}\frac{1}{n}(B_{n}-B_{n-1}),

where
B_{n}=\sum_{b\leq n}\psi(b)\chi_{\Delta}(b)
.
We claim that

B_{n}\ll_{\rho,A}\frac{n}{\log^{A}n}|\Delta|.

This leads to a bound of

\sum_{n>e^{Z^{\epsilon}}}\frac{\psi(n)\chi_{\Delta}(n)}{n}\ll_{\rho,A}Z^{-A\epsilon}|\Delta|.

Summing this over
\Delta
gives an error of size
Z^{-A\epsilon}Y^{2}
which is clearly sufficient if we choose
A
large enough.

We first suppose that
\rho
is irreducible.
The twisted representation
\rho\otimes\chi_{\Delta}
factors through
\Gal(L/{\mathbb{Q}})\times{\mathbb{Z}}/2{\mathbb{Z}}
. The conductor of this twisted representation is of size
O_{\rho}(|\Delta|)
. By the Brauer induction theorem (see
[
IK04
, Eqn. 5.108]
), the Artin
L
-function attached to the twisted representation can be written as a product of integer powers of Hecke
L
-functions. Since we cannot rule out the possibility that the powers are negative, we bound using the approach of the prime number theorem for Hecke
L
-functions (e.g.
[
Gol70
]
). Since
\Delta\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)
, the product
\psi\chi_{\Delta}
is a non-principal Hecke character and thus we have the bound

\sum_{b\leq n}\psi(n)\chi_{\Delta}(n)\ll_{A}\frac{n}{\log^{A}n}q(\rho\otimes\chi_{\Delta})\ll_{\rho,A}\frac{n}{\log^{A}n}|\Delta|,

(6.1)

as claimed.
Now suppose that
\rho
is reducible and has
t
irreducible subrepresentations. Then the function
\psi(n)
is the convolution of
t
multiplicative functions
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{t}
.
Moreover, since
K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)
, we have that
\psi_{i}\chi_{\Delta}
is associated to a non-trivial irreducible representation for each
i=1,\ldots,t
.
Hence,

\displaystyle B_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\leq n}(\psi_{1}\ast\ldots\ast\psi_{t})(b)\chi_{\Delta}(b)=\sum_{b_{1}\cdots b_{t}\leq n}\psi_{1}(b_{1})\chi_{\Delta}(b_{1})\cdots\psi_{t}(b_{t})\chi_{\Delta}(b_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{b_{1}\cdots b_{t-1}\leq n}\psi_{1}(b_{1})\chi_{\Delta}(b_{1})\cdots\psi_{t-1}(b_{t-1})\chi_{\Delta}(b_{t-1})\sum_{b_{t}\leq n/b_{1}\cdots b_{t-1}}\psi_{t}(b_{t})\chi_{\Delta}(b_{t}).

Applying the bound (
6.1
) to the innermost sum, we have the bound

\displaystyle B_{n}

\displaystyle\ll_{\rho,A}|\Delta|^{\deg\rho}\sum_{b_{1}\cdots b_{t-1}\leq n}\frac{n}{b_{1}\cdots b_{t-1}\log(n/b_{1}\cdots b_{t-1})^{A+t}}

\displaystyle\ll_{\rho,A}|\Delta|^{\deg\rho}\frac{n}{\log^{A+t}n}\sum_{b_{1}\cdots b_{t-1}\leq n}\frac{1}{b_{1}\cdots b_{t-1}}\left(1+O\left(\frac{\log(b_{1}\cdots b_{t-1})}{\log n}\right)\right),

from which the claim is immediate.

Finally, we estimate
S_{2}
, which is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{\Delta\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)}c_{\Delta}\sum_{Z<n\leq e^{Z^{\epsilon}}}\frac{\psi(n)\chi_{\Delta}(n)}{n}

Since
\Delta
is a fundamental discriminant, we may write it as either a squarefree integer
d\equiv 1\mod 4
or
4d
for squarefree
d\equiv 2,3\mod 4
. We partition
\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)
into 4 disjoint sets

\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)=\mathcal{D}_{-}\cup\mathcal{D}_{+}\cup\mathcal{E}_{-}\cup\mathcal{E}_{+}.

Here
\mathcal{D}
and
\mathcal{E}
correspond to a fundamental discriminant being either 1 or 0 mod 4, and the subscript indicates the sign of the fundamental discriminant.
We will bound the contribution from
\mathcal{D}_{+}
, the other bounds being mutatis mutandis.
We break the
n
sum into dyadic intervals

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z\leq N\leq e^{Z^{\epsilon}}\\
\text{dyadic}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\Delta\in\mathcal{D}_{+}}c_{\Delta}\sum_{N\leq n<2N}\frac{\psi(n)\chi_{\Delta}(n)}{n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z\leq N\leq e^{Z^{\epsilon}}\\
\text{dyadic}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<d\leq Y\\
d\equiv 1\text{mod}4\\
d\in\mathcal{Q}(Y)\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(d)c_{d}\sum_{N\leq n<2N}\frac{\psi(n)\chi_{d}(n)}{n}.

Set
\beta_{n}=\frac{\psi(n)}{n}
and
\alpha_{d}
to be
\mu^{2}(d)c_{d}
multiplied by the indicator function of being congruent to 1 mod 4 and the indicator function of belonging to the set
\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)
. Now we can apply Lemma
6.5
. Observe that by definition
\alpha_{d}
is absolutely bounded and
|\beta_{n}|\ll\frac{\tau_{r}(n)^{C}}{n}
with
r
as in Lemma
6.6
.
Hence, our sum is bounded by

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z<N\leq Y^{2}\\
\text{dyadic}\end{subarray}}\left(Y^{1+\epsilon}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}+Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y^{2}<N\leq e^{Z^{\epsilon}}\\
\text{dyadic}\end{subarray}}Y^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\log N\right)^{\frac{r^{2C}-1}{2}}.

The first of these terms is at most
Y^{1+\epsilon}Z^{-\frac{1}{2}}+Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}
,
and the latter is

Y^{\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z<N\leq e^{Z^{\epsilon}}\\
\text{dyadic}\end{subarray}}(\log N)^{\frac{r^{2C}-1}{2}}\ll_{\epsilon}Y^{\frac{1}{2}}Z^{\frac{r^{2C}-1}{2}\epsilon}.

This suffices since
r
depends only on
\rho
.
∎

6.3.
Functions arising from Euler factors

Our ultimate aim in the sequel is to sum an Euler product defined in quadratic fields over all such quadratic fields.
We define here some terms which will appear in these Euler products.

Definition 6.7
.

For
p\notin\mathbb{S}
and
w_{p}
,
w_{p^{2}}
,
\lambda_{p}
and
\bar{\lambda}_{p}
as defined in Definition
4.14
we define

f_{p}=\lambda^{-1}_{p}w_{p}\quad\text{ and }\quad f_{p^{2}}=\bar{\lambda}^{-1}_{p}\lambda^{-1}_{p}w_{p^{2}}.

Recall that
X
is a weak Fano variety over
{\mathbb{Q}}
of dimension at least 2. The functions
f_{p}
and
f_{p^{2}}
arise naturally within
\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})
.
Indeed, if
p
is a prime which ramifies in
K
, then the Euler factor in
\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})
is simply
f_{p}
. If the prime is inert, then it is
f_{p^{2}}
and if it is split, the factor equals
f_{p}^{2}
. Hence, for unramified primes, the Euler factor in
\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})
at
p
can be expressed as

\frac{f_{p}^{2}+f_{p^{2}}}{2}+\chi_{K}(p)\frac{f_{p}^{2}-f_{p^{2}}}{2}.

(6.2)

In manipulating
\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})
, we will encounter several multiplicative functions created from these
f_{p}
factors.

Definition 6.8
.

We define the following functions

(i)

f(d)=\prod^{S}_{p\mid d}f_{p}
;

(ii)

F(a)=\prod^{S}_{p\mid a}\frac{f_{p}^{2}+f_{p^{2}}}{2}
;

(iii)

G(b)=\prod^{S}_{p\mid b}\frac{f_{p}^{2}-f_{p^{2}}}{2}
;

(iv)

R(b)=\frac{G(b)}{F(b)}
, and

(v)

g(d)=\prod^{S}_{p\mid d}\frac{2f_{p}}{f_{p}^{2}+f_{p^{2}}}
.

Lemma 6.9
.

The following properties all hold.

(a)

All the functions in Definition
6.8
are multiplicative.

(b)

We have
f_{p}=1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)
and
f_{p^{2}}=1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3}}\right)
.

(c)

The product
\prod_{p}f_{p}
converges.

(d)

The function
g
is 1 on primes in
\mathbb{S}
and otherwise
g(p)=1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)
.

(e)

There exists an absolute constant
A
such that
|g(n)|\leq A
for all
n
.

(f)

For squarefree
n
, we have
R(n)\ll\frac{1}{n^{3/2}}.

Proof.

(a)

This is immediate from the definition as a product.

(b)

Since
p\notin\mathbb{S}
, we have that
w_{p}=\frac{\#{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})}{p^{\dim X}}
and
w_{p^{2}}=\frac{\#{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p^{2}})}{p^{2\dim X}}
. Peyre
[
Pey95
, p. 117]
) then provides the expression

\lambda_{p}^{-1}\frac{\#{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p})}{p^{\dim X}}=1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\bar{\lambda}_{p}^{-1}\lambda_{p}^{-1}\frac{\#{\mathcal{X}}({\mathbb{F}}_{p^{2}})}{p^{2\dim X}}=1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3}}\right).

(c)

This follows immediately from the previous part. Indeed, the correction factors
\lambda^{-1}_{p}
are introduced precisely to ensure this convergence.

(d)

By the definition of
\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}
, the function
g
takes the value 1 on primes in
\mathbb{S}
. For
p\not\in\mathbb{S}
, we have

g(p)=\frac{2f_{p}}{f_{p}^{2}+f_{p^{2}}}=\frac{2\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)\right)}{\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)\right)^{2}+1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3}}\right)}=\frac{2+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)}{2+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)}.

(e)

This follows immediately from the previous part.

(f)

Similarly, we have

R(p)=\frac{f_{p}^{2}-f_{p^{2}}}{f_{p}^{2}+f_{p^{2}}}=\frac{\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)\right)^{2}-1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3}}\right)}{\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)\right)^{2}+1+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3}}\right)}=\frac{O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)}{2+O\left(\frac{1}{p^{3/2}}\right)}.

We conclude that
R(p)\ll p^{-3/2}
and the claim follows by multiplicativity. ∎

6.4.
Summing Euler products over quadratic fields

We are now ready to prove our main result on summing Tamagawa numbers.

Theorem 6.10
.

Let
X/{\mathbb{Q}}
be a weak Fano variety of dimension at least
2
.

(a)

If
\dim X\geq 3
then the sum
\sum\limits_{K\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}(Y)}\tau(X_{K})
converges absolutely.

(b)

If
X
is a surface, then as
Y\to\infty
, we have

\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}(Y)}\tau(X_{K})=\tau(\Hilb^{2}X)\log Y+O(1).

We remark that the sum can be extended to all quadratic fields of bounded discriminant with the same result, as there are only finitely many quadratic fields not linearly disjoint to
L
. We have opted to exclude them as it is possible that
N_{K}(X,B)
possible has a larger order of magnitude for
K\notin{\mathcal{K}}(X)
by the discussion in §
4.1
.

We first start by specifying the local splitting behaviour of the extension at the places in
\mathbb{S}
. Theorem
6.10
follows by summing over all possible such choices. Recall that in the case
X
is a surface we have

\tau(\Hilb^{2}X)=\lim_{s\to 1}\left[(s-1)^{\rho(X)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})\right]\left[\sum_{\Xi}\widetilde{\delta}_{\Xi}w_{\Xi}(X)\right]\left[\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{p}\widetilde{w}_{p}\right],

so the theorem follows from the following proposition.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
denote the set of places of
{\mathbb{Q}}
defined in Definition
4.5
, and fix
\Xi\in\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times,2}
.

(a)

If
\dim X>2
then
