\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}\tau(X_{K})

converges absolutely.

(b)

If
\dim X=2
then

\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}\tau(X_{K})\sim\lim_{s\to 1}\left[(s-1)^{\rho(X)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})\right]\left[\widetilde{\delta}_{\Xi}w_{\Xi}(X)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{p}\widetilde{w}_{p}\right]\log Y

as
Y\to\infty
.

Proof.

As discussed in the beginning of this section, the Tamagawa number
\tau(X_{K})
may be factored as the product
\kappa_{\Xi}|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{-\frac{\dim X}{2}}\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})
.
We will first determine
\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})
, then trivially incorporate
\kappa_{\Xi}
and then use partial summation to work in the remaining factor
|\Delta_{K}|^{-1}
.

In (
6.2
), we deduced an expression for the Euler factor at an unramified prime
p
. Plugging this expression, and the functions defined in Definition
6.8
, into the expression for
\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})
we find

\displaystyle\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})

\displaystyle=\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(1,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K})\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\mid\Delta_{K}}f_{p}\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\nmid\Delta_{K}}\left[F(p)+\chi_{K}(p)G(p)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(1,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K})\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}F(p)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\mid\Delta_{K}}\frac{f_{p}}{F(p)}\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\nmid\Delta_{K}}\left[1+\chi_{K}(p)R(p)\right]

\displaystyle=\left(\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}F(p)\right)\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}\left(L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(1,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K})g(\Delta_{K})\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{b\geq 1}\mu^{2}(b)\chi_{K}(b)R(b)\right),

where we have used that
\prod^{S}F(p)
converges absolutely since
\prod^{S}f_{p}
does and
f_{p^{2}}=1+O(p^{-3})
.
The sum over
b
is absolutely convergent by Lemma
6.9
(e), and is bounded uniformly in
K
.
Re-arranging, we have

\left(\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}F(p)\right)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{b\geq 1}\mu^{2}(b)R(b)\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(1,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K})g(\Delta_{K})\chi_{K}(b).

As the sum defining
L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(1,\Pic{\overline{X}}\otimes\chi_{K})
is only conditionally convergent and so to aid further manipulation of the sum, we will truncate it.

We apply Proposition
6.3
with
c_{\Delta}
equal to
\chi_{K}(b)g(\Delta)
. For
Z
a power of
Y
to be determined later,
we conclude that
\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})
is given by

\left(\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}F(p)\right)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{b\geq 1}\mu^{2}(b)R(b)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{n\leq Z}\left(\frac{\psi(n)}{n}\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}\chi_{K}(nb)g(\Delta_{K})\right),

(6.3)

plus an error of size
O_{\mathbb{S},\epsilon}(Z^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}Y^{1+\epsilon}+Z^{\epsilon}Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon})
.

We will attack the innermost sum in (
6.3
) with Proposition
6.2
, which shows that the main term will come from terms
n
and
b
such that
nb
is a square. If
nb
is not a square then applying Proposition
6.2
produces an error term of size

S^{\epsilon}\sum_{n\leq Z}\sum_{b\geq 1}\mu^{2}(b)|R(b)|\frac{\psi(n)}{n}Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}(nb)^{\frac{1}{4}+\epsilon}.

By Lemma
6.9
(e), we have that
|R(b)|\ll b^{-3/2}
. Hence the
b
sum converges and the total error is
O_{\mathbb{S},\epsilon}(S^{\epsilon}Z^{\frac{1}{4}+\epsilon}Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon})
.

We turn our attention to the contribution of
nb=u^{2}
for an integer
u\geq 1
. For the inner sum, we get

\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}}{{(\Delta_{K},u)=1}}}g(\Delta_{K})=\delta_{\Xi}Y\prod_{p\in\mathbb{S}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\mid u}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\nmid u}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)+O_{\mathbb{S},\epsilon}\left(u^{\epsilon}Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right)

This error term is of size
O_{\mathbb{S},\epsilon}(Z^{\epsilon}Y^{1/2+\epsilon})
when summed over
u
.
The main term becomes

\delta_{\Xi}Y\left[\prod_{p\in\mathbb{S}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}F(p)\right]=\widetilde{\delta}_{\Xi}Y\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}F(p)

multiplied by the sum over
u
, which is

\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{u\geq 1}\left[\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\mid u}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\prod_{p\nmid u}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle nb=u^{2}}}{{n\leq Z}}}\left(\mu^{2}(b)R(b)\frac{\psi(n)}{n}\right)\right].

We extend the summation over
n
to all integers so that

\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{u\geq 1}\left[\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\mid u}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\nmid u}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\geq 1,b\geq 1}}{{nb=u^{2}}}}\left(\mu^{2}(b)R(b)\frac{\psi(n)}{n}\right)\right]

at the expense of an error term of size

Y\sum_{u\geq 1}f(u)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}nb=u^{2}\\
b\geq 1,n>Z\end{subarray}}\mu^{2}(b)R(b)\frac{\psi(n)}{n}\ll Y\sum_{u\geq Z}\frac{f(u)}{u^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}nb=u^{2}\\
b\geq 1,n>Z\end{subarray}}\psi(n)\ll YZ^{-1+\epsilon},

where we have used
|R(b)|\ll 1/b
,
f(u)\ll 1
and
\psi(n)\ll n^{\epsilon}
. Note that

P:=\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)

converges, so we can write

\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\mid u}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\nmid u}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)=P\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\mid u}\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)^{-1}

and get the main term

P\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{u\geq 1}\left[\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\mid u}\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)^{-1}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\geq 1,b\geq 1}}{{nb=u^{2}}}}\left(\mu^{2}(b)R(b)\frac{\psi(n)}{n}\right)\right].

Since the
u
sum converges, we may write it instead as an Euler product, where the local factor at
p\not\in\mathbb{S}
is given by

1+\sum_{r\geq 1}\left(\prod_{p\mid p^{r}}\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)^{-1}\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\geq 1,b\geq 1}}{{nb=p^{2r}}}}\left(\mu^{2}(b)R(b)\frac{\psi(n)}{n}\right)\right).

On expanding the inner sum in the product, only the terms with
b=1
and
b=p
are non-zero,

\sum_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b\geq 1,n\geq 1}}{{nb=p^{2r}}}}\mu^{2}(b)R(b)\frac{\psi(n)}{n}=\frac{\psi(p^{2r})}{p^{2r}}+\frac{\psi(p^{2r-1})}{p^{2r-1}}R(p)

and we multiply the infinite products
P
and
\prod^{S}_{p}F(p)
back in

\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}\left(F(p)\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)+\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\sum_{r\geq 1}\left(\frac{\psi(p^{2r})}{p^{2r}}F(p)+\frac{\psi(p^{2r-1})}{p^{2r-1}}G(p)\right)\right).

Using the power series

\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{\psi(p^{2r})}{p^{2r}}=\frac{\lambda_{p}+\bar{\lambda}_{p}}{2}-1\quad\text{ and }\quad\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{\psi(p^{2r-1})}{p^{2r-1}}=\frac{\lambda_{p}-\bar{\lambda}_{p}}{2}

we find

\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left[F(p)\left(1+\frac{g(p)}{p}\right)+\left(\frac{\lambda_{p}+\bar{\lambda}_{p}}{2}-1\right)F(p)+\left(\frac{\lambda_{p}-\bar{\lambda}_{p}}{2}\right)G(p)\right].

Expanding and using
F(p)=\frac{f_{p}^{2}+f_{p^{2}}}{2}
,
G(p)=\frac{f_{p}^{2}-f_{p^{2}}}{2}
and
g(p)F(p)=f_{p}
gives

\displaystyle\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}\limits_{p}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left[F(p)+\frac{g(p)F(p)}{p}+\frac{\lambda_{p}f_{p}^{2}+\bar{\lambda}_{p}f_{p^{2}}}{2}-F(p)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}\limits_{p}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\left(\frac{f_{p}}{p}+\frac{\lambda_{p}f_{p}^{2}+\bar{\lambda}_{p}f_{p^{2}}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}\limits_{p}\left[\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\lambda_{p}^{-1}\right]\left(\frac{w_{p}}{p}+\frac{w_{p}^{2}+w_{p^{2}}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}\limits_{p}\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{p}\widetilde{w}_{p}.

Hence, we arrive at

\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})=\left[\widetilde{\delta}_{\Xi}\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{p}\widetilde{w}_{p}\right]Y+O_{\mathbb{S},\epsilon}\left(Z^{\frac{1}{4}+\epsilon}Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}+Z^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}Y^{1+\epsilon}\right).

This error term is minimal when
Z=Y^{\frac{2}{3}}
which results in a total error term of size
Y^{\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon}
.

We can now work in the factor
\kappa_{\Xi}=\lim_{s\to 1}\left[(s-1)^{\rho(X)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})\right]w_{\Xi}(X)
which is constant on
K\in{\mathcal{F}}^{\circ}_{\Xi}
. Finally, we will reintroduce the factor
|\Delta_{K}|^{-\frac{\dim X}{2}}
which was left by the roadside at the beginning of this journey. aA application of partial summation yields

\displaystyle\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}\tau(X_{K})=\kappa_{\Xi}\sum_{K\in\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}(Y)}\frac{\tau_{\mathbb{S}}(X_{K})}{|\Delta_{K}|^{\frac{\dim X}{2}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\kappa_{\Xi}\left(\left[\left(\widetilde{\delta}_{\Xi}\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}\limits_{p}\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{p}\widetilde{w}_{p}\right)Y+O_{\mathbb{S},\epsilon}(Y^{\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon})\right]\times\frac{1}{Y^{\frac{\dim X}{2}}}+\int_{1}^{Y}\frac{\left(\widetilde{\delta}_{\Xi}\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}\limits_{p}\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{p}\widetilde{w}_{p}\right)t+O_{\mathbb{S},\epsilon}(t^{\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon})}{t^{\frac{\dim X}{2}+1}}\mathrm{d}t\right).

The result depends on whether
\dim X=2
or whether it is greater.

(a)

In the case that
\dim X>2
each of the terms is
O(1)
, and the sum converges.

(b)

If
X
is a surface, the first term in the integral dominates and we find

\left(\lim_{s\to 1}\left[(s-1)^{\rho(X)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(s,\Pic{\overline{X}})\right]\widetilde{\delta}_{\Xi}w_{\Xi}(X)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p}\widetilde{\lambda}^{-1}_{p}\widetilde{w}_{p}\right)\log Y+O_{\mathbb{S},\epsilon}(1).\qed

6.5.
Moments of twisted
L
-functions

This section is dedicated to establishing a corollary to our on-average
L
-function truncation result, Proposition
6.3
.
Our main result is to give an asymptotic formula for all the moments of the value at
s=1
of the quadratic twists (with fixed splitting data) of an Artin
L
-function.
One way to view the result is that the values at
s=1
of Artin
L
-functions are constant on average in quadratic twist families, an idea which we will use to estimate certain error terms in
\lx@sectionsign
9.5
.

Corollary 6.12
.

Let
\rho
be a Galois representation which factors through a finite extension
L/{\mathbb{Q}}
, let
\mathbb{S}
a finite set of places and let
\Xi=(\xi_{v})_{v}\in\prod_{v\in\mathbb{S}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times}/{\mathbb{Q}}_{v}^{\times,2}
. Define
\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}^{\circ}(Y)
to be the discriminants of fields in
\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}(Y)
which are linearly disjoint with
L
. Then for any
Y\geq 1
and
t\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
, we have

\sum_{\Delta\in\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}(Y)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(1,\rho\otimes\chi_{\Delta})^{t}=\delta_{\Xi}C_{\rho,t,\mathbb{S}}Y+O_{\mathbb{S},\epsilon,t,\rho}\left(Y^{\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon}\right),

for
C_{\rho,t,S}=\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\prod_{p\in\mathbb{S}}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)^{-1}\prod\nolimits^{S}_{p}\left(1+\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)^{-1}\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\psi^{\ast t}(p^{2k})}{p^{2k}}\right)
where
\psi^{\ast t}
is the
t
-fold convolution product of
\psi
with itself.

As far as the authors are aware the only existing results concerning twisted moments at
s=1
are for the trivial representation. We hope that Corollary
6.12
will find some new application, for instance perhaps for Malle’s conjecture in towers or in the study of the distribution of relative class numbers.

Proof.

We will first prove it for
t=1
. Throughout we will allow all implicit constants in error terms to depend on
\rho
,
t
and
\mathbb{S}
. By Proposition
6.3
with
c_{\Delta}
the characteristic function for
\mathcal{F}^{\circ}_{\Xi}
we obtain

\sum_{\Delta\in\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}^{\circ}(Y)}L_{{\mathbb{Q}},\mathbb{S}}(1,\rho\otimes\chi_{\Delta})=\sum_{\Delta\in\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}^{\circ}(Y)}\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{n\leq Z}\frac{\chi_{\Delta}(n)\psi(n)}{n}+O_{\epsilon}\left(Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}Z^{\epsilon}+Y^{1+\epsilon}Z^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right).

(6.4)

Exchanging summation in the main term, and observing that we are removing at most finitely many quadratic fields (c.f. Lemma
4.3
), we have

\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{n\leq Z}\frac{\psi(n)}{n}\sum_{\Delta\in\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}^{\circ}(Y)}\chi_{\Delta}(n)=\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{n\leq Z}\frac{\psi(n)}{n}\sum_{\Delta\in\mathcal{F}_{\Xi}(Y)}\chi_{\Delta}(n)+O_{\epsilon}(Z^{\epsilon}).

Here we used
\psi(n)\ll n^{\epsilon}
from Lemma
6.6
, which we will apply without mention in the remainder of the argument.

Applying Proposition
6.2
with
g=1
, the remaining double sum is equal to

\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq Z\\
n=\square\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi(n)}{n}\delta_{\Xi}Y\prod_{p\in\mathbb{S}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\mid n}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\prod}_{p\nmid n}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)

+O(n^{\epsilon}Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon})+O_{\mathbb{S}}(Z^{\epsilon})+O\left(\sqrt{Y}\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq Z\\
n\neq\square\end{subarray}}\frac{\psi(n)}{n}n^{1/4}\sqrt{\log(4n)}\right).

The final error term is easily seen to be of size
O\left(Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}Z^{\frac{1}{4}+\epsilon}\right).

We extend the sum over
n
into an infinite sum at the expense of an error term
O_{\mathbb{S}}(YZ^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon})
since it converges absolutely, and obtain a main term of

Y\left[\delta_{\Xi}\frac{6}{\pi^{2}}\prod_{p\in\mathbb{S}}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)^{-1}\right]\sideset{}{{}^{S}}{\sum}_{n=\square}\frac{\psi(n)}{n}\prod_{p\mid n}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)^{-1}.

We can write the sum over
n
as the required Euler product.
The error term is minimised upon taking
Z=Y^{\frac{2}{3}}
.

In the case of general
t
we note that
L(1,\rho\otimes\chi_{\Delta})^{t}=L(1,(\rho\otimes\chi_{\Delta})^{\oplus t})
and the coefficients of that representation are
\psi^{\ast t}
.
∎

To demonstrate the power of this result, we improve the error term for the moments of
L
-functions associated to quadratic fields.

Corollary 6.13

(Moments of
L(1,\chi)
)
.

For
Y\geq 1
and
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we have

\sum_{\Delta\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\pm}(Y)}L(1,\chi_{\Delta})^{t}=Y\frac{3}{\pi^{2}}\prod_{p}\left(1+\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)^{-1}\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\tau_{t}(p^{2k})}{p^{2k}}\right)+O_{t,\epsilon}(Y^{\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon}),

where
{\mathcal{F}}_{+}
is the set of positive, and
{\mathcal{F}}_{-}
the set of negative fundamental discriminants.

Proof.

This is simply the special case of the previous result where
\rho
is the trivial representation,
\mathbb{S}=\{\infty\}
and
\xi_{\infty}=\pm 1
, since the corresponding local factor
\delta_{\infty}
equals
\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

While the moments of
L\left(\frac{1}{2},\chi_{d}\right)
are mysteries central to prime number theory, the moments of the values at
s=1
have been more successfully studied. Stepanov
[
Ste59
]
was the first in 1959 to compute the asymptotic formula for all
k
, followed by Barban
[
Bar62
]
in 1962. They established the same asymptotic formula as above except with an error term of size
O_{\epsilon}\left(Ye^{-c(\log Y)^{\frac{1}{2}-\epsilon}}\right)
. Wolke
[
Wol72
]
reduced the error term to
O_{\epsilon}\left(Y^{\frac{3}{4}+\epsilon}\right)
. Our result hence bridges the gap between the best previously known error term for this problem and the conjectured truth of
O\left(Y^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right)
. One can immediately deduce from this, and the class number formula, Corollary
1.9
advertised in the introduction. Namely, if
h(-d)
denotes the class number of the quadratic field
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-d})
then

\sum_{d\in{\mathcal{F}}_{-}(Y)}\mu^{2}(d)h(-d)^{t}=\widetilde{c}_{t}Y^{\frac{t}{2}+1}+O_{\epsilon}\left(Y^{\frac{t}{2}+\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon}\right).

If one so desired Proposition
6.3
is flexible enough to allow one to find the moments of the class number among other families of quadratic fields, say thinner families or discriminants in arithmetic progressions and so forth.

Similarly, one may deduce an analogous result for real quadratic fields although in this case, the class number is inseparable from the regulator and one concludes that

\sum_{d\in\mathcal{F}_{+}(Y)}h(d)^{t}R(d)^{t}=\widetilde{c}_{t}Y^{\frac{t}{2}+1}+O_{t,\epsilon}\left(Y^{\frac{t}{2}+\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon}\right),

where
R(d)
the regulator of the associated real quadratic field.
In the case
t=1
, the main term for the related sum over all integers
d
was first written down by Gauss in the Disquisitiones Arithmeticae. An asymptotic was established by Siegel
[
Sie44
]
and a secondary term was then established by Shintani
[
Shi75
]
. Restricting this mean value to fundamental discriminants was first accomplished by Goldfeld–Hoffstein
[
DGH03
]
and was then extended to arbitrary base fields by Datskovsky
[
Dat93
]
and Taniguchi
[
Tan07
]
. Taniguchi
[
Tan08
]
proved an asymptotic with error term
o(Y^{2})
for the case
t=2
. This appears to be the only result for higher moments in this particular problem, however we note that Raulf
[
Rau16
]
established an asymptotic for all
t
with an error term saving
Y^{\epsilon}
when ordering the sum by the size of the regulator. This builds upon work of Sarnak
[
Sar82
]
who performed a similar sum for
t=1
.

7.
Cutoffs

When counting points of bounded height on
\Sym^{2}V
, many of them arise as a quadratic point on
V
and its conjugate. By the Northcott property, there are only finitely many such points and thus only finitely many quadratic fields will contribute points of bounded height. In this section, we introduce terminology to measure how large the quadratic field can be and still contain a point of small height.

7.1.
Cutoffs

For this section we fix a projective variety
V
with an adelically metrised line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
over a number field
F
, with an induced absolute adelic height
H\colon V(\bar{F})\to{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
. Any constants in this section are allowed to depend on this data.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
K
be a finite extension of the number field
F
.

(i)

For a subset
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\Sym^{2}V(F)
define a family of sets
\mathcal{U}_{K}=\{P\in V(K)\colon[P,\bar{P}]\in\mathcal{U}\}\setminus V(F)
, where
K
ranges over all quadratic extensions of
F
.

(ii)

We say that
\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
is a
quadratic cutoff
, or simply a
cutoff
, for
{\mathcal{U}}
with respect to
{\mathcal{L}}
if there exists some constant
C_{\mathcal{U}}>0
such that, for any quadratic extension
K/F
and any
P\in{\mathcal{U}}_{K}
, we have

|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}\Delta_{K/F}|\leq C_{\mathcal{U}}H_{{\mathcal{L}}}(P)^{2\gamma}.

(7.1)

(iii)

We say
\gamma
is a cutoff for
V
if it is a cutoff for
\mathcal{U}=\Sym^{2}V(F)
.

A priori, cutoffs depend on the adelic metrisation on
{\mathcal{L}}
. We now show that it actually depends only on the underlying line bundle.

Proposition 7.2
.

Any cutoff
\gamma
for a subset
{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\Sym^{2}V(F)
with respect to an adelic height depends only on the underlying line bundle. That is, if
H
and
H^{\prime}
are two heights coming from adelic metrics on the same line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
, then
\gamma
is a cutoff with respect to the
H
if and only if it is so with respect to the
H^{\prime}
.

Proof.

If
\gamma
is a cutoff with respect to
H
, then for any quadratic extension
K/F
and any
P\in{\mathcal{U}}_{K}
we have

|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}\Delta_{K/F}|\leq CH(P)^{2\gamma}.

It is a basic property of the height machine that for two heights coming from different adelic metrisations on the same line bundle there are positive constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
, such that
c_{1}H^{\prime}\leq H\leq c_{2}H^{\prime}
. By letting
C^{\prime}:=c_{2}^{2\gamma}C
, we obtain

|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}\Delta_{K/F}|\leq CH(P)^{2\gamma}\leq Cc_{2}^{2\gamma}H^{\prime}(P)^{2\gamma}=C^{\prime}H^{\prime}(P)^{2\gamma},

meaning that
\gamma
is also a cutoff with respect to
H^{\prime}
, as required.
∎

Our next fundamental fact is that cutoffs are well behaved under decreasing subsets
{\mathcal{U}}
and increasing
\gamma
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\Sym^{2}V(F)
be a subset.

(a)

A cutoff
\gamma
for
{\mathcal{U}}
is also a cutoff for any
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
.

(b)

Let
K/F
be a quadratic extension. A
\gamma\geq 0
is a cutoff for
{\mathcal{U}}
if and only if it is a cutoff for

{\mathcal{U}}\setminus\{[P,\bar{P}]\colon P\in V(K)\}.

(c)

If
\gamma
is a cutoff for
{\mathcal{U}}
with respect to
{\mathcal{L}}
, then so is any real number
\gamma^{\prime}\geq\gamma
.

Proof.

(a)

Clearly if (
7.1
) is satisfied for all points
P\in{\mathcal{U}}
then it is for all points in
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
.

(b)

By (a), if
\gamma
is a cutoff for
{\mathcal{U}}
then it is a cutoff for

{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{U}}\setminus\{[P,\bar{P}]\colon P\in V(K)\}.

Now suppose that
\gamma
is a cutoff for
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
with constant
C^{\prime}
. Let
h
be the minimal height among points in
X(K)
, and define
C=\max\{C^{\prime},|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}\Delta_{K/F}|/h^{2\gamma}\}
. Then (
7.1
) is satisfied with constant
C
for
P\in{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
since

|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}\Delta_{F(P)/F}|\leq C^{\prime}H(P)^{2\gamma}\leq CH(P)^{2\gamma}

and for
P\in V(K)
we have

|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}\Delta_{K/F}|\leq Ch^{2\gamma}\leq CH(P)^{2\gamma}.

(c)

By the Northcott Property there is a minimal height
h>0
among the points in
V(\bar{F})
of degree
2
. Define
C^{\prime}=Ch^{2(\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma)}>0
. Now let
K/F
be a quadratic extension. Then for
P\in{\mathcal{U}}_{K}
we have

|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}\Delta_{K/F}|\leq CH(P)^{2\gamma}=C^{\prime}h^{2(\gamma^{\prime}-\gamma^{\prime})}H(P)^{2\gamma}\leq C^{\prime}H(P)^{2\gamma^{\prime}}.

It follows that
\gamma^{\prime}
satisfies the cutoff condition with the constant
C^{\prime}
, as required. ∎

The situation where
\gamma=0
is quite special, and demands some attention.

Lemma 7.4
.

The value
\gamma=0
is a cutoff for
{\mathcal{U}}
if and only if we have
{\mathcal{U}}_{K}=\emptyset
for all but finitely many
K
.

Proof.

If
\gamma=0
is a cutoff we have found that for all quadratic
K/F
and
P\in{\mathcal{U}}_{K}
we have
|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}\Delta_{K/F}|\leq C
. Hence, either
K/F
has a small discriminant or
{\mathcal{U}}_{K}=\emptyset
. The reverse implication is immediate.
∎

Corollary 7.5
.

If
\gamma=0
is a cutoff for a cothin
{\mathcal{U}}
then
\Sym^{2}V
fails the Hilbert property, that is,
\Sym^{2}V({\mathbb{Q}})
is a thin set.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.4
we see that
{\mathcal{U}}_{K}
is nonempty for finitely many quadratic fields
K
. Hence
{\mathcal{U}}
is a thin set as it is contained in the union of the images of the finite morphisms

\eta_{K}\colon\Res_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}V_{K}\to\Sym^{2}V

from Proposition
3.12
. Since
{\mathcal{U}}
is thin and
\Sym^{2}V({\mathbb{Q}})\setminus{\mathcal{U}}
is thin we conclude that
\Sym^{2}V
fails the Hilbert property.
∎

Remark 7.6
.

As any rationally connected variety has finite Brauer group modulo constant elements
[
CTS21
, p. 347]
, the conjecture by Colliot-Thélène mentioned in Remark
5.6
implies that rationally connected varieties satisfy weak weak approximation
[
CTS21
, Defn. 13.2.4]
whenever they have rational points. Therefore, by a result of Ekedahl
[
Ser08
, Thm. 3.5.7]
their sets of rational points are not thin. In particular, conjecturally
\Hilb^{2}V({\mathbb{Q}})
is not thin if it is not empty, which implies that
\Sym^{2}V({\mathbb{Q}})
is also not thin. Hence, by Corollary
7.5
, it is conjecturally impossible for 0 to be a cutoff for rationally connected varieties
V
for which
\Hilb^{2}V({\mathbb{Q}})\neq\emptyset
.

To determine a cutoff in some cases, the following result of Silverman is useful.

Theorem 7.7

(
[
Sil84
, Lem. 2]
)
.

Let
F
be a number field and
\delta_{F}
the number of archimedean places of
F
. For a point
P\in{\mathbb{P}}^{n}(\overline{F})
we define
H_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}(P)
as the naïve absolute
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-height on
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}(\overline{F})
,
F(P)
the minimal field of definition for
P
, and
m=[F(P)\colon F]
. Then we have

|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{F(P)/F})|\leq m^{m\delta_{F}}H_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}(P)^{2m(m-1)[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}.

Example 7.8
.

Let
F
be a number field. The value
\frac{2[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}{k}
is a cutoff for
{\mathbb{P}}_{F}^{n}
with respect to
{\mathcal{O}}(k)
. Hence a cutoff for the anticanonical height is given by
\frac{2[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}{n+1}
.

In Corollary
7.24
we show that these are actually the smallest possible cutoffs for
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{F}
. For now, let us use the result to show that all varieties under consideration at least have a cutoff.

Proposition 7.9
.

Let
V/F
be a variety with a adelic height function
H
on an ample line bundle, then there is a
\gamma\geq 0
which is a cutoff for
V
.

Proof.

Embed
V
in some projective space
{\mathbb{P}}^{N}_{F}
via
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes k}
for some
k
. Then
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
on
{\mathbb{P}}_{F}^{N}
restricts to
{\mathcal{L}}^{k}
on
V
. Since
[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]
is a cutoff for
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
, we see that
k[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]
is a cutoff for
{\mathcal{L}}
.
∎

We will see that in many examples the cutoff produced in this proof is far from optimal. For example, for a smooth quadric surface
X\subseteq{\mathbb{P}}^{3}_{\mathbb{Q}}
we will see that
\frac{1}{2}
is an anticanonical cutoff, but this result only shows that the
{\mathcal{O}}(2)
-cutoff
1
of
{\mathbb{P}}^{3}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
is also a cutoff for
X
.

7.2.
Cutoffs of product varieties

Suppose that
V^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime\prime}
are two projective varieties over
F
each equipped with an adelic metric on their anticanonical line bundles. Consider
V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}
with the induced product adelic metric metric on its anticanonical line bundle. Given
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}\subseteq\Sym^{2}V(F)
and
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\Sym^{2}V^{\prime\prime}(F)
subsets with respective cutoffs
\gamma^{\prime}\geq 0
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
, we can consider the induced subset of
\Sym^{2}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})
. In particular, we write
\mathcal{U}^{\text{prod}}
for the preimage of
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}
under the natural morphism
\Sym^{2}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})\to\Sym^{2}V^{\prime}\times\Sym^{2}V^{\prime\prime}
. One can compare
\mathcal{U}^{\text{prod}}
with
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}
and, in particular, compute a cutoff for such subsets.

Proposition 7.10
.

(a)

If
\,{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}=\Sym^{2}V^{\prime}({\mathbb{Q}})
and
\,{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}=\Sym^{2}V^{\prime\prime}(F)
then
\,{\mathcal{U}}^{\text{prod}}=\Sym^{2}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})(F)
.

(b)

We have
\gamma=\max\{\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime}\}
is a cutoff for
{\mathcal{U}}^{\text{prod}}
.

Proof.

Let
H^{\prime},H^{\prime\prime}
, and
H=H^{\prime}H^{\prime\prime}
be the associated height functions on
V^{\prime}
,
V^{\prime\prime}
, and
V=V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}
respectively. By the definition of
\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
there are constants
C^{\prime}>0
and
C^{\prime\prime}>0
such that for every quadratic
K/F
and
P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{K}
and
P^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}_{K}
we have

|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{K/F})|\leq C^{\prime}H^{\prime}(P^{\prime})^{2\gamma^{\prime}}\quad\text{ and }\quad|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{K/F})|\leq C^{\prime\prime}H^{\prime\prime}(P^{\prime\prime})^{2\gamma^{\prime\prime}}.

(a)

This follows from the definition; the preimage of
\,{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}=\Sym^{2}V^{\prime}(F)\times\Sym^{2}V^{\prime\prime}(F)
under
\Sym^{2}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})\to\Sym^{2}V^{\prime}\times\Sym^{2}V^{\prime\prime}
equals
\Sym^{2}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})(F)
.

(b)

There is a constant
C_{1}>0
such that any (at most) quadratic point on
V^{\prime}
or
V^{\prime\prime}
has height at least
C_{1}
.
Now consider a point
P=(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})\in{\mathcal{U}}^{\text{prod}}_{K}\subseteq(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})(K)\setminus(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})(F)
. Hence at least one of the coordinates must be a pure
K
-point. Assume
P^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}(K)\setminus V^{\prime}(F)
. By construction of
\,{\mathcal{U}}^{\text{prod}}
we have
P^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
. Hence for
\gamma=\max\{\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime}\}
we find

|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|C_{1}^{2\gamma}\leq H^{\prime}(P^{\prime})^{2\gamma}C_{1}^{2\gamma}\leq H^{\prime}(P^{\prime})^{2\gamma}H^{\prime\prime}(P^{\prime\prime})^{2\gamma}=H(P)^{2\gamma},

and similarly if
P^{\prime\prime}
is a pure
K
-point. We conclude that
\gamma
is a cutoff for
\,{\mathcal{U}}^{\text{prod}}
. ∎

It can be shown under mild assumptions (for example if
V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}
has pure
K
-points for infinitely many quadratic extensions
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
) that if
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}
are cothin then
{\mathcal{U}}^{\text{prod}}
is cothin. In the counting examples considered so far (including the one in the next section) this is obvious from the choice of
\mathcal{U}
.

For now we use
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{F}:=\{P\in V(F)\colon\exists P^{\prime}\in V(F)\textrm{ s.t. }[P,P^{\prime}]\in{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\}
for the set of
F
-points of
V
that appear as a part of a point in
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
, and we define
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}_{F}
similarly. For any quadratic field
K
, we have

{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{K}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}_{K}\;\subseteq\;{\mathcal{U}}_{K}^{\text{prod}}\;\subseteq\;({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{K}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}_{K})\bigcup({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{K}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}_{F})\bigcup({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{F}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}_{K}).

As we have seen in this section, if a point is quadratic then its height needs to be large.
If we have a point
(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})\in{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{K}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}_{K}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{prod}
with both components purely quadratic, the height of this point is
H_{V^{\prime}}(P^{\prime})H_{V^{\prime\prime}}(P^{\prime\prime})
, say, and since both heights need to be large, and their product is bounded, neither can be
too
large.
However for a point
(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})\in({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{F}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}_{K})\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{prod}
the height of
P^{\prime}
can be fairly small since it is a rational (not quadratic) point. This means that the height of
P^{\prime\prime}
is not as constrained and can be quite large.
This in turn means that the fields
K
in which this point can live could also possibly be quite large.

In summary, the fields which contribute pairs of pure quadratic points on
V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}
might be smaller than those that can contribute pure quadratic points on either
V^{\prime}
or
V^{\prime\prime}
. We introduce now a definition to deal with this phenomenon.

Definition 7.11
.

A point on
\Sym^{2}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})
defined over
F
is called
pristine
if it is contained in
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{K}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}_{K}
for some quadratic field
K
. That is to say pristine points are those points on
V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}
which are pure quadratic in both coordinates. We denote the set of all such points in
\mathcal{U}^{prod}
by
\mathcal{U}^{pris}
.

Remark 7.12
.

In general, one could say a subset
{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\Sym^{2}V(F)
is
pristine
if there are finitely many dominant fibration
\phi_{i}\colon V\to C_{i}
with
0<\dim C_{i}<\dim V
, such that
{\mathcal{U}}
is contained in the set

\{[P_{1},P_{2}]\in\Sym^{2}V(F)\colon\phi_{i}(P_{1}),\phi_{i}(P_{2})\text{ are pure quadratic points for all }i\}.

This concept behaves well with respect to thin sets; the conjugate points
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
must lie in conjugate fibres, so either the fibre over a
F
-point or over a pure quadratic point. One can show that the points on
\Sym^{2}V(F)
which lie in a fibre over a
F
-point form a thin set of type I.
Clearly, the pristine points on the product
V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}
are precisely the points which do not lie in fibres over
F
-points of either projection.

For example, the Hirzebruch surfaces
\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
\mathbb{F}_{2}
admit a fibration over
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, and the cutoff of the pure quadratic points is strictly larger than the cutoff of the pristine points with respect to this fibration. This is related to the fact that the pure quadratic points which lie over
F
-points on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
dominate the point count on
\Sym^{2}\mathbb{F}_{r}
, and must be included in a thin set for Manin’s conjecture to hold.

As discussed above the set of fields in which the pristine points of bounded height can arise might not be the same as the set of fields in which we can have quadratic points on
V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}
. In particular, the cutoff may be different. We formulate some results around pristine points.

Proposition 7.13
.

(a)

If
\gamma^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}\neq 0
then
\gamma=\frac{\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}}{\gamma^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
is a cutoff for
{\mathcal{U}}^{\text{pris}}
.

(b)

{\mathcal{U}}^{prod}\setminus{\mathcal{U}}^{pris}
is a thin subset of
\Sym^{2}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})(F)
.

Proof.

We keep the same conventions as in the proof of Proposition
7.10
.

(a)

From
{\mathcal{U}}_{K}={\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}_{K}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}_{K}
we find for
P=(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})\in{\mathcal{U}}_{K}
that

\displaystyle|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{K/F})|^{\gamma^{\prime}+\gamma^{\prime\prime}}

\displaystyle\leq\left[C^{\prime}H^{\prime}(P^{\prime})^{2\gamma^{\prime}}\right]^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\left[C^{\prime\prime}H^{\prime\prime}(P^{\prime\prime})^{2\gamma^{\prime\prime}}\right]^{\gamma^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=C^{\prime\gamma^{\prime\prime}}C^{\prime\prime\gamma^{\prime}}\left[H^{\prime}(P^{\prime})H^{\prime\prime}(P^{\prime\prime})\right]^{2\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}}=CH(P)^{2\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}},

where
C=C^{\prime\gamma^{\prime\prime}}C^{\prime\prime\gamma^{\prime}}>0
.

(b)

The product points that are not pristine on
V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}
look like pure
K
-points
(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})\in(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})(K)
where either
P^{\prime}
or
P^{\prime\prime}
is actually a
F
-point. Consider the case
P^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}(K)\setminus V^{\prime}(F)
and
P^{\prime\prime}\in V^{\prime\prime}(F)
. Then the corresponding point
[(P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime}),(\bar{P}^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime})]\in\Sym^{2}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})
lies in the preimage of the diagonal
\Delta^{\prime}\subseteq\Sym^{2}V^{\prime}
under
\Sym^{2}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime})\to\Sym^{2}V^{\prime},[(P_{1},P_{2}),(P_{3},P_{4})]\mapsto[P_{1},P_{3}]
. Hence the product points which are non-pristine lie in a thin subset of type I.
∎

One can show that the product points coming from the subsets
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\setminus\{[P_{1},P_{2}]\ |P_{1},P_{2}\in V^{\prime}(F)\}
and
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}\setminus\{[P_{1},P_{2}]\ |P_{1},P_{2}\in V^{\prime\prime}(F)\}
are precisely the pristine product points coming from
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}
, hence we conclude from the remark following Proposition
7.10
that
{\mathcal{U}}^{\text{pris}}
is also cothin if
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime\prime}
are cothin.

Example 7.14
.

.

(1)

The number
1
is a cutoff for
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}
with respect to the anticanonical line bundle.

(2)

For the pristine points a cutoff is given by
\tfrac{1}{2}
.

The second cutoff is the one implicitly used by Le Rudulier
[
LR14
, p. 93]
, although the normalisation of her height is different in this section. Specifically, she is using a relative
\mathcal{O}(1)
-height and so replacing
B
in her conventions by
B^{1/4}
recovers the cutoff of
\frac{1}{2}
.

7.3.
Permanence of cutoffs under field extensions

For the next examples we use the following result on norms of relative discriminants under base change.

Proposition 7.15
.

Let
F^{\prime}/F
be an extension of number fields. Then for any
K/F
linearly disjoint from
F^{\prime}
we have

|N_{F^{\prime}/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{KF^{\prime}/F^{\prime}})|=|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{K/F})|^{[F^{\prime}\colon F]}.

Proof.

Because
F^{\prime}
and
K
are linearly disjoint we have, by
[
Neu99
, Prop. I.2.11]
, and the remark following it, that

\Delta_{KF^{\prime}/F}=\Delta_{F^{\prime}/F}^{[K\colon F]}\Delta_{K/F}^{[F^{\prime}\colon F]}.

Computing the discriminant of
KF^{\prime}/F
using the intermediate field
F^{\prime}
we have, by
[
Neu99
, Cor. III.2.10]
, that

\Delta_{KF^{\prime}/F}=N_{F^{\prime}/F}(\Delta_{KF^{\prime}/F^{\prime}})\Delta_{F^{\prime}/F}^{[KF^{\prime}\colon F^{\prime}]}=N_{F^{\prime}/F}(\Delta_{KF^{\prime}/F^{\prime}})\Delta_{F^{\prime}/F}^{[K\colon F]}.

We conclude

N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{F^{\prime}/F}^{[K\colon F]})=N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(N_{F^{\prime}/F}(\Delta_{KF^{\prime}/F^{\prime}}))=N_{F^{\prime}/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{KF^{\prime}/F^{\prime}}).\qed

We will now prove a stability result on cutoffs under base change.

Proposition 7.16
.

Consider a variety
V
over a number field
F
, and let
F^{\prime}/F
be a field extension of finite degree. Let
\gamma
be a cutoff for the base change
V_{F^{\prime}}/F^{\prime}
. Then
\frac{\gamma}{[F^{\prime}\colon F]}
is a cutoff for
V/F
.

Proof.

Let
C>0
be such that for every quadratic extension
K^{\prime}/F^{\prime}
and every point
P\in V_{F^{\prime}}(K^{\prime})
we have

|N_{F^{\prime}/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{K^{\prime}/F^{\prime}})|\leq CH(P)^{2\gamma}.

For any quadratic field
K
there is a bijection between the
K
-points on
V
and the
K^{\prime}=KF
-points on
V_{F^{\prime}}
, which respects heights. Hence for any
P\in V(K)
we have

|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{K/F})|=|N_{F^{\prime}/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{K^{\prime}/F^{\prime}})|^{\frac{1}{[F^{\prime}\colon F]}}\leq CH(P)^{2\frac{\gamma}{[F^{\prime}\colon F]}}\qed

We can use this result to provide cutoffs for twists of projective spaces.

Proposition 7.17
.

Let
X/F
be a Severi–Brauer variety of dimension
n
with an adelic anticanonical height. Then
\frac{2[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}{n+1}
is a cutoff for
X
.

Proof.

Let
L/{\mathbb{Q}}
be such that
X(L)\neq\emptyset
. Since
X_{L}\cong{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{L}
we see that
\frac{2[L\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}{n+1}
is a cutoff for
X_{L}
and by Proposition
7.17

\frac{2[L\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}{[L\colon F](n+1)}=\frac{2[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}{n+1}
is a cutoff for
X
.
∎

Remark 7.18
.

The converse to Proposition
7.16
need not hold in general; not all quadratic extensions of
F^{\prime}
are of the form
KF^{\prime}
! Hence a cutoff of
V/F
cannot possibly say anything about all quadratic extensions of
F^{\prime}
. This explains why a cutoff of
V
need not say anything about cutoffs of
V_{F^{\prime}}
.

For example, consider a variety
V/F
for which
\Sym^{2}V(F)=\emptyset
, but
V_{F^{\prime}}/F^{\prime}
does have pure
K^{\prime}
-points for infinitely many quadratic extensions
K^{\prime}/F^{\prime}
, then by Lemma
7.4

0
is a cutoff for
V
, but not for
V_{F^{\prime}}
. This already happens in the situation in Proposition
7.17
; a Severi–Brauer surface split by a cubic extension will have
0
as a cutoff, but
0
is not a cutoff for
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}
as
{\mathbb{Q}}
is Hilbertian. Other examples among del Pezzo surfaces are quartic del Pezzo surface of index
4
, which were proven to exist in
[
CV
]
.

We conclude this subsection by considering another geometric construction which is of critical importance to this paper, the restriction of scalars.

Proposition 7.19
.

Let
V^{\prime}/F^{\prime}
be an anticanonically adelically metrised
F^{\prime}
-variety, where
F^{\prime}/F
is a number field.
If
\gamma\geq 0
is a cutoff for
V^{\prime}
, then
\frac{\gamma}{[F^{\prime}\colon F]^{2}}
is a cutoff for
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}
.

Proof.

All but finitely many quadratic field
K/F
produce a quadratic field
KF^{\prime}/F^{\prime}
. For those
K
the pure
K
-points in
V(K)
correspond to the pure
KF^{\prime}
-points in
\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}
under the natural bijection

\nu_{V^{\prime}}\colon\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}(K)\xrightarrow{\cong}V(KF^{\prime}).

If
P\in V(KF^{\prime})
corresponds to a
Q\in\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}V^{\prime}(K)
we have
H_{\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}\omega}(Q)=H(P)^{[F^{\prime}\colon F]}
by Proposition
3.10
. Then we find for all such
P
and
Q

|N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{K/F})|^{[F^{\prime}\colon F]}=|N_{F^{\prime}/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{KF^{\prime}/F^{\prime}})|\leq C_{V^{\prime}}H(P)^{2\gamma}=C_{V^{\prime}}H_{\Res_{F^{\prime}/F}\omega}(Q)^{\frac{2\gamma}{[F^{\prime}\colon F]}},

where we have used Proposition
7.15
.
∎

Remark 7.20
.

As in Remark
7.18
, the converse of Proposition
7.19
need not hold, since there might be infinitely many quadratic extensions of
F^{\prime}
which are not of the form
KF^{\prime}/F^{\prime}
for
K/F
quadratic.
When we have good bounds for an individual quadratic extension, usually Silverman’s result for products of projective space, we do get an equivalence.

7.4.
Optimal cutoffs and lower bounds

In Proposition
7.9
we saw how to use the Silverman bound to find examples of cutoffs. Often this does not produce the best possible cutoff, but it does provide an upper bound for the smallest possible cutoff. In this section we will concern ourselves with establishing lower bounds for cutoffs, which turns out to be much harder.

Definition 7.21
.

We say
\gamma\geq 0
is the
optimal cutoff
if it is a cutoff and if there is no smaller cutoff.

Remark 7.22
.

In order to prove the Manin–Peyre conjecture for a surface using the strategy outlined in this paper, it is necessary to compute precisely the optimal cutoff as, thanks to Theorem
6.10
, this value will appear in the leading constant. However, one can prove lower bounds for the counting function
N_{\mathbb{Q}}({\mathcal{U}},B)
of the correct order of magnitude without needing to compute the cutoff. Indeed, if one simply sums over all fields of discriminant bounded by
B^{\epsilon}
for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, one obtains the correct order of magnitude.

Clearly if the infimum of all cutoffs is a cutoff too, it must be optimal. However, proving that a cutoff is optimal, or even that the optimal cutoff must exist is non-trivial in general.
Here is however a positive result.

Proposition 7.23
.

Consider
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}({\mathbb{Q}})
, then
2
is the optimal
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-cutoff.

Proof.

We will find a constant
C^{\prime}
such that there are infinitely quadratic fields
K_{i}/{\mathbb{Q}}
, and points
P_{i}\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}(K_{i})
such that
C^{\prime}H(P_{i})^{4}\leq|\Delta_{K_{i}}|
for each
i
. Now if
\gamma\geq 0
is a cutoff for
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
with constant
C>0
then we have for the point
P_{i}

C^{\prime}H(P_{i})^{4}\leq|\Delta_{K_{i}}|\leq CH(P_{i})^{2\gamma}.

Since there are finitely many quadratic points of bounded height, we conclude that from
i\gg 1
we get
\gamma\geq 2
.

We will use the approach in
[
Rup98
]
to produce infinitely many points of small height, though this paper considers them with a different height.

Let
m>0
be an integer such that
\Delta_{m}=1-4m^{2}
is squarefree, and
K_{m}/{\mathbb{Q}}
be the associated quadratic field. We claim that the points
P_{m}=[2m\colon-1+\sqrt{\Delta_{m}}]=\left[1\colon\frac{-1+\sqrt{\Delta_{m}}}{2m}\right]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}(K_{m})
satisfy the conditions required above. Indeed, we see that
\frac{-1+\sqrt{\Delta_{m}}}{2m}
has minimal polynomial
P(x)=mx^{2}+x+m=m(x-\alpha_{1})(x-\alpha_{2})
in
\mathbb{Z}[x]
and hence
H(P_{m})=M(P)^{\frac{1}{2}}
where
M(P)
denotes the Mahler measure (see
[
Smy08
]
). The Mahler measure also satisfies
M(P)=m\prod_{i}\max\{1,|\alpha_{i}|\}=m
, as
|\alpha_{1}|=1=|\alpha_{2}|
. Hence we get
M(P)=m
, and now
H(P_{m})^{4}=m^{2}\leq 4m^{2}-1=|\Delta_{K_{m}}|
follows for infinitely many points in different quadratic fields since there are infinitely many squarefree values of
1-4m^{2}
.
∎

Corollary 7.24
.

For every
k,n\geq 1
and number field
F/{\mathbb{Q}}
, the optimal
{\mathcal{O}}(k)
-cutoff of
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{F}
exists and is equal to
\tfrac{2[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}{k}
.

Proof.

Since cutoffs behave well with respect to powers of line bundles it is enough to prove the statement for
k=1
. Proposition
7.16
shows that the statement holds for all number fields
F
, if it does so for
F={\mathbb{Q}}
.

Since
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{\mathbb{Q}}
contains a line
L={\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
with
\left.{\mathcal{O}}(1)\right|_{L}={\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}(1)
we see from
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}({\mathbb{Q}})\subseteq{\mathbb{P}}^{n}({\mathbb{Q}})
that any cutoff for
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{\mathbb{Q}}
must be cutoff for
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. By the previous result,
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
does not have a smaller cutoff than
1
, hence the same holds for
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Since we saw that
1
is a
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-cutoff for
{\mathbb{P}}^{n}_{\mathbb{Q}}
it is in fact optimal.
∎

The same proof shows that if
\gamma
is the optimal cutoff for
V/F
then
\gamma[F^{\prime}\colon F]
is a lower bound for all cutoffs for
V_{F^{\prime}}
.

In general, proving that optimal cutoffs exist or determining their value appears hard. We offer the following two approaches for producing lower bounds for cutoffs. The first of these approaches was used in the proof of Corollary
7.24
.

Example 7.25
.

Let a variety
V/{\mathbb{Q}}
contain a rational curve
C
with
C({\mathbb{Q}})\neq\emptyset
, and an ample line bundle
{\mathcal{L}}
. Define
d=C\cdot{\mathcal{L}}
. Then any cutoff for
V
with respect to
{\mathcal{L}}
is at least
\tfrac{2}{d}
, since a cutoff for
\Sym^{2}V({\mathbb{Q}})
determines one for the subset
\Sym^{2}C({\mathbb{Q}})
.

More generally, for subsets
{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\Sym^{2}V({\mathbb{Q}})
which contain all (or even just many) pure quadratic points of
C
, we can use the same trick. For example, the diagonal
\Delta
on
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}
shows that
\frac{2}{\deg\left.{\mathcal{O}}(2,2)\right|_{\Delta}}=\frac{2}{4}=\frac{1}{2}
is a lower bound for both the product and pristine points on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. For the latter this is sharp, for the former the actual optimal cutoff is
1
, as seen by the many horizontal and vertical rational curves, which all have anticanonical degree
2
.

In some cases proving that a cutoff
\gamma
for
X
is actually the optimal cutoff is a byproduct of establishing Manin’s conjecture for
\Sym^{2}X
.

Example 7.26
.

Consider
X={\mathbb{P}}^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}
then Manin’s conjecture was proven by Schmidt
[
Sch95
]
by establishing

N_{K}^{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{2},B^{\frac{1}{2}})=c_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2},K}B+O(c^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2},K}B^{\frac{5}{6}}),

and an
{\mathcal{O}}(3)
-cutoff for
X={\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}
is given by
\gamma=\tfrac{2}{3}
. Hence for
{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{P}}^{2}({\mathbb{Q}})
the cothin set of conjugate pairs of pure quadratic points on
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}
he obtains

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{Q}}({\mathcal{U}},B)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|\Delta|\leq B^{\gamma}}N_{K}^{*}({\mathbb{P}}^{2},B^{\frac{1}{2}})=\sum_{|\Delta|\leq B^{\gamma}}\left[c_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2},K}B+O(c^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2},K}B^{\frac{5}{6}})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|\Delta|\leq B^{\gamma}}\left[\alpha({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{2})\tau({\mathbb{P}}_{K}^{2})B+O(c^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2},K}B^{\frac{5}{6}})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\alpha({\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2})\tau(\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2})B\log(B^{\gamma})+O\left(\sum_{|\Delta|\leq B^{\gamma}}c^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2},K}B^{\frac{5}{6}}\right).

Schmidt proved
[
Sch95
, §8]
that

\sum_{|\Delta|\leq Y}c^{*}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{2},K}\ll Y^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\log^{+}Y\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and hence

\displaystyle N_{\mathbb{Q}}({\mathcal{U}},B)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\gamma\alpha({\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2})\tau(\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2})B\log B+O\left(B^{\frac{1}{4}\gamma}\left(\log^{+}B\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}B^{\frac{5}{6}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\gamma\alpha({\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2})\tau(\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2})B\log B+O\left(B^{\frac{5}{6}+\frac{\gamma}{4}}\left(\log B\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

Using the cutoff
\gamma=\frac{2}{3}
he obtains the desired result.
The main point of this example is that if there would be a cutoff
\gamma<\frac{2}{3}
, then the fields with discriminant between
B^{\gamma}
and
B^{\frac{2}{3}}
should contain no points and thus summing beyond
B^{\gamma}
should not change the asymptotic.
This is contradicted by the formula above; changing
\gamma
does alter the constant of the main term. Note that there is no such logical inconsistency in choosing
\gamma>\frac{2}{3}
since then the error term will dominate the main term.
Hence
\frac{2}{3}
is the optimal anticanonical cutoff for
{\mathbb{P}}^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

We have thus seen the relevance of the optimal cutoff in the study of Manin’s conjecture of
\Sym^{2}X
, and the result above shows that for the optimal anticanonical cutoff
\gamma=\frac{2}{3}
for
X=\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}
we have the relation

\frac{1}{2}\gamma\alpha({\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2})=\alpha(\Hilb^{2}{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}).

One might naïvely expect something similar for all surfaces
X
.
However, the relation between the cutoff and the
\alpha
-constants of the surface and it’s Hilbert scheme can be much more complicated in the presence of fibrations.

Remark 7.27
.

We can even use the trick in Example
7.26
to prove that the
{\mathcal{O}}(2)
-cutoff
1
for
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is the optimal cutoff, though Manin’s conjecture for
\Sym^{2}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
has not been established by summing the individual terms
N^{\prime}_{K}({\mathbb{P}}^{1},B)
, see
[
Sch95
, §9]
.
Le Rudulier
[
LR14
]
implicitly used the induced cutoff
\frac{1}{2}
for the pristine point on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
to successfully prove Manin’s conjecture for
\Sym^{2}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}})
. This proves that
\frac{1}{2}
is the optimal cutoff for the pristine points on the product, and in turn
1
must be the optimal anticanonical cutoff for
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

However, for this surface the relation between the
\alpha
constants of
X
and
\Hilb^{2}X
is more mysterious due to the presence of fibrations. Implicitly, Le Rudulier arrives at the following equation for
X={\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}

\alpha\left(\Hilb^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1})\right)=\frac{1}{2}\alpha({\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1})\gamma\left(1-\frac{1}{\rho({\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1})}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}+\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}\right)\gamma\right),

where
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
is the optimal cutoff for pristine points on
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}
,
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}=1
are the optimal cutoffs for the factors.

We have been able to find a similar expression for
\alpha(\Hilb^{2}X)
for the Hirzebruch surfaces
\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, but the relation for a general del Pezzo surface seems to be much more complicated.

8.
Lattices over general rings of integers

In this section, we collect basic properties of lattices over the ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
of a number field
F
following
[
Ser79
, Ch. III]
. In particular, we develop a theory of the geometry of numbers over number fields, estimating the number of lattice points
\Lambda\cap{\mathcal{S}}
of an
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice
\Lambda
in a bounded region
{\mathcal{S}}
. The main term is given by some notion of the size of the counting region divided by the determinant of the lattice, and the error term in terms of the successive minima of the
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice. The advantage of viewing
\Lambda
as an
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice, as opposed to its
{\mathbb{Z}}
-structure, is that the dimension of the lattice and hence the number of successive minima are reduced due to the additional symmetry. This yields an improved error term in comparison to the classical Davenport’s lemma.

8.1.
The algebra of lattices

An
\mathbf{{\mathcal{O}}}_{F}
-lattice

\Lambda
is a finitely generated torsion-free
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-module. Then
\Lambda
is an
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-submodule of the
F
-vector space
V=\Lambda\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{F}}F
, so that
\Lambda
spans
V
over
F
. We will call
\dim_{F}V
the
rank
of
\Lambda
.

Consider two
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattices
\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda
for which
\Lambda/\Lambda^{\prime}
is an
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-module of finite length, then there is a composition series

0\subsetneq M_{1}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq M_{r}=\Lambda/\Lambda^{\prime},

with simple composition factors of the form
M_{i}/M_{i-1}\cong{\mathcal{O}}_{F}/\mathfrak{p}_{i}
where
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
is a prime ideal.
We define the
index ideal
of the two lattices as

(\Lambda:\Lambda^{\prime})=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{p}_{i}.

By the Jordan–Hölder theorem this ideal is well defined, see
[
Ser79
, I§5]
.
Moreover, given any two
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattices
\Lambda
and
\Lambda^{\prime}
which span the same
F
-vector space, their index ideal is given by the quotient

(\Lambda:\Lambda^{\prime}):=\frac{(\Lambda:\Lambda\cap\Lambda^{\prime})}{(\Lambda^{\prime}:\Lambda\cap\Lambda^{\prime})}.

We note that this definition is independent of any choices and does not depend on a basis. Furthermore, the index ideal also does not depend on the base field, in the sense that for a field extension
F^{\prime}/F
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{F^{\prime}}
-lattices
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2}\subseteq V
we have
[
Ser79
, Prop. I.12]

N_{F^{\prime}/F}(\Lambda_{1}\colon\Lambda_{2})_{F^{\prime}}=(\Lambda_{1}\colon\Lambda_{2})_{F}.

Therefore we will write
(\Lambda_{1}\colon\Lambda_{2})
if the underlying field is unambiguous.

8.2.
The shape of lattices

We describe the shape of the
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice
\Lambda
in relation to a collection of sets
B_{v}\subseteq V_{v}
indexed by the archimedean places
v
of
F
. We assume that each
B_{v}\subseteq V_{v}
is open, convex, bounded, and rotationally invariant (in the sense that
uB_{v}=B_{v}
for all
|u|_{v}=1
in
F_{v}
).

We embed
V
(and hence
\Lambda
) diagonally in
V_{F,\infty}:=\prod_{v\mid\infty}V_{v}
and we write
B_{F,\infty}:=\prod_{v\mid\infty}B_{v}
. We note that
V_{F,\infty}=V_{{\mathbb{Q}},\infty}
, and so in what follows we denote this by
V_{\infty}
.
For each
1\leq i\leq\dim_{F}V
, the
i
-th successive minimum

\lambda_{i}
of
\Lambda
(with respect to
B_{F,\infty}
) is defined as

\lambda_{i}:=\inf\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\colon\Lambda\cap\lambda B_{F,\infty}\text{ contains }i\text{ $F$-linearly independent vectors}\}.

Suppose further that
V
is endowed with a non-degenerate
F
-bilinear
pairing
T\colon V\times V\to F
.
Then the
discriminant

\Delta_{\Lambda/F}
of a lattice
\Lambda
(with respect to the pairing
T
) is uniquely defined by the following two properties
[
Ser79
, Prop. III.3 and 5]
.

(1)

If
\Lambda
is a free
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-module with basis
v_{i}
, then
\Delta_{\Lambda/F}
is the principal fractional ideal generated by
\det(T(v_{i},v_{j})_{i,j})\in F
.

(2)

For two lattices
\Lambda_{i}
in the same vector space we have
\Delta_{\Lambda_{1}/F}=(\Lambda_{2}:\Lambda_{1})^{2}\Delta_{\Lambda_{2}/F}
.

Alternatively, by
[
Ser79
, Prop. III.10]
discriminants are determined locally so one can compute the local discriminants using the above properties then take the product to compute the global discriminant. Finally, discriminants can also be defined via the dual lattice

\Lambda^{\vee}:=\{x\in V\colon\forall y\in\Lambda,T(x,y)\in{\mathcal{O}}_{F}\},

and then

\Delta_{\Lambda/F}=(\Lambda^{\vee}\colon\Lambda)

[
Ser79
, Prop. III.4]
. For details, we refer the reader to
[
Ser79
]
and
[
Rei03
]
.

8.3.
The covolume of a lattice

To measure the volume of a lattice with respect to a chosen pairing
T\colon V\times V\to F
, which is usually suppressed in the notation, and relate the successive minima to the discriminant, we first define the
determinant
of a lattice
\Lambda
as

{\det}_{F}\,\Lambda:=\sqrt{N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{\Lambda/F})},

or simply
\det\Lambda
when the base field is understood.

Another invariant is given by Haar measures on
V_{v}=V\otimes_{F}F_{v}
which is a locally compact group as well as a finite-dimensional
F_{v}
-vector space for each archimedean place
v
of
F
. We normalise the Haar measure via the pairing
T
in the following way. Fix an isomorphism from
V_{v}
to its Pontryagin dual

V_{v}\to\hat{V}_{v},\eta\mapsto\chi_{\eta}:=\left(x\mapsto\exp(2\pi i\Tr_{F_{v}/{\mathbb{R}}}T(x,\eta))\right)
and let
\mu_{v}
be the unique Haar measure which identifies with its dual measure
\hat{\mu}_{v}
under this isomorphism. If
V=A
is a separable
F
-algebra with pairing given by the trace pairing, as it will be in our applications, then under the natural identification
A_{v}:=\prod_{w\mid v}A_{w}
is a product of copies of
{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathbb{C}}
as
F_{v}
-algebras, the measure
\mu_{v}
on
A_{v}
agrees with the product measure of our normalised Haar measures on
{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathbb{C}}
.

We now show that the determinant and the measure on
V_{\infty}
have a similar dependence on the pairing.
By our convention on normalisation of norms on local fields, we have
|\alpha|_{v}^{[F_{v}\colon{\mathbb{R}}]}=\mu_{v}(\alpha S)/\mu_{v}(S)
for any non-zero measure set
S\subseteq V_{v}
.

Proposition 8.1
.

On
V_{\infty}
we consider the measure
\mu_{F,\infty}:=\prod_{v\mid\infty}\mu_{v}
. The ratio

\frac{\mu_{F,\infty}}{\det_{F}\Lambda}

is independent of the chosen pairing.

Proof.

Let
T
and
T^{\prime}
be two non-degenerate bilinear pairings on
V
, we will write
\mu
and
\Delta
, and
\mu^{\prime}
and
\Delta^{\prime}
for the measures and discriminant defined using these respective pairings.
Define the automorphism
\phi
as the composition
V\xrightarrow{T}V^{\vee}\xleftarrow{T^{\prime}}V
.
We will first see how
\det_{F}\Lambda
varies under
\phi
then show that
\mu_{F,\infty}
changes by the same factor.

We start by proving
\Delta_{\Lambda/F}=(\det\phi)\Delta^{\prime}_{\Lambda/F}
. By the defining property (2) of determinants it suffices to prove this for a single lattice spanning
V
, since the index ideal is independent of the pairing. So suppose that
\Lambda
is free with basis
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
then by property (1) of determinants we find

\det(T(e_{i},e_{j}))=\det(T^{\prime}(e_{i},\phi e_{j}))=\det\phi\det(T^{\prime}(e_{i},e_{j})).

We conclude
\det\Lambda=\sqrt{N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{\Lambda/F})}=\sqrt{N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\det\phi)N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta^{\prime}_{\Lambda/F})}=\sqrt{N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\det\phi)}\det^{\prime}\Lambda
.

We now turn to understanding how
\mu_{F,\infty}
varies under
\phi
. Let
v
be an archimedean place and
let
\mu_{v}
be the self-dual measure on
V_{v}
under the identification
V_{v}\to\hat{V}_{v}
above using the pairing
T
. Using the measure
\mu_{v}
we compute the double Fourier transform of a suitable function
f
on
V_{v}
with respect to the two identifications
V\to\hat{V}
coming from the two pairings
T
and
T^{\prime}
and find

\hat{\hat{f}}^{\prime}=|\det\phi|^{[F_{v}\colon{\mathbb{R}}]}_{v}\hat{\hat{f}}.

This implies that the self-dual measures with respect to the pairings
T
and
T^{\prime}
are related by
\mu^{\prime}_{v}:=\mu_{v}|\det\phi|^{-\frac{[F_{v}\colon{\mathbb{R}}]}{2}}_{v}
. We therefore have

\prod_{v\mid\infty}\mu_{v}=\prod_{v\mid\infty}|\det\phi|_{v}^{\frac{[F_{v}\colon{\mathbb{R}}]}{2}}\mu^{\prime}_{v}=N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\det\phi)^{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{v\mid\infty}\mu^{\prime}_{v},\qed

which concludes the proof.

Remark 8.2
.

Let
F
be a number field of degree
d
, and identify
F_{\infty}
with
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
as
{\mathbb{R}}
-algebras using the identification
{\mathbb{C}}\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
coming from the basis
\{1,i\}
. The trace pairing on
F_{\infty}
and the standard pairing on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
differ by a factor
2^{s}
, where
s
is the number of complex places of
F
. Under these pairings
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is endowed with the Lebesque measure, and
F_{\infty}
with
2^{s}
times the Lebesque measure. This explains why the determinant of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
equals
2^{-s}\sqrt{|\Delta_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}|}
.

For lattices in the
F
-vector space
F^{n}
with the corresponding standard pairing we find that
\sqrt{N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{\Lambda/F})}=[{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{n}\colon\Lambda]
since
\Delta_{{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{n}/F}=1
. This establishes the following result for Serre’s more general notion of lattice, which was proven in the case
V=F^{n}
independently by Bombieri–Vaaler
[
BV83
, Thm. 3&6]
and McFeat
[
McF71
]
.

Proposition 8.3
.

For each
v\mid\infty
let
B_{v}\subseteq V_{v}
be open, convex, bounded, and rotationally invariant. Then

(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{n})^{d}\asymp_{F,n}\frac{\det_{F}\Lambda}{\prod_{v|\infty}\mu_{v}(B_{v})},

for
n=\rank\Lambda
and
d=[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]
.

For the lower bound it suffices to assume the regions
B_{v}
are just symmetric (i.e.
B_{v}=-B_{v}
); the rotationally invariance (i.e.
B_{v}=uB_{v}
for all
|u|_{v}=1
in
F_{v}
) is only required for the upper bound.

Proof.

Let us fix an
F
-isomorphism
V=F^{n}
, then
\det_{F}\Lambda=[{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{n}\colon\Lambda]
. The upper bound on
(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{n})^{d}
follows from
[
BV83
, Thm. 3]
, and the lower bound from
[
BV83
, Thm. 6]
. These show that the left hand side is asymptotically equal to an infinite product given by
\prod_{v\mid\infty}\mu_{v}(B_{v})\prod_{v\nmid\infty}\mu_{v}(\Lambda_{v})
. We now interpret
\prod_{v\nmid\infty}\mu_{v}(\Lambda_{v})
as the reciprocal index
[{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{n}\colon\Lambda]^{-1}
as in the proof of the Corollary to Theorem 5 in
[
Bro01
]
. Note here that the implied pairing on
V
comes from the canonical one on
F^{n}
but we conclude using Proposition
8.1
.
∎

8.4.
Lattices over different base rings

A lattice over the ring of integers of a number field is naturally a lattice over a smaller ring of integers. In this section, we will study an
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice under its
{\mathbb{Z}}
-structure as this is the relevant case for our later work. However, the results contained in this section hold for general separable extensions. We write
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
for
V
as a
{\mathbb{Q}}
-vector space, and as
F/{\mathbb{Q}}
is separable
T_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\Tr_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}\circ T
defines a non-degenerate
{\mathbb{Q}}
-bilinear pairing on
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

The following statement generalises a well-known property for rings of integers which was used in the proof of Proposition
7.15
.

Proposition 8.4
.

For a field extension
F/{\mathbb{Q}}
and an
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice
\Lambda
we have

\Delta_{\Lambda/{\mathbb{Q}}}=N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{\Lambda/F})\Delta_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}^{\rank\Lambda}.

Proof.

The
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice
\Lambda
naturally lives in the
F
-vector space
V
, and the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-lattice
\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Z}}}
naturally lives in
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
. We will compare the dual of
\Lambda
in
V
and
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
in
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

We have

\displaystyle(\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}})^{\vee}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in V_{\mathbb{Q}}\colon\forall y\in\Lambda_{{\mathbb{Z}}},T_{\mathbb{Q}}(x,y)\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}

\displaystyle=\{x\in V\colon\forall y\in\Lambda,T(x,y)\in\delta_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}^{-1}\}

\displaystyle=\delta_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}^{-1}\Lambda^{\vee},

where
\delta_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}^{-1}
is the codifferent of
F/{\mathbb{Q}}
, which consists of the elements of
F
whose integral multiples have integral traces. Now we conclude

\displaystyle\Delta_{\Lambda/{\mathbb{Q}}}

\displaystyle=((\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}})^{\vee}\colon\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}})_{\mathbb{Q}}=N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\delta_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}^{-1}\Lambda^{\vee}\colon\Lambda)_{F}

\displaystyle=N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\delta_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}^{-1})^{\rank\Lambda}N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Lambda^{\vee}\colon\Lambda)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}^{\rank\Lambda}N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{\Lambda/F}).\qed

Recall that the pairing
T
induces a particular measure
\mu_{F,\infty}
on
V_{\infty}=\prod_{v\mid\infty}V_{v}
, and
T_{\mathbb{Z}}
induces a measure
\mu_{{\mathbb{Q}},\infty}
on
V_{\infty}=V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}{\mathbb{R}}
. These spaces are canonically identified and the following result compares the two measures.

Proposition 8.5
.

The measures
\mu_{{\mathbb{Q}},\infty}
and
\mu_{F,\infty}
on
V_{\infty}
are the same.

Proof.

The pairing
T
on
V
induces an identification
V_{v}\to\hat{V}_{v}
for all archimedean places
v
for
F
. These give an isomorphism
\prod_{v\mid\infty}V_{v}\xrightarrow{\cong}\prod_{v\mid\infty}\hat{V}_{v}
, hence
V_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\cong}\hat{V}_{\infty}
.
By definition of the pairing
T_{\mathbb{Q}}
on
V_{\mathbb{Q}}
, we see that the induced identification
V_{{\mathbb{Q}},\infty}\to\hat{V}_{{\mathbb{Q}},\infty}
agrees with the one above. Hence, the induced measures agree as well.
∎

Thus, we drop the field from the notation and just write
\mu_{\infty}
instead.

As an application, the generalised Davenport’s lemma in Proposition
8.11
approximates the number of lattice points of the
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice
\Lambda
in a bounded region
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq V_{\infty}
. Its main term

\frac{\mu_{\infty}({\mathcal{S}})}{|\Delta_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{\frac{\rank_{F}\Lambda}{2}}\det_{F}\Lambda}.

matches the main term obtained from
\Lambda
viewed as a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-lattice, by Proposition
8.4
and Proposition
8.5
.

On the other hand, the error term in Davenport’s lemma depends on the successive minima, which changes depending on how we view
\Lambda
.
Indeed, denoting the successive minima of
\Lambda
by
\lambda_{F,i}
and
\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},i}
for
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we first note that
\rank_{\mathbb{Z}}\Lambda=[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]\rank_{{\mathcal{O}}_{F}}\Lambda
, so the number of successive minima differs in these two cases. Also, the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-minima depend on a region
B_{{\mathbb{Q}},\infty}\subseteq V_{\infty}
and the
F
-minima come from regions
B_{F,v}\subseteq V_{v}
. So the successive minima are a priori completely different when viewing the same lattice over both
{\mathbb{Z}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
.

In contrast, the combination of Propositions
8.3
and
8.5
shows that their product remains the same. Hence, information about
\Lambda
which could be spread out over
n
successive minima is instead consolidated into the remaining
\frac{n}{[F\colon{\mathbb{Q}}]}
minima. That is, over
F
, there will be fewer factors to control, but each will have a stronger dependence on
\Lambda
, yielding a stronger error term in Proposition
8.11
than when using just the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-structure.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
F
be a number field of degree
d
. For an
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice
\Lambda\subseteq V
of rank
n
, suppose that the
n
successive minima
\lambda_{F,i}
are computed with respect to open, convex, bounded and rotationally invariant
B_{v}\subseteq V_{v}
.

Let
\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},i}
for
1\leq i\leq nd
be the successive minima of
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
computed with respect to
B_{\infty}:=\prod_{v\mid\infty}B_{v}\subseteq V_{\infty}
. Then they agree with

\underbrace{\lambda_{F,1},\lambda_{F,1},\ldots,\lambda_{F,1}}_{d\text{ times}},\underbrace{\lambda_{F,2},\lambda_{F,2},\ldots,\lambda_{F,2}}_{d\text{ times}},\ldots,\underbrace{\lambda_{F,n},\lambda_{F,n},\ldots,\lambda_{F,n}}_{d\text{ times}}

up to an absolute constant only depending on
F
and
n
. That is,
\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},(j-1)d+k}\asymp_{n,F}\lambda_{F,j}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
and
1\leq k\leq d
.

We remark that under this choice of unit balls
B_{v}
and
B_{\infty}
we trivially have
\lambda_{F,1}=\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},1}
, but a precise relation for the higher successive minima without the implicit constant depending on
F
and
n
seems not to exist in general.

Proof.

Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be
F
-linearly independent elements in
\Lambda\subseteq A
such that
x_{j}\in\lambda_{F,j}B_{v}
for all
v
.
Fix an integral basis
e_{1},\dots,e_{d}
for
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
. Now
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
is the free
{\mathbb{Z}}
-lattice
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}\subseteq A
spanned by the
e_{k}x_{j}
. For some constant
C
depending on
F
only, we have
|e_{k}|_{v}\leq C
for all
k
and
v
.
We then can diagonally embed
e_{k}x_{j}
into
\prod_{v\mid\infty}(\lambda_{F,j}CB_{v})=C\lambda_{F,j}B_{\infty}
using the identification of real vector spaces
\prod_{v\mid\infty}V_{v}=V_{\infty}
. This provides an upper bound
\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},(j-1)d+k}\ll_{F}\lambda_{F,j}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
and
1\leq k\leq d
.

By Proposition
8.3
we have

\frac{\det_{\mathbb{Q}}\Lambda}{\mu_{\infty}(B_{\infty})}\ll_{dn}\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},1}\cdots\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},dn}\ll_{F,n}(\lambda_{F,1}\cdots\lambda_{F,n})^{d}\ll_{F,n}\frac{\det_{F}\Lambda}{\mu_{\infty}(\prod_{v\mid\infty}B_{v})}.

By Proposition
8.4
and the choice of unit ball for
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
we see that the lower and upper bound agree up to a factor depending only on
F
and
n
, which establishes

\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},1}\cdots\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},dn}\asymp_{F,n}(\lambda_{F,1}\cdots\lambda_{F,n})^{d}.

As we can upper bound each of the
dn
factors on the left by the corresponding factor on the right, we obtain the reverse bound up to a factor only depending on
F
and
n
from the commensurability of the product. This proves the claim.
∎

8.5.
Ideals as lattices

Now we specialise to ideals of number fields or separable algebras, which can be viewed as lattices over the ring of integers of any subfield. We establish some general facts about these lattices. Let
A
be a separable algebra of rank
n
over
F
, with the nondegenerate trace pairing
T:=\text{Tr}_{A/F}
.

Lemma 8.7
.

A fractional ideal
{\mathfrak{d}}\subseteq A
is a rank
n
lattice over
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
with determinant
\det_{F}{\mathfrak{d}}=N_{A/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{d}})|\Delta_{A/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{1/2}
.

Proof.

A fractional ideal of
A
is a finitely generated
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-submodule of
A
and satisfies
{\mathfrak{d}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}_{F}}F=A
. Hence
{\mathfrak{d}}
is a lattice of rank
\dim_{F}A=n
. We compute its discriminant. From
\Delta_{{\mathfrak{d}}/F}=({\mathcal{O}}_{A}:{\mathfrak{d}})^{2}\Delta_{{\mathcal{O}}_{A}/F}
it suffices to compute the discriminant of
{\mathcal{O}}_{A}
and the index ideal
({\mathcal{O}}_{A}:{\mathfrak{d}})
. However, by definition,
({\mathcal{O}}_{A}:{\mathfrak{d}})=N_{A/F}({\mathfrak{d}})
and by
[
Ser79
, Ch. III, Prop. 6]
we have
\Delta_{{\mathcal{O}}_{A}/F}=\Delta_{A/F}
. In this last equation, the first
\Delta
refers to the discriminant of the lattice and the second to the discriminant of the extension as is standard in algebraic number theory. In conclusion, we see that

{\det}_{F}{\mathfrak{d}}=\sqrt{N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\Delta_{{\mathfrak{d}}/F})}=\sqrt{N_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}(N_{A/F}({\mathfrak{d}}))^{2}\Delta_{A/F}}=N_{A/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{d}})\sqrt{\Delta_{A/F}},

as claimed.
∎

To study the successive minima of an ideal
{\mathfrak{a}}\subseteq A
we work with the archimedean places of
A
, which are the ring homomorphisms
A\to{\mathbb{C}}
up to conjugation. We say that an archimedean place
w
extends, or divides, a place
v
of
F
if the homomorphism factors as
F\to A\to{\mathbb{C}}
. If we write
A=\oplus A_{i}
as the product of fields, one shows that a place
w
of
A
correspond to the places of the
A_{i}
through the factorisation
A\to A_{i}\to{\mathbb{C}}
. In particular, we have that
A_{v}:=A\otimes F_{v}
is naturally isomorphic to
\prod_{w\mid v}A_{w}
. The
F_{v}
-algebra
A_{v}:=A\otimes_{F}F_{v}
for an archimedean place
v
of
F
is naturally isomorphic to
A_{v}=\prod_{w\mid v}A_{w}
and can be identified as
F_{v}
-algebras with
{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{v}}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}^{s_{v}}
where
r_{v}
and
s_{v}
are the number of real and complex archimedean places of
A
dividing
v
. By choice of normalisation, the local measure
\mu_{v}
on
A_{v}
is then just the product measure of the usual Haar measures on
{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathbb{C}}
. For a region
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq A_{\infty}
, we write
\vol({\mathcal{S}}):=\mu_{\infty}({\mathcal{S}}).

From now on we write
B_{v}:=\{(x_{w})_{w\mid v}\colon\max_{w\mid v}|x_{w}|_{w}\leq 1\}
. We compute the successive minima of an ideal with respect to these unit balls or small variants, usually denoted
B^{\prime}_{v}
, thereof. We have made this choice such that
B_{\infty}:=\prod_{v\mid\infty}B_{v}\subseteq A_{\infty}
is the unit ball under the maximum norm
\max_{w\mid\infty}|x_{w}|_{w}
. This independence of the base field will simplify notation and some arguments when comparing an
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice to itself as a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-lattice.

Remark 8.8
.

The reader is warned that this choice of unit balls is not typical! Usually one considers unit balls in
A_{v}
under the Euclidean norm, that is,
\{(x_{w})_{w\mid v}:\sum_{w\mid v}|x_{w}|_{w}^{2}\leq 1\}.

However, the two choices are commensurable, indeed

\left\{(x_{w})_{w\mid v}:\max_{w\mid v}|x_{w}|_{w}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right\}\subseteq\left\{(x_{w})_{w\mid v}:\sum_{w\mid v}|x_{w}|_{w}^{2}\leq 1\right\}\subseteq\left\{(x_{w})_{w\mid v}:\max_{w\mid v}|x_{w}|_{w}\leq 1\right\},

and yield the same successive minima up to an absolute constant which depends only on the rank
n
. The choice for the Euclidean norm would not have changed the application, but would have complicated some arguments later in the paper. The ability to designate successive minima in the way most suited to the problem at hand is yet another advantage of setting up our lattices in the level of generality which we have in this section.

We now turn to our chief tool for counting lattice points in
A
in a bounded region, which can be seen as a version of Davenport’s lemma for
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattices and as a generalisation of
[
GH
, Lem. 3.3]
. Indeed, we are able to produce an asymptotic for the number of points of an
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-lattice lying in some bounded region, without requiring that the lattice lives inside
F^{{\rm{rk}}\Lambda}
.
Some constraint on the shape of the region is required and we here make a natural generalisation of the standard condition that the boundary of the region can be parametrised by a controlled number of Lipschitz maps with bounded Lipschitz constant.

Definition 8.9
.

Given a map
\phi:[0,1)^{m}\rightarrow A_{\infty}
for some
m\leq\deg_{\mathbb{R}}A_{\infty}
, we say that
\phi
is
(m,R)
-Lipschitz with respect to
B^{\prime}_{\infty}
if

\phi(\mathbf{x})-\phi(\mathbf{y})\in R|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|B^{\prime}_{\infty},

for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in[0,1)^{m}
.

Recall that
B^{\prime}_{\infty}
is a variant of the unit ball
B_{\infty}\subseteq A_{\infty}
under maximum norm. We recall the familiar situation of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-lattices where the unit ball is instead given by the unit ball in
{\mathbb{R}}^{dn}
under the Euclidean distance and
A_{\infty}
is identified with
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Definition 8.10
.

A subset
S\subseteq A_{\infty}
is said to be in
\text{Lip}_{A}(R,N)
(with respect to
B^{\prime}_{\infty}
) if there exist
N
maps
\phi
which are each
(nd-1,R)
-Lipschitz (with respect to
B^{\prime}_{\infty}
) and whose images together cover
S
.

This definition has been made to introduce a notion of Lipschitz-parametrisability which mirrors the
\text{Lip}(n,1,L,M)
constraint in Widmer’s work
[
Wid10b
]
, but also takes account of the different structure of an
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice and that makes use of the notion of length induced by the choice of local unit balls
B^{\prime}_{v}
. Indeed when one considers the image of
A_{\infty}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{dn}
, the two notions are the same except that the Lipschitz parameter may differ by a constant depending at most on
F
and
n
.

Proposition 8.11
.

Let
F
be a number field,
\Lambda
an
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattice in
A
of rank
n
, and
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq A_{\infty}
a bounded counting region. Let
\tau=(\tau_{w})_{w}\colon A_{\infty}\to A_{\infty}
be an
F_{\infty}
-linear map with
\det_{\mathbb{R}}\tau=1
, and write
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
for the successive minima of
\Lambda
with respect to the local regions
B^{\prime}_{v}:=\tau_{v}^{-1}B_{v}\subseteq A_{v}
.

Suppose that the boundary of
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq A_{\infty}
is in
\text{Lip}_{A}(R,N)
with respect to
B^{\prime}_{\infty}

then there exists a constant
C_{F,n}
, depending at most on
F
and
n
, such that

\left|\#(\Lambda\cap{\mathcal{S}})-\frac{\vol({\mathcal{S}})}{|\Delta_{F/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{\frac{\rank_{F}\Lambda}{2}}\det_{F}\Lambda}\right|\leq C_{F,n}N\left(\frac{R^{nd-1}}{(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{n-1})^{d}\lambda_{n}^{d-1}}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\frac{R^{id}}{(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{i})^{d}}\right),

where we take
\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{i}=1
when when
i=0
.

Notice that
\#(\Lambda\cap{\mathcal{S}})
does not depend on the base ring of
\Lambda
. That is, in estimating the cardinality of this set, we are free to pick the lattice structure on
\Lambda
. The result says we get a counting result with generally acceptable error terms if we pick the unit balls
B^{\prime}_{v}
with a similar relative shape as the counting region.

Remark 8.12
.

One immediate consequence of the result is the useful upper bound

\#(\Lambda\cap S)\ll\sum_{i=0}^{n}\frac{R^{id}}{(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{i})^{d}},

which removes the top error term since it is dominated by the size of the main term.

Proof of Proposition
8.11
.

We naturally identify
A_{\infty}
with
{\mathbb{R}}^{nd}
. Our assumption on the boundary of
{\mathcal{S}}
means that the region
\tau{\mathcal{S}}
is contained within a Euclidean ball of radius
R
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{nd}
. Applying a traditional lattice point counting result (such as
[
Wid10b
, Lem. 5.4]
) to the torsion-free
{\mathbb{Z}}
-module
\tau\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{nd}
, we have that

\displaystyle\left|\#(\tau\Lambda\cap\tau{\mathcal{S}})-\frac{\vol_{{\mathbb{R}}^{nd}}(\tau{\mathcal{S}})}{\det\tau\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C(d,n)\sum_{i=0}^{nd-1}\frac{R^{i}}{\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},1}\cdots\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},i}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}(F,n)\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}\frac{R^{id+k}}{(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{i})^{d}\lambda_{i+1}^{k}},

where we applied Proposition
8.6
in the last step.
We may simplify the main term since
\det\tau\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}=\det\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\vol_{{\mathbb{R}}^{nd}}(\tau S)=\mu_{\infty}(S)
.

Finally, we observe that we can dominate each term
\frac{R^{id+k}}{(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{i})^{d}\lambda_{i+1}^{k}}
independently of
k
according to the size of
R
. Indeed if
R\geq\lambda_{n}
then the term
\frac{R^{nd-1}}{(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{n-1})^{d}\lambda_{n}^{d-1}}
dominates. If
\lambda_{j}>R\geq\lambda_{j-1}
for some
2\leq j\leq n
, then the term
\frac{R^{(j-1)d}}{(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{j-1})^{d}}
dominates. Finally, if
R<\lambda_{1}
, then each term
\frac{R^{id+k}}{(\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{i})^{d}\lambda_{i+1}^{k}}
is at most
O_{F,n}(1)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

In §
9
we will apply the counting result to lattices of rank at most
8
