over a fixed quadratic base field, so the implicit dependence on
n
and
F
is good enough for our applications.

Remark 8.13
.

Traditionally, symmetries in lattices have played major roles in understanding structures of algebraic objects – for example, the theory of cyclotomic fields and the theory complex multiplication. So it is not surprising that studying
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattices, which implies extra symmetry over
{\mathbb{Z}}
-lattices, gives us additional information.

For example, the
{\mathbb{Z}}[i]
-lattices in
{\mathbb{C}}
must be invariant under the rotation by
\pi/2
(which corresponds to multiplication by
i
), so they must be square lattices. In particular, we get that
\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},1}=\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},2}
when the successive minima are computed using a rotationally invariant region.

Thus, the theory of
{\mathcal{O}}_{F}
-lattices should not be viewed as the simple generalisation of Davenport’s lemma to number fields; rather, this should be seen as a strengthening of Davenport’s lemma in the cases where the underlying lattice has extra symmetry.

9.
A family of examples: non-split quadrics

Finally, we provide a new family of examples of symmetric squares of surfaces for which our strategy applies and on which we can explicitly count rational points in a cothin subset. The surfaces that we consider are of the form
X_{d}=\{x^{2}-dy^{2}=zw\}\subseteq{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{3}
, where
d
is a squarefree integer. The first key idea in our method is to use the fact that
X_{d}
is the restriction of scalars of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{d})}
from
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{d})
to
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Notation.
We denote by
L={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{d})
the splitting field of
\Pic X_{d}
, and
K={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{\Delta})
will denote the various quadratic extensions over which we consider the points of
X_{d}
(which yield rational points of
\Sym^{2}X_{d}
). Their compositum will be denoted by
M:=KL={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{\Delta})
.

Our principal aim in this section is to prove the following, which immediately gives assertion (a) in Theorem
1.3
.

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{U}}\subseteq\text{Sym}^{2}X_{d}({\mathbb{Q}})
denote the set of points which do not arise as a pair of
{\mathbb{Q}}
-points or a conjugate pair of
L
-points on
X_{d}
. Then for the absolute anticanonical height
H^{(2)}
from Proposition
3.3
induced from the height
H
defined in Definition
9.4
,

\#\{Q\in{\mathcal{U}}\colon H^{(2)}(Q)\leq B\}=c_{\Hilb^{2}X_{d},{\mathbb{Q}}}B\log B+O_{d}\left(B(\log B)^{3/4}\right).

We remark that the points in
{\mathcal{Z}}:=\Sym^{2}X_{d}({\mathbb{Q}})\setminus{\mathcal{U}}
contribute
\asymp B\log B
, but they need to be removed to obtain the correct leading constant. Indeed,

\#\{P\in X_{d}(L)\colon H(P)\leq B^{\frac{1}{2}}\}\asymp B\log B\quad\mbox{and}\quad\#\{P\in X_{d}({\mathbb{Q}})\colon H(P)\leq B\}\asymp B

and from partial summation, it follows that the contributions to
\#\{Q\in\Sym^{2}X_{d}({\mathbb{Q}})\colon H(Q)\leq B\}
from points of the form

Q=[P_{1},P_{2}]
with
P_{1},P_{2}\in X_{d}({\mathbb{Q}})
, and of the form
Q=[P,\overline{P}]
for
P\in X_{d}(L)
are also of size
\asymp B\log B
. By Lemma
4.1
, these points form a thin subset of
\text{Sym}^{2}X_{d}({\mathbb{Q}})
.

For the remainder of the section, we focus on proving Theorem
9.1
. In the first subsection, we will identify the quadric surface of
X_{d}
with the restriction of scalars of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
from
L
to
{\mathbb{Q}}
. This allows us to compute the various Peyre constant factors associated to
\Sym^{2}X_{d}
as well as the optimal cutoff of
X_{d}
. Moreover, in §
9.2
we identify the pure quadratic points on the surface
X_{d}
with quartic points on
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}
defined over biquadratic extensions containing
L
.
Our tool for counting these points comes from the theory of
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
-lattices which we have just developed in §
8
.
In §
9.3
, we apply this theory to produce an asymptotic for these quartic points on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, generalising Schmidt’s
[
Sch95
]
arguments and drawing ideas from Widmer
[
Wid10b
]
. In particular, we extend all of Schmidt’s approach to a quadratic base field, making very specific use of the structure of biquadratic points over a fixed quadratic subfield to gain an advantage over what can be achieved by considering just quartic points over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

9.1.
Geometry of quadric surfaces

The following result seems well known, but we add a proof for completeness.

Lemma 9.2
.

The surface
X_{d}/\mathbb{Q}
is isomorphic to
\Res_{L/\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{P}^{1}_{L}
.

Proof.

We first construct an
L
-isomorphism

\phi\colon\begin{array}[]{ccc}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}&\longrightarrow&(X_{d})_{L}\\
([s:t],[u:v])&\longmapsto&\left[\frac{sv+tu}{2}:\frac{sv-tu}{2\sqrt{d}}:tv:su\right].\end{array}

which admits an inverse constructed from the two projections

(X_{d})_{L}\to{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L},[x:y:z:w]\mapsto[x\pm\sqrt{d}y:z]=[w:x\mp\sqrt{d}y].

Note that the action of the non-trivial element of order two
\sigma\in\Gal(L/{\mathbb{Q}})
swaps the two factors, and hence
\phi
respects the descent datum on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
, defined in Proposition
3.7
, and the effective descent datum on
(X_{d})_{L}
. Hence
\phi
descends along
L/{\mathbb{Q}}
and yields an isomorphism
\Res_{L/{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}\xrightarrow{\cong}X_{d}
.
∎

From now on we identify
X_{d}
with
\Res_{L/{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
. Using this identification we can compute many geometric invariants of
X_{d}
and its Hilbert scheme of two points directly from understanding the invariants of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.

Lemma 9.3
.

For any non-square
d
, we have

(a)

\beta(X_{d})=1=\beta(\Hilb^{2}X_{d})
;

(b)

\rho(X_{d})=1
,
\rho(\Hilb^{2}X_{d})=2
;

(c)

\alpha(X_{d})=\frac{1}{2}
,
\alpha(\Hilb^{2}X_{d})=\frac{1}{8}
;

(d)

equality of the Peyre constants
c_{X_{d}}=c_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},L}
, and

(e)

The optimal cutoff (in the sense of Definition
7.21
) for
X_{d}
is
\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

(a)

By
[
Lou15
, Lem. 4.2]
we have
\beta(X_{d})=\beta({\mathbb{P}}_{L}^{1})=1
. The second part follows from Proposition
3.15
(c).

(b)

Geometrically
X_{d}
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
. Hence
\Pic{\overline{X}}_{d}={\mathbb{Z}}H_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}H_{2}
, corresponding to the two rulings on
{\overline{X}}_{d}
. The Galois action factors through
\Gal(L/{\mathbb{Q}})
and is generated by the element exchanging
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
. Since
d
is non-square,
\Pic({\overline{X}}_{d})^{\Gamma}
is generated by
H:=H_{1}+H_{2}
. This establishes that the rank of
\Pic X_{d}
is 1, and the rank of the Picard group of the Hilbert scheme is one greater by Proposition
3.15
(b).

(c)

The effective cone of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
is spanned by
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, hence the Galois invariant part of the effective cone is spanned by
H=H_{1}+H_{2}
(Lemma
2.11
). From
-K_{X}=2H
we deduce
\alpha(X_{d})=\frac{1}{2}
.
From
[
LR14
, §3.1]
we see that the effective cone of
\Hilb^{2}({\mathbb{P}}^{1}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{1})
is spanned by
H^{[2]}_{1}-\Delta^{[2]},H^{[2]}_{2}-\Delta^{[2]}
and
\Delta^{[2]}
(see §
3.4
), so the Galois invariant part is generated by
H^{[2]}-2\Delta^{[2]}
. The dual cone is spanned by
h
and
h+\frac{1}{2}\delta
, so the part given by
\langle\cdot,-K_{X}\rangle\leq 1
equals
\{ah+b\delta\colon 0\leq a\leq\frac{1}{2},0\leq b\leq\frac{1}{4}\}
. We conclude that
\alpha(\Hilb^{2}X_{d})=\frac{\alpha(X)}{4}=\frac{1}{8}
.

(d)

We can apply the results from
[
Lou15
, §4.2]
, since
X_{d}=\Res_{L/{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
.

(e)

Since 2 is an
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-cutoff for
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
by Proposition
7.8
,
\frac{1}{2}
is a cutoff for
X_{d}=\Res_{L/{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
by Proposition
7.19
.
Consider the conic
C\subseteq X_{d}
given by
x^{2}-zw=0=y
which satisfies
\deg\left.\omega_{X_{d}}\right|_{C}=4
, whose optimal cutoff is
\frac{2}{4}=\frac{1}{2}
. Since a cutoff for
X_{d}
would also be a cutoff for
C
we conclude that
\frac{1}{2}
is the optimal cutoff for
X_{d}
. ∎

We note that for this surface, as for
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we have the relation
\alpha(\Hilb^{2}X)=\tfrac{1}{2}\alpha(X)\gamma
, where
\gamma
is the optimal anticanonical cutoff for
X
. For the approach of counting the points from each quadratic field
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
separately to work for a del Pezzo surface
X
with
\rank\Pic X=1
, this relation between the
\alpha
-constants and the optimal cutoff will need to hold.

9.2.
Counting pure quadratic points

From the functorial definition of the restriction of scalars
X_{d}=\Res_{L/{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
, we have for any extension
K
of
{\mathbb{Q}}
the bijection
\nu_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1}}\colon{\mathbb{P}}^{1}(K\otimes_{{\mathbb{Q}}}L)\xrightarrow{\cong}X_{d}(K)
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}(L)\xrightarrow{\cong}X_{d}({\mathbb{Q}})
. For instance, instead of counting pure quadratic points in
X_{d}
whose field of definition is
K={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{\Delta})
for some
\Delta
, we may count the points of
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{1}
which are defined over the biquadratic extension
M:={\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{d},\sqrt{\Delta})
but which are not defined over
L
. Let us define the heights that we are considering on either side of this identification between
X_{d}
and
\Res_{L/{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
. We will denote the absolute heights on
X_{d}
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
by
H
and
\mathcal{H}
, respectively. If they are decorated by the subscript
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
, then they will denote the
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-heights; otherwise, they will denote the anticanonical heights on the appropriate varieties.

We start off with the naïve absolute
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-height on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
given by

\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}\colon{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}(\overline{L})\to{\mathbb{R}}_{>0},\quad[s:t]\mapsto\prod_{v\in\Omega_{L}}\max\{|s|_{v},|t|_{v}\}^{\frac{[L_{v}\colon{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}]}{2}}

and the anticanonical height
\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}^{2}
. By Proposition
3.10
these induce heights on
\Res_{L/{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
.

Definition 9.4
.

Let
H_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}
be the
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-height on
X_{d}
induced from the
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-height
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
under the identification
X_{d}=\Res_{L/{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
. We will use the anticanonical height
H:=H^{2}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}
on
X_{d}
.

In particular, by Proposition
3.10
, for a point
P
on
X_{d}
and its corresponding point
Q
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
we have
H(P)=\mathcal{H}(Q)^{2}
.

Remark 9.5
.

Note that
H_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}
is an adelic height defined by the adelic metric of Proposition
3.9
(a), so we have
H_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}(P)=\prod_{v}H_{{\mathcal{O}}(1),v}(P)
for
P=[x\colon y\colon z\colon w]\in X_{d}({\overline{{\mathbb{Q}}}})
. By construction we have

H_{{\mathcal{O}}(1),v}(P)=\prod_{\nu\mid v}\max\{|x+\sqrt{d}y|_{\nu},|x-\sqrt{d}y|_{\nu},|w|_{\nu},|z|_{\nu}\}^{\frac{[L_{\nu}:{\mathbb{Q}}_{p}]}{[L:{\mathbb{Q}}]}}

for
x,y,z,w\in L
. We can simplify these local heights easily in many cases.

(1)

At all primes but the finitely many
p\mid 2d
we have

H_{{\mathcal{O}}(1),p}(P)=\max\{|x|_{p},|y|_{p},|w|_{p},|z|_{p}\},

as it should be for all but finitely many places, as
H_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}
is an adelic height.

(2)

If
p\mid d
is odd, then we find that

H_{{\mathcal{O}}(1),p}(P)=\max\{|x|_{p},|d|_{p}^{\frac{1}{2}}|y|_{p},|z|_{p},|w|_{p}\}.

Instead of counting
K
-points on
X_{d}
we count
M
-points on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
. We define

Z({\mathbb{P}}^{1},K,d,Y):=\mathcal{\#}\left\{P\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}(M)\,:\,{\mathbb{Q}}\left(\sqrt{d},P\right)=M,\,\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}(P)\leqslant Y\right\}.

Providing a suitably uniform count of such quartic points is the subject of the following theorem and the major ingredient in the proof of Manin’s conjecture for
\Sym^{2}X_{d}
.

Theorem 9.6
.

For any quadratic field
K/{\mathbb{Q}}
disjoint from
L
, any
\epsilon>0
and any
Y\geq 1
, we have

Z({\mathbb{P}}^{1},K,d,Y)=c_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},M}Y^{8}+O_{d,\epsilon}\left(Y^{7}\frac{(h_{M}R_{M})^{3/4}(1+\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s))}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/2}}\right),

where
h_{M}
and
R_{M}
are the class number and regulator of the compositum
M=KL
respectively, and
c_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},M}
is Schanuel’s constant for
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
over
M

[
Sch79
, Cor., p. 447]
. The implicit constant does not depend on
K
.

Remark 9.7
.

This result should be compared to the works of Gao
[
Gao95
, Thm. 3.1]
and Widmer
[
Wid10b
, Thm. 3.1]
. In our particular setting, the dependence on
\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}
in the error term of Theorem
9.6
improves on both results, neither of which would have been sufficient for our application.

Theorem
9.6
is proven by adapting Schmidt’s proof to a quadratic number field base, namely
L
, and applying the theory of
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
-lattice counting, rather than the traditional
{\mathbb{Z}}
-lattice counting. Much of Schmidt’s argument was already generalised to a number field base by Widmer in the proof of
[
Wid10b
, Thm. 3.1]
, however one particular argument could not be since Widmer handles higher degree extensions than the quadratic extensions considered in this paper and by Schmidt. In particular, we make the error terms in the geometry of numbers explicit in a particular parameter (namely, the partition parameter
\mathbf{i}
, see §
9.3.2
) which Widmer treats uniformly. Using a trick of Schmidt, we are able to achieve a saving in the sum over this parameter.
Moreover, further savings are accrued by using the
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
-lattice perspective, which provides sharper error terms as we have improved control of the successive minima. The union of these savings means that the error term in Theorem
9.6
only just suffices to establish Theorem
1.3
, as we shall see later.

9.3.
Reinterpreting the counting problem

We are interested in the counting function, using the notation from the beginning of §
9
,

Z({\mathbb{P}}^{1},K,d,Y)=\#\{[x_{0}:x_{1}]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}(M):\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}([x_{0}:x_{1}])\leq Y\text{ and }L(x_{0}\colon x_{1})=M\},

where
L(x_{0}\colon x_{1})
is the residue field of
[x_{0}\colon x_{1}]\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{L}
.

We begin with basic manipulations to move the counting problem to
M^{2}
instead of
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}(M)
. Since rescaling
(x_{0},x_{1})
corresponds to the same projective point, we first remove rescaling by non-units, and reduce the problem into a lattice point counting problem at the same time.

9.3.1.
Reduction to counting in ideals

Fix
\mathfrak{a}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{a}_{h_{M}}
integral ideals representing the classes of
\Cl M
, the class group of
M
. Now represent a projective point by coordinates
(x_{0},x_{1})
such that the fractional ideal
\langle x_{0},x_{1}\rangle
of
M
generated by the coordinates equals one of the
{\mathfrak{a}}_{i}
. This representation is unique up to rescaling by a unit in
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}^{\times}
, which is a finitely generated abelian group of rank
r+s-1
, where
r=r_{M}
denotes the number of real embeddings of
M
and
s=s_{M}
the number of its conjugate pairs of complex embeddings. Let us decompose this group into two parts.

Definition 9.8
.

Consider the three quadratic subfields
K_{j}
of
M
. For the real fields
K_{j}
we define
\epsilon_{j}
as the unique fundamental unit in
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{j}}
with
\epsilon_{j}>1
. We let
\mathbb{U}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}_{M}
be the subgroup generated by the
\epsilon_{j}
, and
\bar{\mathbb{U}}
the saturation of
\mathbb{U}
in the full unit group.

To simplify the exposition we will occasionally use
\epsilon_{j}=1
when
K_{j}
is imaginary.

Proposition 9.9
.

(a)

The abelian group
\mathbb{U}
, and hence
\bar{\mathbb{U}}
, is torsion-free and has rank
r+s-1
.

(b)

The index
q_{M}
of
\mathbb{U}
in
\bar{\mathbb{U}}
satisfies
q_{M}\mid 8
.

(c)

The index of
\mathbb{U}
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}^{\times}
equals
q_{M}w_{M}
, for
w_{M}
the number of torsion units in
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}
.

Proof.

In this proof we restrict to the
j
for which
K_{j}
is real.

(a)

Suppose that
\eta:=\prod_{j}\epsilon_{j}^{c_{j}}
for
c_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
is a torsion unit. Let
\sigma_{j}\in\Aut_{\mathbb{Q}}M
be the unique non-trivial element fixing
K_{j}
. Since
\sigma_{j}
preserves
\epsilon_{j}
and sends the other
\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}
to
\pm\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}^{-1}
we see that
\eta\sigma_{j}(\eta)=\pm\epsilon_{j}^{2c_{j}}
is also a torsion unit. Hence
c_{j}=0
for all
j
. This also proves that the
\epsilon_{j}
for
K_{j}
real form a basis for
\mathbb{U}
. Hence the rank equals either
1
or
3
depending on whether
(r,s)=(0,2)
or
(4,0)
.

(b)

From the definition of the
\epsilon_{j}
we have an isomorphism
{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{j}}^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}\cong\langle\epsilon_{j}\}
. Consider the composition
\prod_{j}\langle\epsilon_{j}\rangle\to\bar{\mathbb{U}}\to\prod_{j}{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{j}}^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}\cong\prod_{j}\langle\epsilon_{j}\rangle
of the inclusion and relative norms, which equals squaring. The first homomorphism is injective by (a). We prove that the second homomorphism is injective too. Suppose that
x\in M
has norm
1
in all three quadratic subfields. This means that
\sigma(x)=x^{-1}
for all non-trivial
\sigma\in\Aut_{\mathbb{Q}}M
, hence for
j\neq j^{\prime}
we have
x^{-1}=\sigma_{j}\circ\sigma_{j^{\prime}}(x)=\sigma_{j}(\sigma_{j^{\prime}}x)=\sigma_{j}(x^{-1})=x
. Hence
x=\pm 1
and it represents the unit of
\bar{\mathbb{U}}
.

Hence the composite homomorphism is injective and the rank of the free group is at most 3, the result follows.

(c)

As
\bar{\mathbb{U}}
is a maximal free subgroup of
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}^{\times}
we see that the index of
\bar{\mathbb{U}}
in the unit group is
w_{M}
. The result follows. ∎

Remark 9.10
.

In
[
Sch95
]
and
[
Wid10b
]
the unit action is split into a maximal free subgroup,
\bar{\mathbb{U}}
in our case, and the finite quotient which is isomorphic to the torsion subgroup of
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}^{\times}
. The approach works for any subgroup of finite index, that is of maximal rank
r+s-1
. For our application this particular choice
\mathbb{U}
will be important.

Note that an element of
M^{2}\setminus(0,0)
up to the action by
\mathbb{U}
has
q_{M}w_{M}
representatives which produce the same projective point. We find that for all
Y\geq 1
the function
Z({\mathbb{P}}^{1},K,d,Y)
equals

\frac{1}{q_{M}w_{M}}\sum_{i=1}^{h_{M}}\#\{(x_{0},x_{1})\in M^{2}\setminus(0,0):\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}([x_{0}:x_{1}])\leq Y,\langle x_{0},x_{1}\rangle={\mathfrak{a}}_{i},L(x_{0}\colon x_{1})=M\}/\mathbb{U}.

The definition of the absolute
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
-height for the points in the inner cardinality above evaluates to

\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}([x_{0}:x_{1}])=N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}}_{i})^{-1/4}\prod_{w\in\Omega_{M}^{\infty}}\max\{|x_{0}|_{w},|x_{1}|_{w}\}^{\frac{d_{w}}{4}}.

Let
\mu_{M}
denotes the generalised Möbius function on the ideals of
{\mathcal{O}}_{M}
. Using Möbius inversion we arrive at the expression

\frac{1}{w_{M}q_{M}}\sum_{i=1}^{h_{M}}\sum_{0\neq{\mathfrak{b}}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{M}}\!\!\mu_{M}({\mathfrak{b}})\,\#\{(x_{0},x_{1})\colon\!\prod_{w\mid\infty}\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1),w}(x_{0},x_{1})^{\frac{d_{w}}{4}}\leq T,x_{0},x_{1}\in{\mathfrak{a}}_{i}{\mathfrak{b}},L(x_{0}\colon x_{1})=M\}/\mathbb{U}

with
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1),w}(x_{0},x_{1})=\max\{|x_{0}|_{w},|x_{1}|_{w}\}
and
T=N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}}_{i})^{\frac{1}{4}}Y
.

So, it suffices to count pairs
x_{0},x_{1}\in{\mathfrak{a}}_{i}{\mathfrak{b}}
such that
\prod_{w\mid\infty}{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1),w}(x_{0},x_{1})^{\frac{d_{w}}{4}}\leq T
. We now rewrite this condition while accounting for the action of
\mathbb{U}
.

9.3.2.
Accounting for the action of
\mathbb{U}

For a number field
F
, the unit group modulo torsion units embeds as a lattice in the hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{r_{F}+s_{F}}
given by
\sum d_{v}x_{v}=0
under the map
\ell\colon F^{\times}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{r_{F}+s_{F}},x\mapsto(\log|x|_{v})_{v\mid\infty}
. By definition of the regulator
R_{F}
of
F
the determinant of this lattice equals
\sqrt{r_{F}+s_{F}}R_{F}
. For the biquadratic field
F=M
we will suppress the subscript in the case of
r
and
s
.

We diagonally embed
M
in
M_{\infty}=\prod_{w\mid\infty}M_{w}
via the natural diagonal embedding
\alpha\mapsto(\sigma_{w}(\alpha))_{w\mid\infty}
. We extend
M\hookrightarrow M_{\infty}
componentwise to embed
M^{2}
into
M_{\infty}^{2}
. Given an ideal
{\mathfrak{a}}
in
M
, we write
\Lambda({\mathfrak{a}})
for
{\mathfrak{a}}\times{\mathfrak{a}}\subseteq M^{2}
which naturally lies in
M_{\infty}^{2}
. General pairs of coordinates in
M^{2}
map to
{\mathbb{R}}^{r+s}_{\geq 0}
by

M^{2}\hookrightarrow M^{2}_{\infty}\xrightarrow{({\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1),w})_{w}}{{\mathbb{R}}^{r+s}_{\geq 0}},

(9.1)

where the local height function
{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1),w}\colon M^{2}_{w}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
collects its arguments from the two factors of
M^{2}_{\infty}
indexed by the infinite place
w
of
M/{\mathbb{Q}}
.

To account for the action of
\mathbb{U}
we will find a
\mathbb{U}
-fundamental region. For every
j
with
K_{j}
a real quadratic field let us write
v_{j}
for the place of
K_{j}
for which
|\epsilon_{j}|_{v_{j}}>1
and
v^{\prime}_{j}
for the conjugate place. We define for
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in M^{n}_{\infty}
with
x_{i}=(x_{i,w})_{w}\in\prod_{w\mid\infty}M_{w}
the function

\psi_{j}(x)=\frac{\prod_{w\mid v_{j}}\max_{i}\{|x_{i,w}|_{w}\}^{d_{w}}}{\prod_{w\mid v^{\prime}_{j}}\max_{i}\{|x_{i,w}|_{w}\}^{d_{w}}},

which we use for both
n=1
and
n=2
. Note that for
n=1
the function
\psi_{j}
simplifies to

\psi_{j}(x)=\frac{|N_{M/K_{j}}(x)|_{v_{j}}}{|N_{M/K_{j}}(x)|_{v^{\prime}_{j}}}=\frac{|N_{M/K_{j}}(x)|^{2}_{v_{j}}}{|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(x)|}

where
v^{\prime}_{j}
is the conjugate place of
v_{j}
. Here we have used that
d_{v_{j}}=d_{v^{\prime}_{j}}=1
as the two places of
K_{j}
are real.

Lemma 9.11
.

(a)

Let
v
be a place of one of the
K_{j}
. We have

\prod_{w\mid v}|\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}|_{w}^{d_{w}}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }j\neq j^{\prime};\\
|\epsilon_{j}|_{v}^{2}&\text{ if }j=j^{\prime}.\end{cases}

(b)

For
x\in M^{n}_{\infty}
we have

\psi_{j}(\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}x)=\begin{cases}\psi_{j}(x)&\text{ if }j\neq j^{\prime};\\
\epsilon_{j}^{4}\psi_{j}(x)&\text{ if }j=j^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Proof.

(a)

We find

\prod_{w\mid v}|\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}|_{w}^{d_{w}}=|N_{M/K_{j}}(\epsilon_{j^{\prime}})|^{d_{v}}_{v}.

If
j=j^{\prime}
then
N_{M/K_{j}}(\epsilon_{j})=\epsilon^{2}_{j}
, and either
\epsilon_{j}=1
in the complex case or
d_{v}=1
in the real case, and if
j\neq j^{\prime}
then
N_{M/K_{j}}(\epsilon_{j^{\prime}})=1
.

(b)

By definition and the discussion above we have

\psi_{j}(\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}x)=\psi_{j}(\epsilon_{j^{\prime}})\psi_{j}(x)=\frac{\prod_{w\mid v}|\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}|_{w}^{2d_{w}}}{|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\epsilon_{j^{\prime}})|}\psi_{j}(x).

The result now follows from part (a). ∎

Corollary 9.12
.

An element
(x_{0},x_{1})\in M^{n}\setminus(0,0)
has a unique representative under the action of
\mathbb{U}
for which

\psi_{j}(x)\in[\epsilon_{j}^{-2},\epsilon_{j}^{2})

for all
j
, for which
K_{j}
is real.

Let us write
S^{(n)}(T)
for the union of
(0,0)
and

\left\{(x_{i})_{i}\in M_{\infty}^{n}\setminus(0,0)\colon\prod_{w\mid\infty}\max_{i}\{|x_{i,w}|_{w}\}^{\frac{d_{w}}{4}}\leq T,\,\,\psi_{j}(x)\in[\epsilon_{j}^{-2},\epsilon_{j}^{2})\,\text{ for all }j\text{ with }K_{j}\text{ real}\right\},

so that
S^{(n)}(T)
is cut out by the conditions in Corollary
9.12
and contains all points in
M^{n}\setminus(0,0)
of height at most
T
.

Proposition 9.13
.

We have
S^{(n)}(T)=TS^{(n)}(1)
for any
T>0
, and

\vol S^{(n)}(T)=q_{M}\left(2^{r}\pi^{s}\right)^{n+1}(n+1)^{r+s-1}R_{M}T^{n}.

Proof.

It is clear that the condition on
S^{(n)}(T)
imposed by the
\psi_{j}
is invariant under multiplying an element of
M_{\infty}^{n}
by a positive real number. On the other hand, from
\prod_{w\mid\infty}T^{d_{w}/4}=T
we see that the height condition scales linearly with
T
.

For the second part, the volume of a
\mathbb{U}
-fundamental domain is
q_{M}
times the volume of a
\bar{\mathbb{U}}
-fundamental region. Such a volume was computed by Widmer
[
Wid10b
, p. 33]
to be

\left(2^{r}\pi^{s}\right)^{n+1}(n+1)^{r+s-1}R_{M}.\qed

Now we can write the counting function at the end of §
9.3.1
as

\frac{1}{q_{M}w_{M}}\sum_{i=1}^{h_{M}}\sum_{0\neq{\mathfrak{b}}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{M}}\!\!\mu_{M}({\mathfrak{b}})\,\#\{x_{0},x_{1}\in{\mathfrak{a}}_{i}{\mathfrak{b}}\colon\!(x_{0},x_{1})\in S^{(2)}(T),L(x_{0}\colon x_{1})=M\}

with
T=N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}}_{i})^{\frac{1}{4}}Y
, because of the local height function
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1),w}(x_{0},x_{1})=\max\{|x_{0}|_{w},|x_{1}|_{w}\}
.

9.3.3.
The partition of the fundamental region

We would like to count the appropriate lattice points inside
S(T):=S^{(2)}(T)
. However, a priori, this region might be contained within a ball with radius of order
e^{R_{M}}T
which would give catastrophically large dependence of the error term on the regulator in an application of Proposition
8.11
. To handle this, we follow Schmidt
[
Sch95
, §4]
to partition the counting region
S(T)
into approximately
R_{M}
subsets of radius approximately
T
so that this dependence is reduced significantly.
When
K_{j}
is real, we define
t_{j}=\lfloor R_{K_{j}}\rfloor+1
and
u_{j}=e^{R_{K_{j}}/t_{j}}
. Hence
u_{j}^{t_{j}}=\epsilon_{j}
, and Schmidt shows

1<u_{j}\ll 1.

(9.2)

For
K_{j}/{\mathbb{Q}}
an imaginary quadratic extension, we have
R_{K_{j}}=1
and define
t_{j}=1
and
u_{j}=1
.

We take
\mathbf{i}=(i_{j})_{j}
with
i_{j}
an integer in
[-t_{j},t_{j})
. We then define

S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)

as the region given by the height condition and
\psi_{j}(x)\in[u_{j}^{2i_{j}},u_{j}^{2i_{j}+2})
for all
j
. We now have the partition
S(T)=\bigcup_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{I}}S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)
. We will write
S_{0}(T)
for the part corresponding to
\mathbf{i}=(0,0,0)
. Note that if
K_{j}
is imaginary, we have
i_{j}\in\{-1,0\}
, but
S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)
for any
\mathbf{i}
with
i_{j}=-1
will be empty. In what follows we ignore those parts, and we define
{\mathcal{I}}
as the collection of such triples
\mathbf{i}
as above with
i_{j}=0
whenever
K_{j}
is imaginary. Let us write
t:=\#{\mathcal{I}}
.

Proposition 9.14
.

We have
t\asymp q_{M}R_{M}
with an absolute implicit constant.

Proof.

By definition
t_{j}\asymp R_{K_{j}}
with absolute implicit constant. The equality
q_{M}R_{M}\asymp\prod_{j}R_{K_{j}}
follows from (7.26) and (7.27) in
[
FT93
, Ch. VIII]
.
∎

For every
j
we have an
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear morphism
\tau_{j}\colon M_{w}\to M_{w}
given by
x\mapsto|\epsilon_{j}|_{w}^{-1/2t_{j}}x
, which gives a linear morphism
\tau_{j}\colon M_{\infty}\to M_{\infty}
of determinant
1
. We now define
\tau_{\mathbf{i}}=\prod_{j}\tau_{j}^{i_{j}}
. We often abuse notation and write
\tau_{\mathbf{i}}
also for the induced linear automorphism of
M_{\infty}^{2}
.

Lemma 9.15
.

The linear automorphism
\tau_{\mathbf{i}}
of
M^{2}_{\infty}
maps
S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)
to
S_{0}(T)
.

Proof.

It is immediate by the product formula that the height condition

\prod_{w\mid\infty}\max_{i}\{|x_{i,w}|_{w}\}^{\frac{d_{w}}{4}}\leq T

is satisfied for
x=(x_{0},x_{1})\in M^{2}_{\infty}\setminus(0,0)
if and only if it satisfied for
\tau_{\mathbf{i}}x
.

We consider the point
\tau_{\mathbf{i}}x\in M^{2}_{\infty}
with coordinates
x_{i}\prod_{j^{\prime}}|\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}|_{w}^{-i_{j^{\prime}}/2t_{j^{\prime}}}\in M_{w}
indexed by
i\in\{0,1\}
and
w\in\Omega^{\infty}_{M}
. The function
\psi_{j}
only detects the factor with
j^{\prime}=j
, and we have

\psi_{j}(\tau_{\mathbf{i}}x)=u_{j}^{-2i_{j}}\psi_{j}(x).

Hence
\psi_{j}(x)\in[u_{j}^{2i_{j}},u_{j}^{2i_{j}+2})
for all
j
if and only if
\psi_{j}(\tau_{\mathbf{i}}x)\in[1,u_{j}^{2})
for all
j
.
∎

Remark 9.16
.

Our description of the action of the unit group and the partitioning of the fundamental domain is rather different from that of Gao and Widmer and has more in common with Schmidt’s approach. This is so that we can use explicitly the way in which the unit structure in
M
is more or less inherited from the units in its quadratic subfields. In more general extensions, there is not such a clear relationship and this perspective is no longer practical or useful.

Proposition 9.17
.

The region
S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)
is
\text{Lip}_{M^{2}}(R,N)
with respect to
B^{\prime}_{\infty,\mathbf{i}}:=\tau_{\mathbf{i}}B_{\infty}
, where
R\leq\kappa T
for a constant
\kappa
depending at most on
d
and
N\ll 1
.

Proof.

We only need to prove
S_{0}(T)
is
\text{Lip}_{M^{2}}
with respect to
B_{\infty}
. This follows from
[
Wid10b
, Lem. A.1]
.
∎

9.3.4.
Ideals under different lattice structures

We now consider the ideal
{\mathfrak{d}}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{M}
with lattice structure inherited from the partition function. To that end, define
B_{v}\subseteq M_{v}
to be the unit region under the maximum norm
\{(x_{w})_{w\mid v}\colon|x_{w}|_{w}\leq 1\}
. We consider
{\mathfrak{d}}
as lattice with respect to the local regions
B^{\prime}_{\mathbf{i},v}:=\tau_{\mathbf{i},v}^{-1}B_{v}=\{(x_{w})_{w\mid v}\colon|x_{w}|_{w}\prod_{j}|\epsilon_{j}|_{w}^{-i_{j}/2t_{j}}\leq 1\}
.

Definition 9.18
.

Let
\lambda_{i}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})
denote the successive minimum of the
\mathcal{O}_{L}
-lattice
{\mathfrak{d}}\subseteq M
with respect to
B^{\prime}_{v,\mathbf{i}}
.

To study how the successive minima of
{\mathfrak{d}}
, especially the first, vary with
\mathbf{i}
we introduce the following functions.

Definition 9.19
.

We define for
K_{j}
a real quadratic field

m^{(j)}\colon M_{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0},(x_{w})_{w}\mapsto\max_{v\in\Omega_{K_{j}}^{\infty}}\left\{\prod_{w\mid v}|x_{w}|^{d_{w}/2}_{w}\right\},\quad\text{ and }\quad m:=\left[\prod_{j:K_{j}\text{ real}}m^{(j)}\right]^{1/\#\{j:K_{j}\text{ real}\}}.

We also define

m^{(j)}_{\mathbf{i}}(x):=m^{(j)}(\tau_{\mathbf{i}}x)
and

m_{\mathbf{i}}(x):=m(\tau_{\mathbf{i}}x)
.

Note that
m^{(j)}(x)
is the maximum among the numerator and denominator of
\psi_{j}(x)
for
x\in M_{\infty}
.

Using Proposition
9.11
(a) we see that

\displaystyle m_{\textbf{i}}^{(j)}(x)

\displaystyle=\max_{v\in\Omega_{K_{j}}^{\infty}}\left\{\prod_{w\mid v}\left[(\prod_{j^{\prime}}|\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}|_{w}^{-d_{w}i_{j^{\prime}}/4t_{j^{\prime}}})|x_{w}|^{d_{w}/2}_{w}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\max_{v\in\Omega_{K_{j}}^{\infty}}\left\{\left[\prod_{j^{\prime}}\left(\prod_{w\mid v}|\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}|_{w}^{d_{w}/2}\right)^{-i_{j^{\prime}}/2t_{j^{\prime}}}\right]\prod_{w\mid v}|x|_{w}^{d_{w}/2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\max_{v\in\Omega_{K_{j}}^{\infty}}\left(|\epsilon_{j}|_{v}^{-i_{j}/2t_{j}}\prod_{w\mid v}|x_{w}|^{d_{w}/2}_{w}\right)=\max_{v\in\Omega_{K_{j}}^{\infty}}\left(|\epsilon_{j}|_{v}^{-i_{j}/2t_{j}}|N_{M/K_{j}}(x)|^{d_{v}/2}_{v}\right)

which simplifies further as
d_{v}=1
for
v
a place of a real quadratic field
K_{j}
.

Lemma 9.20
.

The function
m_{\mathbf{i}}
assumes a positive minimum on the non-zero elements of an ideal
{\mathfrak{d}}\subseteq M
.

We will write
\mu_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})>0
for this minimum.

Proof.

It is clear that
m_{\textbf{i}}(x)=0
if and only if
x=0
, hence the minimum is positive if it exists.

Now note that for a fractional ideal
{\mathfrak{d}}
of a number field
F
, and a sequence of numbers
\lambda_{v}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
indexed by the archimedean places of
F
the function

x\mapsto\max_{v}(\lambda_{v}|x|_{v})

on
x\in{\mathfrak{d}}
takes its values in a discrete subset of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
as there are only finitely many elements of
x\in{\mathfrak{d}}
with
\max_{v}(\lambda_{v}|x|_{v})\leq X
for every
X>0
by Proposition
8.11
.

For each
j
with
K_{j}
a real quadratic field, we have

m_{\textbf{i}}^{(j)}(x)^{2}=\max_{v\in\Omega_{K_{j}}^{\infty}}\left(|\epsilon_{j}|_{v}^{-i_{j}/t_{j}}|N_{M/K_{j}}(x)|_{v}\right).

Hence by the result applied to a real
K_{j}
with the ideal
N_{M/K_{j}}({\mathfrak{d}})
we see that
m_{\textbf{i}}^{(j)}
takes its value in a discrete set.

We conclude that the image of
m_{\textbf{i}}({\mathfrak{d}})
is a discrete subset of
{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
, which proves the claim.
∎

The definition of
\mu_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})
allows us to prove the bound in Proposition
9.21
which will be crucial to handle the error terms in our count after the application of Proposition
8.11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an ideal class in
\mathcal{O}_{M}
. Recalling that
t
denotes the number of partitions of the
\mathbb{U}
-fundamental domain of the unit lattice, we define

N(\mathcal{C}):=\sum_{n=1}^{t}\frac{1}{N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})^{1/4}},

where the ideals
{\mathfrak{c}}_{n}
belong to the ideal class
\mathcal{C}
, and they are ordered by increasing norms
N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{1})\leqslant N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{2})\leqslant\cdots
. We will relate the sum of the reciprocals of the first successive minima to this norm, thus allowing for some non-trivial savings in the sum of the error term over the different parts of the partition.

Proposition 9.21
.

We have

\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})}\ll N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{d}})^{-1/4}N({\mathcal{D}}^{-1}),

where
{\mathcal{D}}
is the ideal class of
{\mathfrak{d}}
.

Remark 9.22
.

The above result is more or less the only facet of Schmidt’s paper which was not generalised previously to base fields other than
{\mathbb{Q}}
, although a version was established for ideals viewed as
{\mathbb{Z}}
-lattices by Debaene
[
Deb19
, Thm 11]
. In Widmer’s approach this sum is estimated by
N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{d}})^{-1/4}R_{M}
which would not suffice for our purposes.

Proof of Proposition
9.21
.

Let
\beta\in{\mathfrak{d}}
achieve
\lambda_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})
, that is
\max_{w\mid\infty}\left(|\beta|_{w}\prod_{j}|\epsilon_{j}|_{w}^{-i_{j}/2t_{j}}\right)
is minimal. Firstly, observe that for each
j
we have

\displaystyle m_{\textbf{i}}^{(j)}(\beta)=\max_{v\in\Omega_{K_{j}}^{\infty}}\left\{\prod_{w\mid v}(\prod_{j}|\epsilon_{j}|_{w}^{-d_{w}i_{j}/4t_{j}})|\beta|_{w}^{d_{w}/2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{v\in\Omega_{K_{j}}^{\infty}}\max_{w\mid v}(\prod_{j}|\epsilon_{j}|_{w}^{-i_{j}/2t_{j}})|\beta|_{w}

\displaystyle=\max_{w\mid\infty}(\prod_{j}|\epsilon_{j}|_{w}^{-i_{j}/2t_{j}})|\beta|_{w}=\lambda_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i}).

To deduce the inequality above we use
\prod_{w}x_{w}^{d_{w}/2}\leq\max_{w}x_{w}
since
\sum_{w\mid v}d_{w}=2d_{v}=2
. Hence, we have
m_{\mathbf{i}}(\beta)\leq\lambda_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})
. Thus it suffices to bound

\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}}\frac{1}{\mu_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})}.

Let
\alpha:=\alpha({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})
be an element of
{\mathfrak{d}}
achieving
\mu_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})
. Then we claim that
\psi_{j}(\alpha)\in[\epsilon_{j}^{-4},\epsilon_{j}^{4})
for each
j
such that
K_{j}
is real.

Indeed suppose that
\psi_{1}(\alpha)>\epsilon_{1}^{4}
, noting that this is only possible if
K_{j}
is real. We will prove that
m_{\mathbf{i}}(\alpha)>m_{\mathbf{i}}(\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\alpha)
, contradicting the minimality of
m_{\mathbf{i}}
at
\alpha
.

First note that the terms in
m^{(2)}_{j}
and
m^{(3)}_{j}
have been set up so they are unaffected by multiplying by
\epsilon_{1}
as per Proposition
9.11
(a). To prove
m^{(1)}_{\mathbf{i}}(\alpha)>m^{(1)}_{\mathbf{i}}(\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\alpha)
we recall
m^{(1)}_{\mathbf{i}}(x)=\max_{v\in\Omega_{K_{1}}^{\infty}}\left(|\epsilon_{1}|_{v}^{-i_{1}/2t_{1}}|N_{M/K_{1}}(x)|^{1/2}_{v}\right)
and first prove that the maximum in
m^{(1)}_{\mathbf{i}}(\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\alpha)
is attained for
v=v_{1}
the place
K_{1}
for which
v_{1}(\epsilon_{1})>1
as in §
9.3.2
. Indeed,

\frac{|\epsilon_{1}|_{v_{1}}^{-i_{1}/t_{1}}|N_{M/K_{1}}(\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\alpha)|_{v_{1}}}{|\epsilon_{1}|_{v^{\prime}_{1}}^{-i_{1}/t_{1}}|N_{M/K_{1}}(\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\alpha)|_{v^{\prime}_{1}}}=\epsilon_{1}^{-2i_{1}/t_{1}}\psi_{1}(\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\alpha)\geq\epsilon_{1}^{-2}\epsilon_{1}^{-2}\epsilon_{1}^{4}=1

since
i_{1}\in[-t_{1},t_{1})
and
\psi_{1}(\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\alpha)=\epsilon_{1}^{-2}\psi_{1}(\alpha)
by Proposition
9.11
(b).
We now get

\displaystyle m^{(1)}_{\mathbf{i}}(\alpha)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq|\epsilon_{1}|_{v_{1}}^{-i_{1}/t_{1}}|N_{M/K_{1}}(\alpha)|_{v_{1}}>\epsilon_{1}^{-2}\left(|\epsilon_{1}|_{v_{1}}^{-i_{1}/t_{1}}|N_{M/K_{1}}(\alpha)|_{v_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=|\epsilon_{1}|_{v_{1}}^{-i_{1}/t_{1}}|N_{M/K_{1}}(\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\alpha)|_{v_{1}}=m^{(1)}_{\mathbf{i}}(\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\alpha)^{2}.

Thus we conclude that

m_{\mathbf{i}}(\alpha)=\prod_{j}m_{\mathbf{i}}^{(j)}(\alpha)^{1/\#\{j:K_{j}\text{ real}\}}>\prod_{j}m^{(j)}_{\mathbf{i}}(\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\alpha)^{1/\#\{j:K_{j}\text{ real}\}}=m_{\mathbf{i}}(\epsilon_{1}^{-1}\alpha),

which contradicts the minimality of
\alpha
. The proof in the case
\psi_{1}(\alpha)<\epsilon_{1}^{-4}
is exactly the same just with all the exponents reversed.

For any
\alpha\in{\mathfrak{d}}
with
\psi_{j}(\alpha)\in[\epsilon_{j}^{-4},\epsilon_{j}^{4})
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
\alpha({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\mu_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
\alpha({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})=\alpha\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{j:K_{j}\text{ real}}\min_{v\in\Omega^{\infty}_{K_{j}}}|\epsilon_{j}|_{v}^{i_{j}/2t_{j}}|N_{M/K_{j}}(\alpha)|_{v}^{-1/2}\right)^{1/\#\{j:K_{j}\text{ real}\}}

Since
\prod_{w}|\alpha|^{d_{w}}_{w}=|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\alpha)|
and the
\epsilon_{j}
are linearly independent in the unit lattice of
M
, we can solve for
\mathbf{c}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

|\alpha|_{w}=|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\alpha)|^{1/4}\prod_{j^{\prime}}|\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}|_{w}^{c_{j^{\prime}}},

and hence

\displaystyle|N_{M/K_{j}}(\alpha)|_{v}

\displaystyle=|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\alpha)|^{1/2}\prod_{w\mid v}\prod_{j^{\prime}}|\epsilon_{j^{\prime}}|_{w}^{d_{w}c_{j^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\alpha)|^{1/2}\prod_{j^{\prime}}|N_{M/K_{j}}(\epsilon_{j^{\prime}})|_{v}^{c_{j^{\prime}}}=|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\alpha)|^{1/2}|\epsilon_{j}|_{v}^{2c_{j}}.

Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
\alpha({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\mu_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})}

\displaystyle=|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\alpha)|^{-1/4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
\alpha({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})=\alpha\end{subarray}}\left(\prod_{j:K_{j}\text{ real}}\min_{v\in\Omega_{K_{j}}^{\infty}}|\epsilon_{j}|_{v}^{\frac{\mathbf{i}_{j}}{2t_{j}}-c_{j}}\right)^{1/\#\{j:K_{j}\text{ real}\}}.

The parameters
i_{j}
go up to
t_{j}
but for the purposes of an upper bound we may extend the sum to infinity for those
j
such that
K_{j}
is real. This shows that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
\alpha({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\mu_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})}\leq|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\alpha)|^{-1/4}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j:K_{j}\text{ real}\\
k_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\end{subarray}}\min_{v\in\Omega_{K_{j}}^{\infty}}|\epsilon_{j}|_{v}^{\frac{k_{j}/2t_{j}-c_{j}}{\#\{j:K_{j}\text{ real}\}}}.

When
\frac{k_{j}}{2t_{j}}-c_{j}\leq 0
the minimum is attained at
v=v_{j}
, conversely when
\frac{k_{j}}{2t_{j}}-c_{j}\geq 0
the minimum is attained at
v=v_{j}^{\prime}
. Since
|\epsilon_{j}|_{v_{j}}|\epsilon_{j}|_{v_{j}^{\prime}}=1
, we have that

|\epsilon_{j}|_{v_{j}^{\prime}}^{\frac{k_{j}/2t_{j}-c_{j}}{\#\{j:K_{j}\text{ real}\}}}=|\epsilon_{j}|_{v_{j}}^{\frac{c_{j}-k_{j}/2t_{j}}{\#\{j:K_{j}\text{ real}\}}}.

Therefore we see that each range corresponds to summing the term
|\epsilon_{j}|_{v_{j}}^{\frac{\ell_{j}}{2t_{j}\#\{j:K_{j}\text{ real}\}}}=u_{j}^{\frac{\ell_{j}}{2\#\{j:K_{j}\text{ real}\}}}
as
\ell_{j}
runs over non-positive integers. In summary, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}\\
\alpha({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})=\alpha\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\mu_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})}\leq|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\alpha)|^{-1/4}\prod_{j:K_{j}\text{ real}}\frac{2}{u_{j}^{1/2\#\{j:K_{j}\text{ real}\}}-1}\ll|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\alpha)|^{-1/4},

where we have used
u_{j}>1
from (
9.2
).

For each
\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}
, there exists an integral ideal
{\mathfrak{d}}_{\mathbf{i}}^{\prime}
in the ideal class
{\mathcal{D}}^{-1}
such that
\alpha({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i}){\mathcal{O}}_{M}={\mathfrak{d}}{\mathfrak{d}}_{\mathbf{i}}^{\prime}
. Conversely, suppose we are given an ideal
\mathfrak{c}
in
{\mathcal{D}}^{-1}
and we want to find an element
\alpha\in{\mathcal{O}}_{M}
such that
\alpha{\mathcal{O}}_{M}={\mathfrak{d}}{\mathfrak{c}}
and such that
\psi_{j}(\alpha)\in[\epsilon_{j}^{-4},\epsilon_{j}^{4})
. Since
\psi_{j}(\pm\epsilon_{1}^{k_{1}}\epsilon_{2}^{k_{2}}\epsilon_{3}^{k_{3}}\alpha)=\epsilon_{j}^{4k_{j}}\psi_{j}(\alpha)
by Lemma
9.11
, it follows that there are at most
16q_{M}\ll 1
ways to choose
\alpha
.

Now supposing that
s\leq t
distinct numbers
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{s}
arise among the
\alpha({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})
as
\mathbf{i}
varies, we deduce that

\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}}\frac{1}{\mu_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})}\ll\sum_{n=1}^{s}|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}(\alpha_{n})|^{-1/4}.

Hence for
t
certain distinct integral ideals
{\mathfrak{c}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathfrak{c}}_{t}
, we conclude

\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}}\frac{1}{\mu_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})}\ll|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{d}})|^{-1/4}\sum_{n=1}^{t}|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{c}}_{n})|^{-1/4}\leq|N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{d}})|^{-1/4}N({\mathcal{D}}^{-1}),

as desired.
∎

9.3.5.
Counting pure points in ideals

For a finitely generated
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
-submodule
\Lambda\subseteq M^{2}
and an open bounded
{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq M^{2}_{\infty}
(or the closure thereof), let

Z^{*}(\Lambda,{\mathcal{S}}):=\#\{\omega\in\Lambda\cap{\mathcal{S}}\colon\omega\neq\boldsymbol{0},L(\omega_{0}\colon\omega_{1})=M\}

Recall that any fractional ideal
{\mathfrak{d}}
of
M
defines a lattice
\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})\subseteq M^{2}
. In general, we will be using
\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})
for
\Lambda
and the sets
S(T)
or
S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)
for
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Over the last sections we have recovered the following decomposition of the counting function, analogous to that from
[
Wid10b
]
.

Lemma 9.23
.

Let
w_{M}
denote the number of roots of unity in
M
. Then for
Y>0
, we have

Z({\mathbb{P}}^{1},K,d,Y)=\frac{1}{q_{M}w_{M}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{0\neq{\mathfrak{b}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{M}}\mu_{M}({\mathfrak{b}})\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{I}}Z^{*}(\Lambda({\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{b}}),S_{\mathbf{i}}(N({\mathfrak{a}})^{1/4}Y)),

where the
{\mathfrak{a}}
sum runs over any set of ideal class representatives of
\Cl(M)
and
\mu_{M}
is the number field Möbius function.

Our aim will be to apply the counting result in Proposition
8.11
to estimate the various terms of the form
Z^{*}(\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}}),S_{\mathbf{i}}(T))
. To that end let us remark that
B_{v}\times B_{v}\subseteq M_{v}^{2}
is the unit region under the maximum of the
w
-adic valuations for all
w\mid v
of both coordinates, similarly
B_{\infty}\times B_{\infty}\subseteq M_{\infty}^{2}
is the maximum among all
w
-adic valuations. Recall the linear automorphism
\tau_{\mathbf{i}}
of
M_{\infty}
and the induced
\tau_{\mathbf{i}}
on
M^{2}_{\infty}
from §
9.3.3
, and the notation for successive minima
\lambda_{i}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})
with respect to the transformed unit balls
B^{\prime}_{v,\mathbf{i}}:=\tau_{\mathbf{i}}^{-1}B_{v}
from Definition
9.18
.

Lemma 9.24
.

The successive minima of
\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}}):={\mathfrak{d}}\times{\mathfrak{d}}\subseteq M^{2}
with respect to the balls
B^{\prime}_{v,\mathbf{i}}\times B^{\prime}_{v,\mathbf{i}}
are

\lambda_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i}),\;\lambda_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i}),\;\lambda_{2}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})\quad\text{ and }\quad\lambda_{2}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i}).

Proof.

The proof is identical to the proof of
[
Wid10b
, Lem. 4.2]
.
∎

By Lemma
9.15
and Proposition
9.17
we have

S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)=\tau^{-1}_{\mathbf{i}}S_{0}(T)\subseteq\tau^{-1}_{\mathbf{i}}\kappa T(B_{\infty}\times B_{\infty})=\kappa T(B^{\prime}_{\infty,\mathbf{i}}\times B^{\prime}_{\infty,\mathbf{i}}),

which shows that in the application of Proposition
8.11
to
Z^{*}(\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}}),S_{\mathbf{i}}(T))
it makes sense to compute the successive minima with respect to these regions.

Lemma 9.25
.

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}
be a fractional ideal of
M
and
T\geqslant 1
. Consider the absolute constant
\kappa
from Proposition
9.17
. If
\lambda_{2}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})>\kappa T
, then we have

Z^{\ast}\big(\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}}),S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)\big)=0.

Proof.

Suppose
(x_{0},x_{1})\in\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})\setminus(0,0)
is counted in
Z^{\ast}\big(\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}}),S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)\big)
. The condition
L(x_{0}\colon x_{1})=M
implies that
x_{0},x_{1}\in{\mathfrak{d}}
are
L
-linearly independent, hence by definition of
\lambda_{2}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathfrak{i})
for every
\lambda<\lambda_{2}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathfrak{i})
there is an
i
with
x_{i}\not\in\lambda B^{\prime}_{\infty,\mathbf{i}}
. However, from
(x_{0},x_{1})\in S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)
, we get
x_{i}\in\kappa TB^{\prime}_{v,\mathbf{i}}\subseteq(\lambda_{2}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathfrak{i})-\epsilon)B^{\prime}_{v,\mathbf{i}}
for some
\epsilon>0
. This is a contradiction.
∎

With this result in hand, we have the following point counting estimate.

Proposition 9.26
.

Z^{*}(\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}}),S_{\mathbf{i}}(T))=\frac{\vol S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)}{\det\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})}+O_{L}\left(\frac{T^{7}}{\lambda_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})\det({\mathfrak{d}})^{3/2}}\right)

Proof.

For simplicity let us write
\lambda_{i}
for
\lambda_{i}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})
.

The proof is split into two cases. Firstly, suppose that
\lambda_{2}>\kappa T
. In this case, we know from Lemma
9.25
that
Z^{*}(\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}}),S_{\mathbf{i}}(T))=0
. Moreover, we have

\frac{\vol S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)}{\det\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})}\ll\frac{T^{8}}{\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\det({\mathfrak{d}})^{3/2}}\ll\frac{T^{7}}{\lambda_{1}\det(\mathfrak{d})^{3/2}},

which verifies that the claimed bound holds.

Otherwise, suppose that
\lambda_{2}\ll T
. By Lemma
9.17
, the region
\tau_{\mathbf{i}}S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)
is in
\text{Lip}(\kappa T,N)
, for some constant
\kappa
depending at most on
d
and some absolute constant
N
. Hence we may apply Proposition
8.11
, which gives

\left|\#\big(\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})\cap S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)\big)-\frac{\vol S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)}{\det\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})}\right|\ll_{L}1+\frac{T^{2}}{\lambda^{2}_{1}}+\frac{T^{4}}{\lambda^{4}_{1}}+\frac{T^{6}}{\lambda^{4}_{1}\lambda^{2}_{2}}+\frac{T^{7}}{\lambda^{4}_{1}\lambda^{3}_{2}}.

But since
T\gg\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{1}
, we have

\frac{T}{\lambda_{1}}\geq\frac{T}{\lambda_{2}}\gg 1,

and hence the
T^{7}
term in the sum dominates the others. To turn this into an estimate for
Z^{*}(\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}}),S_{\mathbf{i}}(T))
, we need to remove the set of lattice points in
\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})
which do not generate
M
over
L
. Such points are given by pairs
(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})
with
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathfrak{d}}
which are linearly dependent over
L
. Without loss of generality, assume that
\alpha_{0}\neq 0
. Then
\alpha_{0}
lies in the
\mathbb{U}
-fundamental region
S^{(1)}_{\mathbf{i}}(T)\subseteq M_{\infty}
and hence we may apply Proposition
8.11
to the
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
-lattice
{\mathfrak{d}}
of rank 2 to deduce that the number of choices for
\alpha_{0}
is

\frac{\vol S^{(1)}_{\mathbf{i}}(T)}{\det{\mathfrak{d}}}+O_{L}\left(1+\frac{T^{2}}{\lambda_{1}^{2}}+\frac{T^{3}}{\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}\right)\ll\frac{T^{4}}{\det{\mathfrak{d}}}.

Moreover, given a choice of
\alpha_{0}
, the
L
-linearly independent choices for
\alpha_{1}
lie in
S^{(1)}_{\mathbf{i}}(T)\subseteq M_{\infty}
. We apply Proposition
8.11
once more to the lattice
{\mathfrak{d}}
and the counting region
L_{\infty}\alpha_{0}\cap S^{(1)}_{\mathbf{i}}(T)\subseteq M_{\infty}
. As the volume of this region is
0
, we can bound the number of choices of
\alpha_{1}
by
1+\frac{T^{2}}{\lambda_{1}^{2}}+\frac{T^{3}}{\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}\ll_{L}\frac{T^{3}}{\lambda_{1}^{2}\lambda_{2}}\ll\frac{T^{3}}{\lambda_{1}(\det\mathfrak{d})^{1/2}}
. Combining these two bounds we see that the contribution from these linearly dependent pairs is contained within the claimed error term.
∎

9.4.
Proof of Theorem
9.6

We are now almost ready to conclude our main counting result. Before doing so we require one final ingredient, an upper bound in the vein of Landau’s theorem on ideals of bounded norm. This will allow us to estimate the norm
N({\mathcal{D}}^{-1})
which arises in Proposition
9.21
.

Lemma 9.27
.

With
K
and
L
as before and
M=KL
, we have

\#\{{\mathfrak{a}}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{M}:N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\leq X\}\ll_{L,\epsilon}X\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s)+X^{1/2}|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{1/3+\epsilon}.

Remark 9.28
.

A field uniform version of Landau’s theorem was established by Lowry-Duda–Taniguchi–Thorne
[
LDTT22
, Thm. 3]
. Unfortunately their result contains a
\log^{d-1}X
term in general which is too large for our purposes. Instead we are able to use the fact that we are working with a biquadratic field, that we are only concerned with the dependence on the discriminant of two out of the three quadratic subfields,
and that we only require an upper bound to remove this term. Pointwise the residue appearing in the main term can only be bounded by something like
\log^{3}|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|
which can be as large as
\log^{3}X
again in our application but the advantage that we gain is that on average this residue is constant.

Proof of Lemma
9.27
.

Let
a_{n}
denote the number of ideals of norm
n
and
X\geqslant 1
be fixed. We start by smoothing the sum

\#\{{\mathfrak{a}}:N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\leq X\}=\sum_{n\leq X}a_{n}\leq\sum_{n\geq 1}a_{n}e^{1-n/X}.

Performing a Mellin inversion, we have

\#\{{\mathfrak{a}}:N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\leq X\}\leq\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{(2)}\zeta_{M}(s)X^{s}\Gamma(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Let
T>0
be a parameter that we will ultimately take going to infinity. We first truncate the integral to

\int_{(2)}\zeta_{M}(s)X^{s}\Gamma(s)\mathrm{d}s=\int_{2-iT}^{2+iT}\zeta_{M}(s)X^{s}\Gamma(s)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{|{\rm{Im}}(s)|>T}\zeta_{M}(s)X^{s}\Gamma(s)\mathrm{d}s

and note that for
s=2+it
and
|t|\geqslant T

\zeta_{M}(s)X^{s}\Gamma(s)\ll\zeta(2)^{4}X^{2}|t|^{3/2}e^{-\frac{\pi|t|}{2}}

by Stirling’s formula. Hence,

\int_{|{\rm{Im}}(s)|>T}\zeta_{M}(s)X^{s}\Gamma(s)\mathrm{d}s\ll X^{2}\int_{|t|\geqslant T}|t|^{3/2}e^{-\frac{\pi|t|}{2}}\mbox{d}t=o_{T\to+\infty}(1),

since this is the remainder of a convergent integral.
We now move the contour back to the half line, in the course of which we acquire the contribution of the residue at the simple pole
s=1
. Thus

\#\{{\mathfrak{a}}:N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\leq X\}\leq X\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s)+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\frac{1}{2}-iT}^{\frac{1}{2}+iT}\zeta_{M}(s)X^{s}\Gamma(s)\mathrm{d}s+|I_{-}|+|I_{+}|+o_{T\to+\infty}(1)

where

I_{\pm}=\int_{\frac{1}{2}\pm iT}^{2\pm iT}\zeta_{M}(s)X^{s}\Gamma(s)\mathrm{d}s.

Note that
\zeta_{M}(s)=\zeta(s)L(s,\chi_{K})L(s,\chi_{L})L(s,\chi_{K}\chi_{L})
and that we have available the following Weyl-type subconvexity bounds

\left|\zeta\left(\frac{1}{2}+it\right)\right|\ll(1+|t|)^{1/6+\epsilon}\text{ and }\left|L\left(\frac{1}{2}+it,\chi\right)\right|\ll(1+q|t|)^{1/6+\epsilon},

for
\chi
a Dirichlet character of modulus
q
.
The first bound is classical and due to Weyl, the second is work of Petrow–Young
[
PY23
]
.
Moreover, from Stirling’s formula we infer the bound

\left|\Gamma\left(\sigma+it\right)\right|\ll e^{-\frac{\pi|t|}{2}}|t|^{\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}.

In combination, we have for
s=\sigma\pm iT
with
\sigma\leq 1
,

\zeta_{M}(s)X^{s}\Gamma(s)\ll e^{-\frac{\pi T}{2}}X^{\sigma}T^{\sigma-\frac{1}{2}+\frac{4(1-\sigma)}{3}+\epsilon}|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{\frac{2(1-\sigma)}{3}},

for
\sigma>1
, we use instead the bound
\zeta_{M}(s)\leq\zeta(s)^{4}.

Together this implies that

I_{\pm}\ll e^{-\frac{\pi T}{2}}T^{\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon}|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{1/3}\int_{\frac{1}{2}}^{2}X^{\sigma}\mbox{d}\sigma=o_{T\to+\infty}(1).

We therefore have

\#\{{\mathfrak{a}}:N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})\leq X\}\leq X\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s)+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\frac{1}{2}-iT}^{\frac{1}{2}+iT}\zeta_{M}(s)X^{s}\Gamma(s)\mathrm{d}s+o_{T\to+\infty}(1).

Another application of the subconvexity bounds and Stirling’s formula produces the bound

\left|\zeta_{M}\left(\frac{1}{2}+it\right)X^{\frac{1}{2}+it}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+it\right)\right|\ll X^{\frac{1}{2}}|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{\frac{1}{3}+\epsilon}|t|^{\frac{2}{3}+\epsilon}e^{-\frac{\pi|t|}{2}}.

The remaining
t
integral is then convergent and it suffices to bound
\int_{-T}^{T}
by
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}
and to let
T
go to (positive) infinity.
∎

We are now ready to prove the main theorem of this section.

Proof of Theorem
9.6
.

Fix
\epsilon>0
. Throughout all implicit constants are allowed to depend on
d
, hence
L
, and on
\epsilon
.
For any ideal
{\mathfrak{d}}
and parameter
T\geq 1
, Proposition
9.26
gives the inequality

\left|Z^{*}(\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}}),S_{\mathbf{i}}(T))-\frac{\vol S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)}{\det\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})}\right|\ll\frac{T^{7}}{\lambda_{1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})\det({\mathfrak{d}})^{3/2}}.

Applying Proposition
8.3
and Lemma
8.7
, we may rewrite the error so that

Z^{*}(\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}}),S_{\mathbf{i}}(T))=\frac{\vol S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)}{\det\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})}+O\left(\frac{T^{7}}{\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{i},{\mathfrak{d}})|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/2}N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{d}})^{3/2}}\right).

Upon taking
{\mathfrak{d}}={\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{b}}
,
T=N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})^{1/4}Y
and applying Lemma
9.23
we see that

\displaystyle Z({\mathbb{P}}^{1},K,d,Y)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q_{M}w_{M}}\sum_{{\mathfrak{a}}}\sum_{0\neq{\mathfrak{b}}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{M}}\mu_{M}({\mathfrak{b}})\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{I}}\left[\frac{\vol S_{\mathbf{i}}(N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})^{1/4}Y)}{\det\Lambda({\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{b}})}\right.

\displaystyle+\left.O\left(\frac{(N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})^{1/4}Y)^{7}}{\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{i},{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{b}})|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/2}N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{b}})^{3/2}}\right)\right].

By Proposition
9.13
, we have that

\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}}\vol S_{\mathbf{i}}(N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})^{1/4}Y)=N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}Y^{8}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in{\mathcal{I}}}\vol S_{\mathbf{i}}(1)=N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})^{2}Y^{8}2^{3r+s-1}\pi^{2s}q_{M}R_{M}.

The remaining sums in the main term are precisely as in
[
Wid10b
, p. 33]
, hence we conclude in the same manner that the main term is
c_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},M}Y^{8}
.
We turn to the error term.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
be the ideal classes of
{\mathfrak{a}}
and
{\mathfrak{b}}
respectively.
Then it follows from Proposition
9.21
that

\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{(N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}})^{1/4}Y)^{7}}{\lambda_{1}(\mathbf{i},{\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{b}})|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/2}N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{b}})^{3/2}}\ll\frac{Y^{7}N((\mathcal{AB})^{-1})}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/2}N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{b}})^{7/4}}.

The
{\mathfrak{b}}
sum converges absolutely and both the sum over ideal classes and that defining
N(({\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{B}})^{-1})
are finite so we can exchange summations. The final computation remaining is therefore a sum over
th_{M}
ideals of the reciprocal of the norms. We cannot handle this sum in the elementary manner of the proof of
[
Sch95
, Thm. 2]
as this would result in an outsized power of
\log Y
. Instead, we will use partial summation and Lemma
9.27
. Before this, we must handle the unusual summation condition. In order to do so we split the sum into ideals of norm less than
th_{M}
and those of larger norm. Let us suppose that there are
I
ideals of norm less than
th_{M}
. Then

\sum_{i=1}^{th_{M}}N({\mathfrak{c}}_{i})^{-1/4}\ll\sum_{N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{c}})\leq th_{M}}N({\mathfrak{c}})^{-1/4}+\sum_{i=I+1}^{th_{M}}N({\mathfrak{c}}_{i})^{-1/4}.

Each summand in the second sum is at most
(th_{M})^{-1/4}
and there are at most
th_{M}
terms so the sum is bounded by
(th_{M})^{3/4}
. Applying partial summation to Lemma
9.27
, and writing
X=th_{M}
, the former sum is bounded by

\frac{X\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s)+X^{1/2}|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{1/3+\epsilon}}{X^{1/4}}+\int_{1}^{X}\frac{u\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s)+u^{1/2}|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{1/3+\epsilon}}{u^{5/4}}\mathrm{d}u

\ll X^{3/4}\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s)+X^{1/4}|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{1/3+\epsilon}.

Since
th_{M}\ll q_{M}R_{M}h_{M}\ll h_{M}R_{M}
by Proposition
9.14
with an implicit constant independent of
K
, we find the a total error term of order

\frac{(h_{M}R_{M})^{3/4}+(h_{M}R_{M})^{3/4}\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s)+(h_{M}R_{M})^{1/4}|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{1/3+\epsilon}}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/2}}.

By the Brauer–Siegel theorem the first term dominates the third, and we arrive at the required bound.
∎

Remark 9.29
.

Our passage to the
{\mathcal{O}}_{L}
-lattice structure was critical in providing the error term which we have achieved in Theorem
9.6
. Without doing so, merely treating
\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})
as a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-lattice one would need control of four successive minima. Using Widmer’s lower bounds on the successive minima (
[
Wid10b
, Lem. 9.7]
) one could achieve a result of the shape

\left|Z^{*}(\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}}),S_{\mathbf{i}}(T))-\frac{\vol S_{\mathbf{i}}(T)}{\det\Lambda({\mathfrak{d}})}\right|\ll\frac{T^{7}}{\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},1}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})^{4}\lambda_{{\mathbb{Q}},3}({\mathfrak{d}},\mathbf{i})^{3}}\ll\frac{T^{7}}{N_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}({\mathfrak{d}})^{7}|\Delta_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/8}},

ultimately culminating (assuming equivalent savings in the
\mathbf{i}
and class group sums) in an estimate of the form

Z({\mathbb{P}}^{1},K,d,Y)=c_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},M}Y^{8}+O_{d}\left(Y^{7}\frac{\log^{4}(h_{M}R_{M})}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/4}}\right).

This is just barely insufficient for Theorem
1.3
since the error term overwhelms the main term once
|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|\geq B^{1/2}(\log B)^{-3}
.

9.5.
Proof of Theorem
9.1

We now conclude the proof of Theorem
9.1
. For the optimal cutoff
\gamma=1/2
for
X_{d}
thanks to Lemma
9.3
, we have

\displaystyle\#\{x\in\mathcal{U}:H(x)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\#\{P\in X_{d}(K):{\mathbb{Q}}(P)=K,H(P)H(\overline{P})\leq B\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\#\{P\in X_{d}(K):{\mathbb{Q}}(P)=K,H(P)\leq B^{1/2}\}.

By virtue of the height which we have chosen, the one induced by the naïve relative
\mathcal{O}(1)
-height
\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}
on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, the inner cardinality is precisely

\#\{Q\in{\mathbb{P}}^{1}(M):L(Q)=M,\mathcal{H}_{{\mathcal{O}}(1)}(Q)\leq B^{1/2}\}=Z({\mathbb{P}}^{1},K,d,B^{1/8}).

Applying Theorem
9.6
, the cardinality
\#\{x\in\mathcal{U}:H(x)\leq B\}
is given by

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\left[c_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},M}B+O_{d}\left(B^{7/8}\frac{(h_{M}R_{M})^{3/4}(1+\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s))}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/2}}\right)\right].

We first perform the sum of the main terms.

Lemma 9.30
.

We have

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}c_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},M}=\frac{\gamma}{4}\tau(\Hilb^{2}X_{d})\log B+O(1).

When summing up to the optimal cutoff
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
the leading constant equals
c_{\Hilb^{2}X_{d},{\mathbb{Q}}}
.

Proof.

We have
c_{{\mathbb{P}}^{1},M}=\frac{1}{2}\tau({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{M})
, and from
[
Lou15
, Thm. 4.3]
we obtain

\tau({\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{M})=\tau(\Res_{M/K}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}_{M})=\tau(X_{d,K}),

since
K
and
L
are linearly disjoint.
Applying Theorem
6.10
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\tau(X_{d,K})=\tau(\Hilb^{2}X_{d})\log(B^{\gamma})+O(1),

which proves the first result. The last statement follows from the values
\alpha(\Hilb^{2}X_{d})=\tfrac{1}{8}
and
\beta(\Hilb^{2}X_{d})=1
computed in Lemma
9.3
.
∎

Next, we gather estimates on the sums of the error terms.

Lemma 9.31
.

Fix
\epsilon>0
and
\gamma>0
. Recall that
M=KL=K(\sqrt{d})
. We have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\frac{(h_{M}R_{M})^{3/4}\left(1+\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s)\right)}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/2}}\ll B^{\gamma/4}(\log B)^{3/4}.

where the implicit constant is allowed to depend on
\epsilon,\gamma
and
L
.

Proof.

We start by splitting the sum into two separate sums

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\frac{(h_{M}R_{M})^{3/4}}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/2}}+\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\frac{(h_{M}R_{M})^{3/4}\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s)}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{3/2}}.

We start with the first term. Applying Hölder’s inequality, we may bound this by

\ll B^{\gamma/4}\left(\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\frac{h_{M}R_{M}}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{2}}\right)^{3/4}.

Using
|\Delta_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}|=|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{2}|\Delta_{L/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{2}
and the class number formula, we have

\frac{h_{M}R_{M}}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{2}}\ll_{L}\frac{1}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|}\frac{h_{M}R_{M}}{|\Delta_{M/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{1/2}}=\frac{2^{-r_{M}}(2\pi)^{-s_{M}}w_{M}\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s)}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|}.

Since
M
is a biquadratic field, the Dedekind zeta function can be decomposed and we see that

\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s)=L(1,\chi_{K})L(1,\chi_{L})L(1,\chi_{K}\chi_{L}).

The value
L(1,\chi_{L})
is a constant which does not depend on
K
and hence can be incorporated into the implicit constant.
As in the proof of Theorem
6.10
, we first handle the sum without the term
|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|
. Our starting point then is the sum for
Y\geqslant 1

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K}|\leq Y\end{subarray}}2^{-r_{M}}(2\pi)^{-s_{M}}L(1,\chi_{K})L(1,\chi_{K}\chi_{L}).

The remaining
L
-function
L(s,\chi_{K})L(s,\chi_{K}\chi_{L})
is precisely the one attached to the Galois representation
\mathbf{1}\oplus\chi_{L}
twisted by
\chi_{K}
. Thus we may separate the fields according to their embedding type and number of roots of unity and
appeal to Corollary
6.12
with the conditions incorporated into the sequence
c_{\Delta}
and
t=1
to deduce the upper bound

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(r,s)\\
w\in\{2,3,4,6,8,12\}\end{subarray}}\hskip-17.07182pt2^{-r}(2\pi)^{-s}w\hskip-14.22636pt\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K}|\leq Y\\
(r_{M},s_{M})=(r,s)\\
w_{M}=w\end{subarray}}L(1,\chi_{K})L(1,\chi_{K}\chi_{L})\ll Y.

Thus by partial summation, we arrive at

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\frac{h_{M}R_{M}}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{2}}\ll\gamma\log B,

as desired.

The second sum is handled similarly. Again, one may apply Hölder to arrive at the bound

\ll\left(\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\frac{h_{M}R_{M}}{|\Delta_{K/{\mathbb{Q}}}|^{2}}\right)^{3/4}\left(\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K}|\ll B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}(\res_{s=1}\zeta_{M}(s))^{4}\right)^{1/4}.

We know already that the first term is
O((\log B)^{3/4})
, hence it suffices to produce the bound
O(B^{\gamma/4})
for the second term. This is accomplished in the same way by considering

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}[K:{\mathbb{Q}}]=2\\
|\Delta_{K}|\leq B^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}L(1,\chi_{K})^{4}L(1,\chi_{K}\chi_{L})^{4},

which is bounded by
O_{d}(B^{\gamma})
by the
t=4
case of Corollary
6.12
.
∎

In conclusion, upon summing up to the optimal cutoff
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
we find that

\displaystyle\#\{x\in\mathcal{U}:H(x)\leq B\}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma}{4}\tau(\Hilb^{2}X_{d})B\log B+O(B)+O_{d}(B^{7/8+\gamma/4}(\log B)^{3/4})

\displaystyle=c_{\Hilb^{2}X_{d},{\mathbb{Q}}}B\log B+O_{d}(B(\log B)^{3/4}),

establishing Theorem
9.1
and with it completing the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

From the success of the approach we rediscover, as in Example
7.26
and Remark
7.27
, that the cutoff
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
for
X_{d}
is indeed optimal.

Remark 9.32
.

As Widmer has shown the geometry of numbers approach is compatible with any adelic height which satisfies the Lipschitz parametrisability conditions required for counting. While we have only established our results for the height induced by the standard height on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, the arguments do not use this explicitly and we could have chosen any anticanonical height on
X_{d}
induced from an adelic Lipschitz system on
{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
.
